  function initializeReadingPreferences() {
    let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
    if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
      if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
        saved_theme = "dark";
      }
    }
    if (saved_theme == "dark") {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
    } else {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
    }

    const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
    if (tocDisplay) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
    }
    const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
    if (readingMode) {
      document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
    }
  }

arXiv:2609.02249v2 [math.CO] 17 Sep 2026 
 
 
 Hamiltonicity of graphs of acyclic orientations
 and acyclic polynomials 
 
 
 Leonie Mühlherr and Germain Poullot
 
 Date: September 17, 2026 

 Abstract. 
    
 We study the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  of acyclic orientations of a graph  G G .
Two acyclic orientations are adjacent in this graph if they disagree on the orientation of a single arc.
In particular, we focus on the Hamiltonicity of the graphs  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) . 
    
 Using two methods of pattern lacing which generalize the zig-zag method of Brenner, Cardinal, McConville, Merino and Mütze, we characterize which multipaths are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Moreover, we give a criterion for the gluing of a multipath on a given graph to preserve  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity.
Building towards an inductive certification of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity via the (open) ear decomposition of 2-connected graphs, we propose three ways of gluing several multipaths to a given graph. 
    
 In addition, we define the acyclic polynomials to encapsulate both the number of acyclic orientations of a graph and the “parity problem” proposed by Savage, Squire and West:
if  − 1 -1  is not a root of the acyclic polynomial of  G G , then  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
We explore numerous properties of the acyclic polynomials, proving that they are not instances of the famous Tutte–Whitney polynomials, but that they too exhibit a partial deletion-contraction phenomenon.

Furthermore, the authors are grateful to Jean Cardinal, Torsten Mütze, Vincent Pilaud and Francesco Verciani for many interesting comments, discussions, and literature recommendations.
We acknowledge the CRM conference “Combinatorial Geometries and Geometric Combinatorics” and the PAGCAP workshop “Beyond Permutahedra and Associahedra”, in late 2025, where this project started.

 Use of AI declaration 

 
 The only use we made of AI (namely ChatGPT using GPT-5.5) was to correct mistakes in English writing, to search the literature and to improve some minor explanations of the existing literature ( e.g.  replacing “the number of maximal cones at a given distance from a base maximal cone in the dual graph of a hyperplane arrangement” by “the Whitney numbers”), to improve the readability of the table in Appendix  D , and to parallelize our implementation of the acyclic signature in order to obtain the results of  Example   3.13 .
All statements, proofs, concepts, notations, figures, references to the literature, etc., were made by us, as humans, using non-AI-powered tools (as far as we are aware).
Notably, computations of Hamiltonian cycles and acyclic polynomials were run using SageMath  [ 36 ]  and our own pieces of code, not relying on AI-powered computations, except for the parallelization required in  Example   3.13  for graphs of more than  22 22  edges. 
 
 
 
 1.  Introduction 

 
 The motivation for this article spans across various mathematical disciplines.
At its core, the question we want to answer is for which graphs the acyclic orientations can be enumerated in a nice way.
One can make this statement more precise by formulating it via Gray codes, which are enumerations where consecutive objects differ only by a small change.
The original and most common example is the enumeration of binary strings, see  [ 23 ]  for more background.
Acyclic orientations of a graph can be encoded as binary strings, each bit coding for an edge, and  0 0 ,  1 1  representing its two possible orientations.
Yet, in this representation, it is hard to check whether a binary string encodes an acyclic orientation or not.
We are interested in the following problem. 
 
 
 Formulation 1 . 
 
 Classify the graphs  G G  for which the list of acyclic orientations admits a Gray code. 
 
 
 
 The reader unfamiliar with this version of the problem may take a look at  Tables   2  and  3  for some typical examples.
In this article, the main perspective on this question is the graph-theoretical one:
We study the graph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  whose nodes are the acyclic orientations of a graph  G G , and where two orientations are neighboring if and only if they differ in flipping the orientation of exactly one arc.
We can restate our problem: 
 
 
 Formulation 2 . 
 
 Classify the graphs  G G  whose graph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 This is the research angle of Savage, Squire and West in their seminal work  [ 37 ] .
They achieved a classification for some notable example classes, such as cycles, wheel-graphs, ladder-graphs, and chordal graphs (see  [ 37 ]  or here below  Example   2.7 ).
This quest for efficient ways to produce all the acyclic orientations of a graph was then pursued by numerous authors,
see  e.g.  Squire  [ 39 ]  and Barbosa–Szwarcfiter  [ 3 ]  for generating problems.
Most prominently, Pruesse and Ruskey  [ 34 ]  showed that the prism over the graph of acyclic orientations of any graph is Hamiltonian.
Furthermore, Pilaud  [ 32 ]  studied the properties of the poset obtained from the graph of acyclic orientations of  G G  by orienting it away from a base (acyclic) orientation  D D .
Remarkably, he determined under which conditions on  G G  and  D D  it is a lattice.
In particular, the rank function of this poset gives the coefficients of the acyclic polynomials that we study in  Section   3 .
We also refer to Savage’s survey  [ 38 ]  for a presentation of the famous Steinhaus–Johnson–Trotter algorithm  [ 22 ]  which produces a Hamiltonian cycle in the graph of acyclic orientations for the complete graph.
The interested reader may consult  [ 9 , Figure 5]  for a comprehensive survey on algorithms and Hamiltonicity of graphs of acyclic orientations of combinatorial families beyond graphs. 
 
 
 The results of Savage, Squire and West strongly suggest that parity considerations (such as “Is  C n C_{n}  a cycle with  n n  even or odd?”) play a key role in determining whether  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is Hamiltonian or not.
In particular, they establish a simple yet powerful criterion for deciding that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is not Hamiltonian:
fixing any orientation  D D  of  G G , if  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is Hamiltonian, then the number of acyclic orientations of  G G  disagreeing with  D D  on the orientation of an  even  number of arcs is equal to the number of acyclic orientations of  G G  disagreeing with  D D  on an  odd  number of arcs.
When a graph does not satisfy this condition, Savage, Squire and West  [ 37 ]  speak of a  parity problem  (see also  Lemma   2.9 ). 
 
 
 As a tribute to their foundational work, we extend their methods in  Section   3.1  to study Cartesian products of graphs, and prove: 
 
 
 Theorem A   ( Corollary   3.10 ) . 
 
 The following graphs are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Ladder graphs  L n ≔ P n × K 2 L_{n}\coloneqq P_{n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  even 
 
 (2)  
 
 Book graphs  B n ≔ K 1 , n × K 2 B_{n}\coloneqq K_{1,n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  odd 
 
 (3)  
 
 Prism graphs  Pr n ≔ C n × K 2 \mathrm{Pr}_{n}\coloneqq C_{n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  even 
 
 (4)  
 
 Toroidal grid graphs  T 4 , m ≔ C 4 × C m T_{4,m}\coloneqq C_{4}\times C_{m}  for any  m m  even 
 
 (5)  
 
 Cube graphs  Q n = K 2 × ⋯ × K 2 Q_{n}=K_{2}\times\dots\times K_{2} , with  n n  copies of  K 2 K_{2} , for any  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , 
 
 (6)  
 
 More generally, the graph  T × Q n T\times Q_{n}  for any tree  T T  where either  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2  or  T T  has an even number of nodes, 
 
 (7)  
 
 Thick wheel graphs  TW n ≔ W n × K 2 \mathrm{TW}_{n}\coloneqq W_{n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  odd, where  W n W_{n}  is  C n C_{n}  together with a node adjacent to all nodes of  C n C_{n} , 
 
 (8)  
 
 Any  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles for any tree  T T  and any  n 1 , … , n s ≥ 1 n_{1},\dots,n_{s}\geq 1 , if there is some  i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d]  such that  n i n_{i}  is even and if  ∑ i = 1 s ( n i − 1 ) \sum_{i=1}^{s}(n_{i}-1)  is odd. 
 
 
 
 
 
 This result motivates us to define the rank with respect to  D D  of an acyclic orientation  A A , denoted  rk D ⁡ A \rank_{D}A , as the number of arcs on the orientation of which  A A  and  D D  disagree.
We encapsulate the numbers of acyclic orientations of each rank in a polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) ≔ ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) t rk D ⁡ A \Psi(D;t)\coloneqq\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}t^{\rank_{D}A}  called the  acyclic polynomial  ( Section   3 ).
In particular, constructing the  acyclic signature  as  σ ⁡ ( D ) ≔ Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) \sigma(D)\coloneqq\Psi(D;-1)  (and  σ ⁡ ( G ) ≔ | σ ⁡ ( D ) | \sigma(G)\coloneqq|\sigma(D)|  for any orientation  D D  of  G G ), we can rephrase the parity problem as deciding whether  σ ⁡ ( D ) = 0 \sigma(D)=0  or not.
See  Table   1  for an overview, and  Section   3.3  for various properties of the acyclic signature. 
 
 
 Our acyclic polynomial should not be confused with the  acyclic orientation polynomial  recently defined in  [ 21 ] : there, the authors assign a variable to each node of a graph, and associate each acyclic orientation to the product of the variables assigned to the sinks of the orientation.

 Stanley  [ 40 ]  famously proved that the number of acyclic orientations of a graph  G G  can be recovered from its chromatic polynomial  χ ⁡ ( G , x ) \chi(G;x)  by evaluating it at  − 1 -1 ,  i.e.   Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) = χ ⁡ ( G , − 1 ) \Psi(D;1)=\chi(G;-1)  for any orientation  D D  of  G G  (see  Remark   3.19  for a discussion of this relation).
Since then, the study of the chromatic polynomial itself has grown into a branch of Combinatorics, and the focus on the evaluation of this polynomial at  − 1 -1  together with the combinatorial reciprocity theorems it suggests  [ 20 ,  17 ,  18 ,  25 ,  4 ]  is of particular relevance for our work. 
 
 
 As the chromatic polynomial can be computed exploiting a deletion-contraction phenomenon, it is natural to ask whether the acyclic polynomial also admits one.
The main objective of  Section   3.2  is to show that the acyclic polynomials partially admit a deletion-contraction phenomenon ( Propositions   3.54  and  3.58 ) which extends to the acyclic signatures ( Theorem   3.64 ), allowing for an algorithm that computes the acyclic signatures of some graphs ( Remark   3.66 ).
Furthermore, we prove in  Remark   3.18  that the acyclic polynomial is not an evaluation of the famous Tutte–Whitney polynomial (see  [ 45 ,  44 ,  12 ]  for the original articles, or  [ 29 ]  for a comprehensive presentation in the broader context of matroids).
Yet, it seems that there is more to this story, since recent results show that properties of acyclic orientations, besides counting, are encoded in the Tutte–Whitney polynomial, see  e.g.  the work of Backman  [ 2 ] . 
 
 
 The question of acyclic orientations of graphs also recently arose in the field of hyperplane arrangements  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII ,  KorberSchniedersStrickerWalizadeth25-HamiltonianCyclesSupersolvable ] , where the acyclic orientations of a graph are in one-to-one correspondence with the chambers of the corresponding graphic arrangement, that is the arrangement of hyperplanes  𝒜 G := { 𝒙 ∈ ℝ N ; x i = x j } \mathcal{A}_{G}:=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}~;~x_{i}=x_{j}\}  for  { i , j } \{i,j\}  an edge of  G G  with node set  N N . 
 
 
 The graph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is known in this context as the tope graph of the graphic arrangement, describing the neighboring structure of the chambers.
Equivalently  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is also the vertex-edge graph of the zonotope associated to the arrangement, known under the names  acyclotopes   [ 47 ]  and  graphical zonotopes   [ 33 ,  30 ,  31 ] .
The interested reader may consult  [ 41 , Section 2]  or  [ 6 , Section 1.1]  for a detailed presentation.
The interplay of properties of the tope graph, the arrangement and the zonotope is an active field of research  [ 24 ,  1 ] .
(Note that our question need not be restricted to the graphic case in this setting.) 
 
 
 Formulation 3 . 
 
 Classify all (graphic) arrangements whose tope graph admits a Hamiltonian cycle.
Equivalently, classify all graphs such that the vertex-edge graph of the associated graphical zonotope (a.k.a. acyclotope) is Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 For example, it is known that the tope graph of so-called reflection arrangements, of which  𝒜 K ℓ \mathcal{A}_{K_{\ell}}  is an example, admits a Hamiltonian cycle  [ 11 ] .
Recently, there has been another development for this more general setting as two groups of researchers independently found that the tope graph of so-called  supersolvable arrangements  are Hamiltonian  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII ,  KorberSchniedersStrickerWalizadeth25-HamiltonianCyclesSupersolvable ] .
Applying this result to graphic arrangements recovers the result for chordal graphs from Savage, Squire and West  [ 37 , Section 4.1] .
Furthermore, some of the observations we make in this work about the evaluations of the acyclic polynomial for graphs are already known in this more general hyperplane setting, employing different names and arguments (see  Remark   3.24 ). 
 
 
 In the second main part of our work,  Section   4 , we prove that some operations can be performed on a graph whose graph of acyclic orientations is Hamiltonian, preserving this property.
We introduce two methods of  pattern lacing  ( Corollaries   4.4  and  4.5 ) which can be seen as generalizing the zig-zag method described in  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII ] .
This way, we prove that, under a certain parity condition, adding a path of even length (or two paths of odd lengths) between nodes  u u  and  v v  preserves having a Hamiltonian graph of acyclic orientations.
A graph consisting of several paths between two nodes  u u  and  v v  is called a  multipath .
Using these path gluings together with our results on the acyclic signature, we establish (“ G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian” means “ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is Hamiltonian”): 
 
 
 Theorem B   ( Corollaries   4.13  and  4.14 ) . 
 
 We have the following positive results: 
 
 (1)  
 
 A multipath  G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if the number of edges of  G G  is odd. 
 
 (2)  
 
 Any  2 2 -sum of two cycle graphs  C n C_{n}  and  C m C_{m}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if  n + m n+m  is even. 
 
 (3)  
 
 For a tree  T = ( [ s ] , E ) T=([s],E)  and numbers  n 1 , … , n s ≥ 3 n_{1},\dots,n_{s}\geq 3 , a  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles is any graph isomorphic to a  2 2 -sum  H ⊕ 2 C n s H\oplus_{2}C_{n_{s}}  where  H H  is a  ( T ∖ s , n 1 , … , n s − 1 ) (T\smallsetminus s,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s-1}) -tree of cycles.
If  n p n_{p}  is odd for all  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s] , then any  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, for any tree  T T . 
 
 (4)  
 
 If  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E  is an edge of a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , and  C n C_{n}  is a cycle graph, then the  2 2 -sum  G ⊕ 2 C n G\oplus_{2}C_{n}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if  n n  is odd. 
 
 
 
 
 
 This theorem subsumes the one of Savage, Squire and West: “For a cycle  C n C_{n}  on  n n  nodes,  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( C n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(C_{n})  is Hamiltonian if and only if  n n  is odd”  [ 37 , below Lemma 3.1 & Theorem 4.2] .
The above  Theorem   B  is a specialization of the more general  Corollary   4.11  made easier for the introduction. 
 
 
 The motivation behind considering this type of operation arises from the fact that if  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  is Hamiltonian for all the  2 2 -connected components of  G G , then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is also Hamiltonian.
Moreover, it is well-known (see  [ 13 , Section 3.1]  for details and figures) that  2 2 -connected graphs admit an (open) ear decomposition,  i.e.  we can construct any  2 2 -connected graph by starting with a cycle and subsequently adding paths between pairs of nodes.
Therefore, exploring all the ways of adding paths to a graph that are compatible with the  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity of said graph means iteratively constructing a large example class with hopes of reaching a complete classification.
We show (recall that a node is  simplicial  if all its neighbors pairwise share an edge,  i.e.  form a clique): 
 
 
 Theorem C   (Simplified version  Corollary   4.18 ) . 
 
 Let  G G  be a graph such that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is Hamiltonian.
If  G ′ G^{\prime}  is obtained from  G G  by either 
 
 (1)  
 
 the addition of a simplicial node  x ∘ x_{\circ}  on a clique of odd cardinality in  G G  and the (optional) gluing of a multipath of even cardinality at some nodes  u , v u,v  with  u ≠ x ∘ u\neq x_{\circ}  and  v ≠ x ∘ v\neq x_{\circ} ; or 
 
 (2)  
 
 the (optional) addition of a simplicial node and the gluing of a multipath of even cardinality at some nodes  u , v u,v  that share an edge in  G G ; 
 
 
 
 
 Then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G^{\prime})  is Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 A more flexible yet notation-heavy version of Theorem  C  is proven in  Corollary   4.18 .
We also detail a third way of gluing several multipaths on different pairs of nodes in  Section   4.4 . 
 
 
 This article is organized as follows:
In  Section   2 , we summarize all the graph-theoretic preliminaries that are needed for the present work, especially on orientations, acyclic orientations, but also the usual examples of the Gray code for binary sequences and the Steinhaus–Johnson–Trotter algorithm for permutations.
In  Section   3  we introduce the acyclic polynomial and explain its fundamental properties.
Notably, we decompose it into partial acyclic polynomials ( Proposition   3.43 ), and prove a deletion-contraction formula in certain cases ( Propositions   3.54  and  3.58 ).
This allows us to better understand the acyclic signature of graphs ( Section   3.3 ) and to compute the acyclic signature of multipaths ( Theorem   3.69 ), exemplifying how to certify that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is not Hamiltonian for some graph  G G .
On the constructive side of Hamiltonicity considerations, in  Section   4 , we give explicit constructions of Hamiltonian cycles for graphs obtained via graph operations such as path gluings or addition of a simplicial node. 
 
 
 Generalizations of strategies of pattern lacing can be found in Appendix  A , while Appendix  B  is devoted to complete bipartite graphs, and Appendix  C  proposes an alternative proof that multipaths of even total length are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian using a counting argument.
In Appendix  D , we give  22 22  explicit examples of well-known graphs and the properties of their graph of acyclic orientations, for the reader to enjoy. 
 
 
 
 2.  Preliminaries 

 
 We denote  [ n ] ≔ { 1 , … , n } {[n]}\coloneqq\{1,\dots,n\} , and  [ i , j ] = { i , i + 1 , … , j } [i,j]=\{i,i+1,\dots,j\} .
Moreover, let  2 X ≔ { Y ; Y ⊆ X } {2^{X}}\coloneqq\{Y~;~Y\subseteq X\}  and  ( X k ) ≔ { Y ∈ 2 X ; | Y | = k } {\binom{X}{k}}\coloneqq\{Y\in 2^{X}~;~|Y|=k\} , where  | Y | |Y|  is the size ( i.e.  cardinality) of the set  Y Y .
For two sets  X , Y X,Y  we denote  X ⊔ Y {X\sqcup Y}  for  X ∪ Y X\cup Y  when  X X  and  Y Y  are disjoint sets.
The  Cartesian product  of  X X  and  Y Y  is  X × Y ≔ { ( x , y ) ; x ∈ X , y ∈ Y } {X\times Y}\coloneqq\{(x,y)~;~x\in X,y\in Y\} , and the  symmetric difference  of  X X  and  Y Y  is  X ​ △ ​ Y ≔ ( X ∪ Y ) ∖ ( X ∩ Y ) = ( X ∖ Y ) ⊔ ( Y ∖ X ) {X\triangle Y}\coloneqq(X\cup Y)\smallsetminus(X\cap Y)=(X\smallsetminus Y)\sqcup(Y\smallsetminus X) . 
 
 
 An (undirected)  graph   G ≔ ( N , E ) {G}\coloneqq(N,E)  is composed of a set of  nodes   N N , and a set of  edges   E ⊆ ( [ n ] 2 ) E\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2} .
A  digraph  (or simple directed graph)  D ≔ ( N , F ) {D}\coloneqq(N,F)  is composed of a set of nodes  N N  and a set of  arcs  (or directed edges)  F = { ( i , j ) ; , i , j ∈ N } F=\bigl\{(i,j)~;~,i,j\in N\bigr\} , such that  { i , j } ≠ { k , ℓ } \{i,j\}\neq\{k,\ell\}  for  ( i , j ) , ( k , ℓ ) ∈ F (i,j),(k,\ell)\in F .
To shorten notation we write  i ​ j ij  for  { i , j } \{i,j\}  and  ( i , j ) (i,j)  when the context is clear. We denote by  N ⁡ ( G ) , E ⁡ ( G ) , F ⁡ ( G ) N(G),E(G),F(G)  the sets of nodes, edges and arcs of a given (di)graph  G G .
For a given node  i ∈ N i\in N , the nodes  j ∈ N j\in N  such that  i ​ j ∈ E ij\in E  are called the  neighbors  of  i i . 
 
 
 For a (di)graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , and some  U ⊆ N U\subseteq N , the associated  induced sub(di)graph  is defined as  G ⁡ [ U ] ≔ ( U , { i ​ j ∈ E ; i , j ∈ U } ) {G[U]}\coloneqq\bigl(U,\{ij\in E~;~i,j\in U\}\bigr) . 
 
 
 For a digraph  D = ( N , F ) D=(N,F) , the associated  underlying graph  is the graph  D ¯ ≔ ( N , E ) {\underline{D}}\coloneqq(N,E)  where  E = { { i , j } ; ( i , j ) ∈ F } E=\bigl\{\{i,j\}~;~(i,j)\in F\bigr\} ; conversely an  orientation  of  G G  is a digraph  D D  such that  D ¯ = G \underline{D}=G .
We denote the set of orientations of  G G  by  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {{\mathcal{O}}(G)} .
Similarly, for a digraph  D D , we call orientations of  D D  the orientations of  D ¯ \underline{D} , and denote  𝒪 ⁡ ( D ) {\mathcal{O}}(D)  for  𝒪 ⁡ ( D ¯ ) {\mathcal{O}}(\underline{D}) . 
 
 
 A (di)graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  is  bipartite  if there exists  N 1 , N 2 ⊆ N N_{1},N_{2}\subseteq N  such that  N 1 ⊔ N 2 = N N_{1}\sqcup N_{2}=N  and there is no  i ​ j ∈ E ij\in E  with  i , j ∈ N 1 i,j\in N_{1}  nor with  i , j ∈ N 2 i,j\in N_{2} . 
 
 
 Example 2.1 . 
 
 The following examples are well-studied in the literature. 
 
 (1)  
 
 For  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , the  n th n^{\text{th}}  cube-graph   Q n {Q_{n}}  is the graph on the set of nodes   2 [ n ] 2^{[n]}  where  X , Y ⊆ [ n ] X,Y\subseteq[n]  are linked by an edge if and only if  | X ​ △ ​ Y | = 1 |X\triangle Y|=1 .
Moreover, the  n th n^{\text{th}}  Boolean lattice   ℬ ​ o ​ o ​ l n {{\mathcal{B}}ool_{n}}  is the orientation of  Q n Q_{n}  with arcs  ( X , Y ) (X,Y)  for  X ⊆ Y X\subseteq Y  and  | Y ∖ X | = 1 |Y\smallsetminus X|=1 .
Cube-graphs and Boolean lattices are bipartite:  X X  and  Y Y  belong to the same part if and only if  | X | |X|  and  | Y | |Y|  have the same parity.
See  Figure   1  (top left) for an illustration. 
 
 (2)  
 
 For  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , the  n th n^{\text{th}}  complete graph   K n {K_{n}}  is the graph on the set of nodes  [ n ] [n]  where all  i i  and  j j  are linked by an edge for  i , j ∈ [ n ] i,j\in[n] .
Complete graphs are not bipartite.
A subset of nodes  U ⊆ N U\subseteq N  is a  clique  in a graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  if its induced subgraph  G ⁡ [ U ] G[U]  is isomorphic to a complete graph; and a node   i i  is  simplicial  if its set of neighbors is a clique. 
 
 (3)  
 
 For  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , the  n th n^{\text{th}}  permutahedral graph   Π n {\Pi_{n}}  whose nodes are the permutations of  [ n ] [n] , where  σ \sigma  and  σ ′ \sigma^{\prime}  are linked by an edge if there exists  i ∈ [ n − 1 ] i\in[n-1]  such that  σ ′ = σ ∘ ( i ​ i + 1 ) \sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\circ(i~\,~i+1) .
Moreover, the  n th n^{\text{th}}  weak (Bruhat) order   𝒲 ​ e ​ a ​ k n {{\mathcal{W}}eak_{n}}  is the orientation of  Π n \Pi_{n}  with arcs  ( σ , σ ′ ) (\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})  if  σ ′ = σ ∘ ( i ​ i + 1 ) \sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\circ(i~\,~i+1)  and  σ ⁡ ( i ) < σ ⁡ ( i + 1 ) \sigma(i)<\sigma(i+1) .
Permutahedral graphs and weak orders are bipartite. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 1 .  
The graphs of acyclic orientations of three graphs on  4 4  arcs, drawn as a Boolean lattice  ℬ ​ o ​ o ​ l 4 {\mathcal{B}}ool_{4}  with some elements removed.
Each is a ranked poset where the chosen based orientation is in  blue .
(Top left) For  G G  a tree,  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is a cube which is Hamiltonian as indicated by the  teal cycle .
(Top right) For  G G  the  1 1 -sum of an arc  K 2 K_{2}  and a triangle  C 3 C_{3} , the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is the Cartesian product  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 2 ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( C 3 ) = K 2 × C 6 {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{2})\times{\mathcal{AO}}(C_{3})=K_{2}\times C_{6}  which is Hamiltonian as indicated by the  teal cycle .
(Bottom) For  G G  an even cycle,  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is not Hamiltonian because there are  8 8  elements of odd ranks but  6 6  elements of even ranks.
 
 
 
 2.1.  On general orientations 

 
 For any fixed orientation  D = ( N , F ) D=(N,F)  of  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , the set of orientations of  G G  is in natural bijection with  2 E 2^{E} :
Namely, for  ω ⊆ E \omega\subseteq E , let  F ω ≔ { i ​ j if  ​ i ​ j ∈ ω j ​ i if  ​ i ​ j ∉ ω ; i ​ j ∈ F } {F^{\omega}}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}ij&\text{if }ij\in\omega\\
ji&\text{if }ij\notin\omega\\
\end{array}~;~ij\in F\right\} . 
 
 
 The  re-orientation of  D D  with respect to  ω \omega  is  D ω ≔ ( N , F ω ) {D^{\omega}}\coloneqq(N,F^{\omega}) , and it is immediate that  ω ↦ D ω \omega\mapsto D^{\omega}  defines a bijection  2 E → 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) 2^{E}\to{\mathcal{O}}(G) . We define the  (re-orientation) rank   rk D ⁡ ( D ω ) ≔ | ω | {\rank_{D}(D^{\omega})}\coloneqq|\omega| , see  Figure   1  for orientations drawn in ranked posets.
With respect to  D D , for two orientations  D 1 D_{1}  and  D 2 D_{2}  of  G G , we can find  ω 1 , ω 2 ⊆ E \omega_{1},\omega_{2}\subseteq E  such that  D 1 = D ω 1 D_{1}=D^{\omega_{1}}  and  D 2 = D ω 2 D_{2}=D^{\omega_{2}}  and we write  D 1 ⪯ D D 2 {D_{1}\preceq_{D}D_{2}}  if  ω 1 ⊆ ω 2 \omega_{1}\subseteq\omega_{2} . 
 
 
 When we choose a fixed orientation  D D  of  G G  in order to express all the other orientations of  G G  as a re-orientation of  D D , we call  D D  a  base orientation  and denote the set of orientations of some rank  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0  by
 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) ≔ { D ′ ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ ( D ′ ) = k } {{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)}\coloneqq\{D^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}(D^{\prime})=k\} .
Using the bijection  ω ↦ D ω \omega\mapsto D^{\omega} , we get  | 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = | ( E k ) | = ( | E | k ) |{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)|=\left|\binom{E}{k}\right|=\binom{|E|}{k} , for any base orientation  D D  and any  k k . 
 
 
 Remark 2.2 . 
 
 Using Newton’s binomial formula, one has, for  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 : 
 

 
 
 ( ∑ k ​  even ( m k ) ) − ( ∑ k ​  odd ( m k ) ) = ∑ k = 0 m ( m k ) ​ ( − 1 ) k = ( 1 − 1 ) m = 0 \left(\sum_{k\text{ even}}\binom{m}{k}\right)-\left(\sum_{k\text{ odd}}\binom{m}{k}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}(-1)^{k}=(1-1)^{m}=0 
 
 
 Hence,  ∑ k ​  even ( m k ) = ∑ k ​  odd ( m k ) = 2 m − 1 \sum_{k\text{ even}}\binom{m}{k}=\sum_{k\text{ odd}}\binom{m}{k}=2^{m-1}  for any  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 .
We emphasize that this implies that 
 

 
 
 ∑ k ​  even | 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = ∑ k ​  odd | 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = 2 | E | − 1 , \sum_{k\text{ even}}|{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)|=\sum_{k\text{ odd}}|{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)|=2^{|E|-1}, 
 
 
 for any base orientation  D D  of  G G .
This will prove useful in the following sections,  e.g.   Section   3.3 . 
 
 
 
 Besides, note that for any  D 1 = D ω 1 D_{1}=D^{\omega_{1}}  and  D 2 = D ω 2 D_{2}=D^{\omega_{2}} , we can define the  difference  between  D 1 D_{1}  and  D 2 D_{2}  as  D 1 ​ △ ​ D 2 ≔ ω 1 ​ △ ​ ω 2 {D_{1}\triangle D_{2}}\coloneqq\omega_{1}\triangle\omega_{2}  which does not depend on the base orientation  D D :
these are the edges of  D ¯ \underline{D}  which are not oriented the same way in  D 1 D_{1}  as in  D 2 D_{2} .
Two orientations  D 1 , D 2 D_{1},D_{2}  of  G G  are  adjacent  if  | D 1 ​ △ ​ D 2 | = 1 |D_{1}\triangle D_{2}|=1 .
The  graph of orientations  (respectively  digraph of orientations  with respect to some base orientation  D D ) of  G G  is the graph whose nodes  are the orientations of  G G , and where two orientations are linked by an edge (resp. arc) if they are adjacent (resp. adjacent with  D 1 ⊆ D D 2 D_{1}\subseteq_{D}D_{2} ), see  Figure   1  (top left).
We denote  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {{\mathcal{O}}(G)}  (resp.  𝒪 ⁡ ( D ) {{\mathcal{O}}(D)} ) this (di)graph, where we slightly abuse notation and reuse the notation used for the set of orientations.
Fixing any base orientation  D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G) , the bijection  ω ↦ D ω \omega\mapsto D^{\omega}  defines an isomorphism of graphs from the graph of orientations  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{O}}(G)  to the cube-graph  Q | E | Q_{|E|} , and from the digraph of orientations  𝒪 ⁡ ( D ) {\mathcal{O}}(D)  to the Boolean lattice  ℬ ​ o ​ o ​ l | E | {\mathcal{B}}ool_{|E|} .
Note that each base orientation  D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)  defines a different isomorphism  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) → Q | E | {\mathcal{O}}(G)\to Q_{|E|} , and a different orientation  𝒪 ⁡ ( D ) {\mathcal{O}}(D)  of  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{O}}(G) . 
 
 
 
 2.2.  Cycles, Hamiltonian graphs 

 
 A  (directed) path  in a (di)graph  G G  is a sequence of different nodes   i 1 , … , i r ∈ N ⁡ ( G ) i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in N(G)  such that  i p ​ i p + 1 i_{p}i_{p+1}  is a (directed) edge in  G G  for all  p ∈ [ r − 1 ] p\in[r-1] .
A  (directed) cycle  is a (directed) path  i 1 , … , i r ∈ N i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in N  such that  i r ​ i 1 i_{r}i_{1}  is a (directed) edge in  G G .
A graph is  Hamiltonian  (resp.  path-Hamiltonian ) if it admits a cycle (resp. a path) containing all its nodes.
Clearly, a Hamiltonian graph is path-Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 We state here a well-known proposition concerning Hamiltonicity of bipartite graphs and give a short proof for the sake of self-containment. 
 
 
 Proposition 2.3 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a graph admitting a bipartition  N = N 1 ⊔ N 2 N=N_{1}\sqcup N_{2} . 
 
 (1)  
 
 If  G G  is Hamiltonian, then  | N 1 | = | N 2 | |N_{1}|=|N_{2}| ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 If  G G  is path-Hamiltonian with path  i 1 , … , i r i_{1},\dots,i_{r}  where  i 1 ∈ N 1 i_{1}\in N_{1} , then either  | N 1 | = | N 2 | |N_{1}|=|N_{2}|  and  i r ∈ N 2 i_{r}\in N_{2} , or  | N 1 | = | N 2 | + 1 |N_{1}|=|N_{2}|+1  and  i r ∈ N 1 i_{r}\in N_{1} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 1 
If  G G  is Hamiltonian, let  i 1 , … , i r i_{1},\dots,i_{r}  be a cycle with  N = { i 1 , … , i r } N=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\} .
As  i p ​ i p + 1 ∈ E i_{p}i_{p+1}\in E  for all  p ∈ [ r − 1 ] p\in[r-1] , we get that  i p ∈ N 1 i_{p}\in N_{1}  if and only if  i p + 1 ∈ N 2 i_{p+1}\in N_{2}  and conversely, by definition of the bipartition.
So  i p ↦ i p + 1 i_{p}\mapsto i_{p+1}  for  p ∈ [ r − 1 ] p\in[r-1]  and  i r ↦ i 1 i_{r}\mapsto i_{1}  defines an involution of  N N  mapping  N 1 N_{1}  onto  N 2 N_{2} 
Thus,  | N 1 | = | N 2 | |N_{1}|=|N_{2}| . 
 
 
 2 
If  i r ∈ N 1 i_{r}\in N_{1} , then  i p ↦ i p + 1 i_{p}\mapsto i_{p+1}  for  p ∈ [ r − 1 ] p\in[r-1]  defines an involution of  N ∖ { i r } N\smallsetminus\{i_{r}\}  mapping  N 1 ∖ { i r } N_{1}\smallsetminus\{i_{r}\}  onto  N 2 N_{2} , hence  | N 1 | = | N 2 | + 1 |N_{1}|=|N_{2}|+1 .
Else,  i p ↦ i p + 1 i_{p}\mapsto i_{p+1}  for  p ∈ [ r − 1 ] p\in[r-1]  and  i r ↦ i 1 i_{r}\mapsto i_{1}  defines an involution of  N N  mapping  N 1 N_{1}  onto  N 2 N_{2} , hence  | N 1 | = | N 2 | + 1 |N_{1}|=|N_{2}|+1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example 2.4 . 
 
 The cube-graph is bipartite and Hamiltonian.
As it is a fundamental example supporting the constructions of  Section   4 , we detail the properties of Hamiltonian cycles of the cube-graphs. 
 
 
 There are two ways to encode the nodes of  Q n Q_{n} , either as subsets  X ⊆ [ n ] X\subseteq[n] , or as strings of bits  𝜺 = ( ε 1 , … , ε n ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } n {\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}=(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n} .
The bijection is given by  X ⁡ ( 𝜺 ) = { i ∈ [ n ] ; ε i = 1 } X({\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}})=\{i\in[n]~;~\varepsilon_{i}=1\} , and reciprocally,  ε ​ ( X ) i = 1 \varepsilon(X)_{i}=1  if and only if  i ∈ X i\in X . 
 
 
 The bipartition of  Q n Q_{n}  is given by the parts  { X ⊆ [ n ] ; | X | ​  is even } ≃ { 𝜺 ∈ { 0 , 1 } n ; ∑ i ε i ​  is even } \{X\subseteq[n]~;~|X|\text{ is even}\}\simeq\{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}~;~\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\text{ is even}\}  and  { X ⊆ [ n ] ; | X | ​  is odd } ≃ { 𝜺 ∈ { 0 , 1 } n ; ∑ i ε i ​  is odd } \{X\subseteq[n]~;~|X|\text{ is odd}\}\simeq\{{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}~;~\sum_{i}\varepsilon_{i}\text{ is odd}\} .
In particular, the nodes  ∅ ≃ ( 0 , … , 0 ) \varnothing\simeq(0,\dots,0)  and  [ n ] ≃ ( 1 , … , 1 ) [n]\simeq(1,\dots,1)  belong to the same part if and only if  n n  is even. 
 
 
 There are many ways to construct Hamiltonian cycles in  Q n Q_{n} , see  [ 27 , A066037]  for a count of them for small  n n .
First, note that  Q 2 Q_{2}  is a cycle on  4 4  elements, so it trivially admits a Hamiltonian cycle.
Then, by induction, given two Hamiltonian cycles  𝑪 = ( X 1 , … , X 2 n ) {\boldsymbol{C}}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{2^{n}})  and  𝑪 ′ = ( Y 1 , … , Y 2 n ) {\boldsymbol{C}}^{\prime}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{2^{n}})  of  Q n Q_{n}  with a shared arc  ( X 2 n , X 1 ) = ( Y 2 n , Y 1 ) (X_{2^{n}},X_{1})=(Y_{2^{n}},Y_{1})  ( e.g.   𝑪 = 𝑪 ′ {\boldsymbol{C}}={\boldsymbol{C}}^{\prime} ), one can construct a Hamiltonian cycle of  Q n + 1 Q_{n+1}  as: 
 

 
 
 ( X 1 , X 2 , … , X 2 n , Y 2 n ∪ { n + 1 } , Y 2 n − 1 ∪ { n + 1 } , … , Y 1 ∪ { n + 1 } ) . \bigl(X_{1},\,X_{2},\,\dots,\,X_{2^{n}},\,\,Y_{2^{n}}\cup\{n+1\},\,Y_{2^{n}-1}\cup\{n+1\},\,\dots,\,Y_{1}\cup\{n+1\}\bigr)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 In term of sequences of bits, one constructs a highly symmetric Hamiltonian cycle  ( ε ( 1 ) , … , ε ( 2 n ) ) \bigl(\varepsilon^{(1)},\dots,\varepsilon^{(2^{n})}\bigr)  as follows.
For each  i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] , the sequence  ( ε i ( 1 ) , ε i ( 2 ) , … , ε i ( 2 n ) ) \bigl(\varepsilon_{i}^{(1)},\varepsilon_{i}^{(2)},\dots,\varepsilon_{i}^{(2^{n})}\bigr)  starts with a sequence of  2 n − i 2^{n-i}  copies of  0 0 , then alternates between  2 n − i + 1 2^{n-i+1}  copies of  1 1  and  2 n − i + 1 2^{n-i+1}  copies of  0 0 , until it ends with again  2 n − i 2^{n-i}  copies of  0 0 .
Such a construction is illustrated by the columns of  Table   2 .

 Example 2.5 . 
 
 The permutahedral graph  Π n \Pi_{n}  is bipartite and Hamiltonian.
On the one hand, since two adjacent permutations in  Π n \Pi_{n}  differ by a transposition, the usual signature of permutation provides a bipartition of  Π n \Pi_{n} :
no two even (resp. odd) permutations share an edge in  Π n \Pi_{n} . 
 
 
 We shortly present the famous Steinhaus–Johnson–Trotter algorithm  [ 22 ]  which yields a Hamiltonian cycle of the permutahedral graph.
See the survey of Savage  [ 38 , Section 3]  for a more detailed presentation of the subject. 
 
 
 First, for a permutation  τ \tau  of  n n  elements and  i ∈ [ n + 1 ] i\in[n+1] , we define the permutation  τ ( i ) \tau^{(i)}  of  n + 1 n+1  elements by 
 

 
 
 τ ( i ) ​ ( j ) = { τ ⁡ ( j ) if  ​ j < i n + 1 if  ​ j = i τ ⁡ ( j − 1 ) if  ​ j > i . \tau^{(i)}(j)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau(j)&\text{if }j<i\\
n+1&\text{if }j=i\\
\tau(j-1)&\text{if }j>i\end{array}\right.\,. 
 
 
 
 
 The graph  Π 3 \Pi_{3}  trivially admits a Hamiltonian cycle since it is a cycle-graph on  6 6  elements,  e.g.   123 − 132 − 312 − 321 − 231 − 213 123-132-312-321-231-213 .
By induction, let  ( σ 1 , … , σ n ! ) (\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n!})  be a Hamiltonian cycle of  Π n \Pi_{n} ,
then the following is a Hamiltonian cycle of  Π n + 1 \Pi_{n+1} , see  Table   3 : 
 

 
 
 ( σ 1 ( n + 1 ) , … , σ 1 ( 1 ) , σ 2 ( 1 ) , … , σ 2 ( n + 1 ) , σ 3 ( n + 1 ) , … , σ 3 ( 1 ) , … , σ n ! ( 1 ) , … , σ n !  2.3.  Graph of acyclic orientations 

 
 A directed graph is  acyclic  if it contains no directed cycles, otherwise it is  cyclic .
For a (di)graph  G G , its  graph of acyclic orientations   𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}  is the subgraph of its graph of orientations  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{O}}(G)  induced on the set of acyclic orientations of  G G , see  Figure   1 .
Correspondingly, fixing a base orientation  D D  of  G G , the associated  digraph of acyclic orientations   𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) {{\mathcal{AO}}(D)}  is the subdigraph of its digraph of orientations  𝒪 ⁡ ( D ) {\mathcal{O}}(D)  induced on the set of acyclic orientations of  G G .
As sub(di)graphs of bipartite (di)graphs,  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) {\mathcal{AO}}(D)  are bipartite.
They are graded by the rank functions  rk D \rank_{D} .
We denote  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) ≔ { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A = k } {{\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D)}\coloneqq\{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A=k\} . 
 
 
 We denote  𝒞 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {{\mathcal{CO}}(G)} ,  𝒞 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) {{\mathcal{CO}}(D)}  and  𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) {{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D)}  the counterparts for cyclic orientations. 
 
 
 Example 2.6 . 
 
 For  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , the acyclic orientations of the complete graph  K n K_{n}  are in bijection with the permutations of  [ n ] [n] .
Indeed, any orientation  D D  of  K n K_{n}  yields a reflexive and transitive relation:  i ≤ j i\leq j  if and only if there is a directed path from  i i  to  j j  in  D D .
If this orientation is acyclic, then this relation is anti-symmetric,  i.e.  it is a permutation of  [ n ] [n] .
Two acyclic orientations of  K n K_{n}  are adjacent if they differ on the orientation of a single arc, that is if the associated permutations differ on the order of two consecutive elements.
Hence the graph of acyclic orientations of the complete graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{n})  is isomorphic to the permutahedral graph  Π n \Pi_{n} .
Furthermore, choosing any base acyclic orientation  D D  of  K n K_{n} , the digraph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) {\mathcal{AO}}(D)  is isomorphic to the weak Bruhat order  𝒲 ​ e ​ a ​ k n {\mathcal{W}}eak_{n} . 
 
 
 
 Our main motivation in the following sections is analyzing Formulation  2 , thus we say that  G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian  if  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 Example 2.7 . 
 
 The following are well-known: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Trees are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, see  Figure   1  (top left):
Since cube-graphs are Hamiltonian, and no orientation of a tree  T T  can be cyclic, we have that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( T ) ≃ Q n {\mathcal{AO}}(T)\simeq Q_{n}  is Hamiltonian. 
 
 (2)  
 
 Complete graphs are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian:
The permutahedral graph is Hamiltonian since the Steinhaus–Johnson–Trotter algorithm yields a Hamiltonian cycle on  Π n \Pi_{n} , see  Example   2.5  (or  [ 22 ]  or the survey  [ 38 , Section 3] ); and the graph of acyclic orientations of the complete graph  K n K_{n}  is isomorphic to  Π n \Pi_{n} , see  Example   2.6 . 
 
 (3)  
 
 The cycle graph  C n C_{n}  (see  Figure   1  (bottom), the wheel graph  W n W_{n}  (a cycle graph together with a node connected to all others), and the ladder graph  L n L_{n}  (two disjoint paths on  ( a 1 , … , a n ) (a_{1},\dots,a_{n})  and  ( b 1 , … , b n ) (b_{1},\dots,b_{n})  together with edges  a i ​ b i a_{i}b_{i}  for  i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n] ) are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian  if and only if  n n  is odd, see  [ 37 ] . 
 
 (4)  
 
 Chordal graphs (see  Figure   1  (top right)) are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, see  [ 37 , Theorem 4.1]  and  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII ,  KorberSchniedersStrickerWalizadeth25-HamiltonianCyclesSupersolvable ] . 
 
 (5)  
 
 Complete bipartite graphs  K m , n K_{m,n}  (disjoint sets of nodes  ( a 1 , … , a m ) (a_{1},\dots,a_{m})  and  ( b 1 , … , b n ) (b_{1},\dots,b_{n})  with edges  a i ​ b j a_{i}b_{j}  for all  i ∈ [ m ] i\in[m]  and  j ∈ [ n ] j\in[n] ) are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if either  m m  or  n n  is even.
The authors of  [ 37 ]  proved it using a parity argument, see  Lemma   2.9 .
As we will discuss further in Appendix  B , the case where  n n  and  m m  are both odd is open. 
 
 (6)  
 
 We give  22 22  examples of small graphs in Appendix  D , deciding  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity for some of them. 
 
 
 
 
 
 We are especially interested in operations on graphs that preserve  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity.
In particular, we repeatedly use the following lemma, called the  parity problem  in  [ 37 ] : 
 
 
 Definition 2.8 . 
 
 For a graph  G G , and  D D  an acyclic orientation of  G G , the associated  acyclic signature  is  σ ⁡ ( D ) ≔ ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | {\sigma(D)}\coloneqq\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D)| . 
 
 
 
 Lemma 2.9 . 
 
 Let  D D  be an orientation of a graph  G G .
If  σ ⁡ ( D ) ≠ 0 \sigma(D)\neq 0 , then  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If two orientations are adjacent, then their ranks differ by  1 1 .
Hence a bipartition of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is given by the parts  ⋃ k ​  even 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) \bigcup_{k\text{ even}}{\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D)  and  ⋃ k ​  odd 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) \bigcup_{k\text{ odd}}{\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D) .
The contrapositive of  Proposition   2.3   1  and the definition of the acyclic signature yield the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 2.10 . 
 
 We can state a similar lemma for cyclic orientations:
If  ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | ≠ 0 \sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D)|\neq 0 , then  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 Indeed, for all  k k , we have  𝒪 k ​ ( D ) = 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) ⊔ 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) {\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)={\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D)\sqcup{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D) , and by  Remark   2.2 , we know that  ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = 0 \sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)|=0 , so applying  Lemma   2.9  gives the claim. 
 
 
 
 Section   3.3  is devoted to the properties and the computation of acyclic signatures. 
 
 
 
 2.4.  Operations on graphs 

 
 For the convenience of the reader, we recall here the principal operations that we will use on (di)graphs. For (di)graphs  G = ( N , E ) , G ′ = ( N ′ , E ′ ) G=(N,E),G^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime}) , we define: 
 
 •  
 
 For some (directed) edge  e ∈ E e\in E , the  (edge) deletion   G ∖ e ≔ ( N , E ∖ { e } ) {G\smallsetminus e}\coloneqq\bigl(N,E\smallsetminus\{e\}\bigr) , and for some  ω ⊆ E \omega\subseteq E , the deletion  G ∖ ω ≔ ( N , E ∖ ω ) {G\smallsetminus\omega}\coloneqq\bigl(N,E\smallsetminus\omega\bigr) . 
 
 •  
 
 For a pair of nodes  (forming an edge or not)  i , j ∈ N i,j\in N , the  contraction   G / i ​ j = ( N ′ , E ′ ) {G/_{ij}}=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime})  with  N ′ = ( N ∖ { i , j } ) ∪ { v ∘ } N^{\prime}=\bigl(N\smallsetminus\{i,j\}\bigr)\cup\{v_{\circ}\}  for some arbitrary  v ∘ v_{\circ} , and  E ′ E^{\prime}  is the image (trimmed of its loops and multi-edges) of  E E  by the map  i ↦ v ∘ i\mapsto v_{\circ} ,  j ↦ v ∘ j\mapsto v_{\circ}  and  k ↦ k k\mapsto k  for  k ∈ N ∖ { i , j } k\in N\smallsetminus\{i,j\} .
For some  { e 1 , … , e r } ⊆ ( N 2 ) \{e_{1},\dots,e_{r}\}\subseteq\binom{N}{2} , we define the contraction  G / Y ≔ ( … ( G / e 1 ) / e 2 … ) / e r {G/_{Y}}\coloneqq(\dots(G/_{e_{1}})/_{e_{2}}\dots)/_{e_{r}} .
The isomorphism class of  G / Y G/_{Y}  does not depend on the order of the pairs of nodes. 
 
 •  
 
 The  union   G ∪ G ′ ≔ ( N ⊔ N ′ , E ⊔ E ′ ) {G\cup G^{\prime}}\coloneqq(N\sqcup N^{\prime},E\sqcup E^{\prime}) . 
 
 •  
 
 G × G ′ {G\times G^{\prime}}  the  Cartesian product  on the node set  N × N ′ N\times N^{\prime}  and edge set 
 

 
 
 { ( ( x , x ′ ) , ( x , y ′ ) ) ; x ′ ​ y ′ ∈ E ′ , x ∈ N } ∪ { ( ( x , x ′ ) , ( y , x ′ ) ) ; x ​ y ∈ E , x ′ ∈ N ′ } . \Bigl\{\bigl((x,x^{\prime}),(x,y^{\prime})\bigr)~;~x^{\prime}y^{\prime}\in E^{\prime},\,x\in N\Bigr\}\cup\Bigl\{\bigl((x,x^{\prime}),(y,x^{\prime})\bigr)~;~xy\in E,\,x^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}\Bigr\}\,. 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 For disjoint cliques  K 1 , … , K r K_{1},\dots,K_{r}  of  G G  and disjoint cliques  K 1 ′ , … , K r ′ K_{1}^{\prime},\dots,K_{r}^{\prime}  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  with  | K p | = | K p ′ | |K_{p}|=|K_{p}^{\prime}|  for all  p ∈ [ r ] p\in[r] , and bijections  φ p : K p → K p ′ \varphi_{p}:K_{p}\to K^{\prime}_{p} , the  gluing  of  G G  and  G ′ G^{\prime}  along  φ 1 , … , φ r \varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{r}  is the graph  G ⊕ φ 1 , … , φ r G ′ ≔ ( G ∪ G ′ ) / { ( i , φ p ( i ) ; i ∈ K i , p ∈ [ r ] } {G\oplus_{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{r}}G^{\prime}}\coloneqq(G\cup G^{\prime})/_{\{(i,\varphi_{p}(i)~;~i\in K_{i},\,p\in[r]\}} . We abbreviate  G ⊕ G ′ {G\oplus G^{\prime}}  when  K 1 , … , K r K_{1},\dots,K_{r} ,  K 1 ′ , … , K r ′ K^{\prime}_{1},\dots,K_{r}^{\prime} ,  φ 1 , … , φ p \varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{p}  are clear from the context.
When  r = 1 r=1 , the gluing of  G G  and  G ′ G^{\prime}  on  K K  and  K ′ K^{\prime}  with respect to  φ \varphi  is called a  k k -sum  where  k ≔ | K | = | K ′ | k\coloneqq|K|=|K^{\prime}| , denoted  G ⊕ φ G ′ {G\oplus_{\varphi}G^{\prime}}  or  G ⊕ k G ′ {G\oplus_{k}G^{\prime}}  when  K , K ′ , φ K,K^{\prime},\varphi  are clear from the context. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Non- 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity criteria: modularity, and acyclic polynomials 

 
 3.1.  Prelude: Expanding on the modularity criterion of Savage, Squire and West 

 
 The following  Lemmas   3.3  and  3.4  are directly inspired from the method employed in the proof of  [ 37 , Theorem 3.2]  where Savage, Squire and West showed that ladder graphs  L n ≔ P n × K 2 L_{n}\coloneqq P_{n}\times K_{2}  are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if  n n  is even.
They prove the reciprocal in  [ 37 , Section 4.2] , but this is not the purpose of the present section, rather its aim is to develop a useful tool out of the ideas around the number of acyclic orientations modulo  4 4 .
In particular, we want to understand the  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity of the Cartesian product  G × Q n G\times Q_{n}  where  Q n Q_{n}  is the  n th n^{\text{th}}  cube-graph. 
 
 
 The reader inclined to learn more about classes of graphs which are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian is invited to jump directly to  Corollary   3.10 .
We will need the following two lemmata which are already proven in  [ 37 ] .
We will discuss their proofs in  Section   3.2 . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.1 . 
 
 For any graph  G G , the number of acyclic orientations  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  is even. 
 
 
 
 Lemma 3.2   ( [ 37 , Lemma 3.1] ) . 
 
 If  G G  has an even number of edges and  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  is not divisible by  4 4 , then  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Figure 2 .  (Left) Decomposition of the number of acyclic orientations  ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) \psi\bigl(\oplus^{\ell}_{u,v}G\bigr) .
(Right) Prism over a tree  T × K 2 T\times K_{2} . In  red  the tree  T T , in  blue  the path of length  3 3  that is glued between the nodes  v v  and  v ′ v^{\prime} , through the leaf  u u  of  T T . 
 
 
 Lemma 3.3 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a graph with an edge  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E  and  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 .
Then: 
 

 
 
 ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) = ( 2 ℓ − 1 ) ⋅ ψ ( G ) . \psi\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G\bigr)=\bigl(2^{\ell}-1\bigr)\cdot\psi(G)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 In particular,  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  is divisible by  4 4  if and only if  ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) \psi\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G\bigr)  is divisible by  4 4 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  x x  be the node neighboring  u u  on the induced path of length  ℓ \ell  of  ⊕ u , v ℓ G \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G .
We perform a deletion-contraction on the edge  u ​ x ux .
By Stanley’s formula, we get the following (where  P ℓ − 1 P_{\ell-1}  denotes a path of length  ℓ − 1 \ell-1 ), see  Figure   2  (left): 
 

 
 
 ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) = ψ ( G ⊕ 1 P ℓ − 1 ) + ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ − 1 G ) \psi\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G\bigr)=\psi(G\oplus_{1}P_{\ell-1})+\psi\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell-1}G) 
 
 
 
 
 Recall that  ψ ( H ⊕ 1 H ′ ) = ψ ( H ) ⋅ ψ ( H ′ ) \psi(H\oplus_{1}H^{\prime})=\psi(H)\cdot\psi(H^{\prime}) .
We get  ψ ( G ⊕ 1 P ℓ − 1 ) = ψ ( G ) ⋅ 2 ℓ − 1 \psi(G\oplus_{1}P_{\ell-1})=\psi(G)\cdot 2^{\ell-1} .
We proceed by induction, until  ℓ = 2 \ell=2 , where the contraction  ⊕ u , v ℓ − 1 G \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell-1}G  is just  G G  itself since  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E .
Hence, we get: 
 

 
 
 ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) = ( ∑ k = 1 ℓ − 1 2 k ) ⋅ ψ ( G ) + ψ ( G ) = ( 2 ℓ − 1 ) ⋅ ψ ( G ) \psi\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G\bigr)=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-1}2^{k}\right)\cdot\psi(G)+\psi(G)=\bigl(2^{\ell}-1\bigr)\cdot\psi(G) 
 
 
 
 
 Note that  ( 2 ℓ − 1 ) \bigl(2^{\ell}-1\bigr)  is odd for  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 , so it is co-prime with  4 4 .
Thus, by Euclid’s lemma,  4 4  divides  ψ ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) \psi\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G\bigr)  if and only if  4 4  divides  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Recall that  K 2 K_{2}  is the graph with two nodes and one edge. 
 
 
 Theorem 3.4 . 
 
 Let  T T  be a tree. We have  ψ ⁡ ( T × K 2 ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(T\times K_{2})\equiv 2\mod 4 .
Moreover, if  T T  has an even number of nodes (i.e. an odd number of edges), then  T × K 2 T\times K_{2}  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  n n  be the number of nodes of  T T  (so that  n − 1 n-1  is its number of edges).
Consider a leaf  u u  of  T T , and  v v  its neighboring node in  T T .
We denote  T ′ T^{\prime}  and  T ′′ T^{\prime\prime}  the two copies of  T T  in  T × K 2 T\times K_{2} .
Hence, there is an edge  v ′ ​ v ′′ v^{\prime}v^{\prime\prime}  in  T × K 2 T\times K_{2} , and the graph  T × K 2 T\times K_{2}  is isomorphic to  ⊕ v ′ ​ v ′′ 3 ( T ∖ { u } ) × K 2 \oplus_{v^{\prime}v^{\prime\prime}}^{3}(T\smallsetminus\{u\})\times K_{2} , see  Figure   2  (right).
By  Lemma   3.3 , we get that if  ψ ⁡ ( ( T ∖ { u } ) × K 2 ) \psi\bigl((T\smallsetminus\{u\})\times K_{2}\bigr)  is not divisible by  4 4 , then neither is  ψ ⁡ ( T × K 2 ) \psi(T\times K_{2}) .
By iteratively stripping  T T  of its leaves, we obtain that if  4 4  does not divide  ψ ⁡ ( { x } × K 2 ) \psi(\{x\}\times K_{2})  where  x x  denotes any arbitrarily chosen node of  T T , then  4 4  does not divide  ψ ⁡ ( T × K 2 ) \psi(T\times K_{2}) .
Since  ψ ⁡ ( { x } × K 2 ) = ψ ⁡ ( K 2 ) = 2 \psi(\{x\}\times K_{2})=\psi(K_{2})=2 , and the number of acyclic orientations of a graph is always even, we get the first claim. 
 
 
 Moreover, the number of edges of  T × K 2 T\times K_{2}  is  3 ​ n − 2 3n-2  which is even if and only if  n n  is even.
We deduce by  Lemma   3.2  that if  n n  is even, then  T × K 2 T\times K_{2}  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proposition 3.5 . 
 
 Let  e e  be an edge of a graph  G G , and  Q = K m 1 × ⋯ × K m s Q=K_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times K_{m_{s}}  be a Cartesian product of cliques where  m i = 2 k i m_{i}=2^{k_{i}}  for some  k i ≥ 1 k_{i}\geq 1  for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] .
Then, we have: 
 

 
 
 ψ ( G × Q ) ≡ ψ ( ( G / e ) × Q ) + ψ ( ( G ∖ e ) × Q ) mod 4 . \psi(G\times Q)\equiv\psi\bigl((G/_{e})\times Q\bigr)+\psi\bigl((G\smallsetminus e)\times Q\bigr)\mod 4\,. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  m ≔ 2 k m\coloneqq 2^{k}  for some  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 .
We start by proving the case  s = 1 s=1 , that is  ψ ( G × K m ) ≡ ψ ( ( G / e ) × K m ) + ψ ( ( G ∖ e ) × K m ) mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{m})\equiv\psi\bigl((G/_{e})\times K_{m}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((G\smallsetminus e)\times K_{m}\bigr)\mod 4 .
For  u ∈ N ⁡ ( K m ) u\in N(K_{m}) , we denote  G × u G\times u  the copy of  G G  associated to the node  u u .
Pick an edge  i ​ j ij  of  G G , and, for  u ∈ N ⁡ ( K m ) u\in N(K_{m}) , let  i ​ j × u ij\times u  be the copy the edge  i ​ j ij  in  G × u G\times u .
We are going to apply Stanley’s deletion-contraction formula to all the copies of  i ​ j ij .
Since  i ​ j × u ij\times u  and  i ​ j × v ij\times v  do not share any node as long as  u ≠ v u\neq v , contracting or deleting  i ​ j × u ij\times u  leaves  i ​ j × v ij\times v  unaltered.
Consequently, we can delete or contract all the copies of  i ​ j ij  independently, leading to an expression of  ψ ⁡ ( G × K m ) \psi(G\times K_{m})  as a sum of  | 2 N ⁡ ( K m ) | = 2 m |2^{N(K_{m})}|=2^{m}  terms.
For  X ⊆ N ⁡ ( K m ) X\subseteq N(K_{m}) , we denote  H ⁡ ( X ) H(X)  the graph obtained from  G × K m G\times K_{m}  by contracting all  i ​ j × u ij\times u  for  u ∈ X u\in X  and deleting all  i ​ j × v ij\times v  for  v ∉ X v\notin X .
Using Stanley’s formula, we get: 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 ψ ⁡ ( G × K m ) = ∑ X ⊆ N ⁡ ( K m ) ψ ⁡ ( H ⁡ ( X ) ) \psi(G\times K_{m})=\sum_{X\subseteq N(K_{m})}\psi(H(X)) 
 
 
 
 
 So far, we have not yet used the fact that  K m K_{m}  is a complete graphs.
The group of automorphisms of the complete graph  K m K_{m}  is the symmetric group on  N ⁡ ( K m ) = [ m ] N(K_{m})=[m] .
In particular, for any  X , Y ⊆ N ⁡ ( K m ) X,Y\subseteq N(K_{m})  such that  | X | = | Y | |X|=|Y| , there exists an automorphism  σ \sigma  of  K m K_{m}  that  σ ⁡ ( X ) = Y \sigma(X)=Y .
This extends to an automorphism on  G × K m G\times K_{m}  by defining  σ ¯ : i × u ↦ i × σ ⁡ ( u ) \overline{\sigma}:i\times u\mapsto i\times\sigma(u) .
Moreover, we have  σ ¯ ​ ( H ​ ( X ) ) = H ​ ( Y ) \overline{\sigma}(H(X))=H(Y) , so the graphs  H ⁡ ( X ) H(X)  and  H ⁡ ( Y ) H(Y)  are isomorphic.
In particular,  ψ ⁡ ( H ⁡ ( X ) ) = ψ ⁡ ( H ⁡ ( Y ) ) \psi(H(X))=\psi(H(Y))  as long as  | X | = | Y | |X|=|Y| .
Consequently, we denote  H ⁡ ( k ) H(k)  the graph  H ⁡ ( X ) H(X)  for any  X ⊆ N ⁡ ( K m ) X\subseteq N(K_{m})  with  | X | = k |X|=k .
Note that  H ( 0 ) = ( G / i ​ j ) × K m H(0)=(G/_{ij})\times K_{m}  since all the copies of  i ​ j ij  are contracted in  H ⁡ ( 0 ) H(0) , while  H ⁡ ( m ) = ( G ∖ i ​ j ) × K m H(m)=(G\smallsetminus ij)\times K_{m}  since all the copies of  i ​ j ij  are deleted in  H ⁡ ( m ) H(m) .
Thus, grouping subsets by sizes,  Equation   3.1  simplifies to: 
 

 
 
 ψ ⁡ ( G × K m ) = ∑ k = 0 m ( m k ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ⁡ ( k ) ) \psi(G\times K_{m})=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\binom{m}{k}\cdot\psi(H(k)) 
 
 
 
 
 It remains to use the fact that  m = 2 k m=2^{k}  and that  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  is an even number for any graph  G G , by  Lemma   3.1 .
By Lucas’s theorem  [ 26 ] , for any  k ∉ { 0 , 2 k } k\notin\{0,2^{k}\} , the binomial coefficient  ( 2 k k ) \binom{2^{k}}{k}  is even.
So, for any  k ∉ { 0 , 2 k } k\notin\{0,2^{k}\} , the term  ( m k ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ⁡ ( k ) ) \binom{m}{k}\cdot\psi(H(k))  is divisible by  4 4 : all the terms of the above sum except for  k = 0 k=0  and  k = 2 k k=2^{k}  are equal to  0 0  modulo  4 4 .
We obtain: 
 

 
 
 ψ ( G × K 2 k ) ≡ ψ ( ( G ∖ i j ) × K 2 k ) + ψ ( ( G / i ​ j ) × K 2 k ) mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2^{k}})\equiv\psi\bigl((G\smallsetminus ij)\times K_{2^{k}}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((G/_{ij})\times K_{2^{k}}\bigr)\mod 4 
 
 
 
 
 This proves that the function  f : G ↦ ( ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 k ) mod 4 ) f:G\mapsto\bigl(\psi(G\times K_{2^{k}})\mod 4\bigr)  defined from the set of graphs towards  ℤ / 4 ​ ℤ \mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}  satisfies the same deletion-contraction formula as the function  g : G ↦ ( ψ ⁡ ( G ) mod 4 ) g:G\mapsto\bigl(\psi(G)\mod 4\bigr) ,  i.e.  both  f ( G ) = f ( G / e ) + f ( G ∖ e ) f(G)=f(G/_{e})+f(G\smallsetminus e)  and  g ( G ) = g ( G / e ) + g ( G ∖ e ) g(G)=g(G/_{e})+g(G\smallsetminus e)  for any edge  e e  of  G G .
Now fix  Q = K m 1 × ⋯ × K m s Q=K_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times K_{m_{s}}  to be a Cartesian product of cliques where  m i = 2 k i m_{i}=2^{k_{i}}  for some  k i ≥ 1 k_{i}\geq 1  for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] .
Since we have only used the deletion-contraction formula for  g g  (not the initialization for this recursive formula), one can apply the exact same reasoning to  f f  inductively in order to prove that  ψ ( G × Q ) ≡ ψ ( ( G / e ) × Q ) + ψ ( ( G ∖ e ) × Q ) mod 4 \psi(G\times Q)\equiv\psi\bigl((G/_{e})\times Q\bigr)+\psi\bigl((G\smallsetminus e)\times Q\bigr)\mod 4 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Theorem 3.6 . 
 
 For any  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , the  n ​ th n\textsuperscript{th}  cube graph  Q n ≔ K 2 × ⋯ × K 2 Q_{n}\coloneqq K_{2}\times\dots\times K_{2}  satisfies  ψ ⁡ ( Q n ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(Q_{n})\equiv 2\mod 4 .
Consequently,  Q n Q_{n}  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The argument boils down to applying  Lemma   3.2  using the first part of the theorem, since  Q n Q_{n}  has an even number of edges.
It remains to prove the first claim. 
 
 
 Recall that  K 2 K_{2}  is just an edge.
For  n = 1 n=1 , we have  ψ ⁡ ( Q 1 ) = ψ ⁡ ( K 2 ) = 2 \psi(Q_{1})=\psi(K_{2})=2 . 
 
 
 For  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , note that  Q n = K 2 × K 2 × ⋯ × K 2 = K 2 × Q n − 1 Q_{n}=K_{2}\times K_{2}\times\dots\times K_{2}=K_{2}\times Q_{n-1} .
We denote  e e  the edge of this distinguished “first” copy of  K 2 K_{2} .
By  Proposition   3.5 , we have: 
 

 
 
 ψ ( Q n ) ≡ ψ ( ( K 2 / e ) × Q n − 1 ) + ψ ( ( K 2 ∖ e ) × Q n − 1 ) ≡ ψ ( Q n − 1 ) + ψ ( Q n − 1 ⊔ Q n − 1 ) mod 4 . \psi(Q_{n})\equiv\psi\bigl((K_{2}/_{e})\times Q_{n-1}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((K_{2}\smallsetminus e)\times Q_{n-1}\bigr)\equiv\psi(Q_{n-1})+\psi(Q_{n-1}\sqcup Q_{n-1})\mod 4\,. 
 
 
 Last, remark that  ψ ⁡ ( H ⊔ H ′ ) = ψ ⁡ ( H ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ′ ) \psi(H\sqcup H^{\prime})=\psi(H)\cdot\psi(H^{\prime})  when  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  are node-disjoint graphs.
As  ψ ⁡ ( H ) \psi(H)  is divisible by  2 2  for all graph  H H , by  Lemma   3.1 , we get that  4 4  divides  ψ ⁡ ( H ⊔ H ′ ) \psi(H\sqcup H^{\prime})  when  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  are node-disjoint graphs.
Thus, we get:
 ψ ⁡ ( Q n ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( Q n − 1 ) mod 4 \psi(Q_{n})\equiv\psi(Q_{n-1})\mod 4 .
By induction, we get  ψ ⁡ ( Q n ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(Q_{n})\equiv 2\mod 4  for all  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 For the sake of completeness, we also prove the following: 
 
 
 Proposition 3.7 . 
 
 Let  Q = K p × K m 1 × ⋯ × K m s Q=K_{p}\times K_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times K_{m_{s}}  be a Cartesian product of cliques where  m i = 2 k i m_{i}=2^{k_{i}}  for some  k i ≥ 1 k_{i}\geq 1  for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s]  with  s ≥ 1 s\geq 1 .
If  p ≥ 3 p\geq 3 , then  ψ ⁡ ( Q ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(Q)\equiv 0\mod 4 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  u u  be a node of  K p K_{p} , and let  e 1 , … , e p − 1 e_{1},\dots,e_{p-1}  be the edges adjacent to  u u  in  K p K_{p} .
Note that  K p / e i K_{p}/_{e_{i}}  is isomorphic to  K p − 1 K_{p-1} , while  e j e_{j}  is an edge in  K p ∖ e i K_{p}\smallsetminus e_{i}  as long as  i ≠ j i\neq j .
Furthermore,  ( K p ∖ X ) / e i (K_{p}\smallsetminus X)/_{e_{i}}  is isomorphic to  K p − 1 K_{p-1}  for any  X ⊆ { e 1 , … , e p − 1 } ∖ { e i } X\subseteq\{e_{1},\dots,e_{p-1}\}\smallsetminus\{e_{i}\} .
Hence, we can perform  p − 1 p-1  deletion-contractions on the edges  e 1 , … , e p − 1 e_{1},\dots,e_{p-1}  using  Proposition   3.5 .
We get, modulo  4 4  (where we denote  Q ′ ≔ K m 1 × ⋯ × K m s Q^{\prime}\coloneqq K_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times K_{m_{s}} ): 
 

 
 
 ψ ⁡ ( K p × Q ′ ) \displaystyle\psi(K_{p}\times Q^{\prime}) 
 ≡ ψ ( ( K p / e 1 × Q ′ ) + ψ ( ( K p ∖ e 1 × Q ′ ) \displaystyle\equiv\psi\bigl((K_{p}/_{e_{1}}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((K_{p}\smallsetminus{e_{1}}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 ≡ ψ ( K p − 1 × Q ′ ) + ψ ( ( ( K p ∖ e 1 ) / e 2 ) × Q ′ ) + ψ ( ( K p ∖ { e 1 , e 2 } ) × Q ′ ) \displaystyle\equiv\psi\bigl(K_{p-1}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)+\psi\bigl(((K_{p}\smallsetminus{e_{1}})/_{e_{2}})\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((K_{p}\smallsetminus\{e_{1},e_{2}\})\times Q^{\prime}\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 ≡ 2 ⋅ ψ ( K p − 1 × Q ′ ) + ψ ( ( ( K p ∖ { e 1 , e 2 } ) / e 3 ) × Q ′ ) + ψ ( ( K p ∖ { e 1 , e 2 , e 3 } ) × Q ′ ) \displaystyle\equiv 2\cdot\psi\bigl(K_{p-1}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)+\psi\bigl(((K_{p}\smallsetminus\{e_{1},e_{2}\})/_{e_{3}})\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((K_{p}\smallsetminus\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\})\times Q^{\prime}\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 ≡ … \displaystyle\equiv\ldots 
 
 
 
 
 ≡ ( p − 1 ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( K p − 1 × Q ′ ) + ψ ⁡ ( ( K p − 1 ⊔ { u } ) × Q ′ ) \displaystyle\equiv(p-1)\cdot\psi\bigl(K_{p-1}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)+\psi((K_{p-1}\sqcup\{u\})\times Q^{\prime}) 
 
 
 
 
 Recall that  ψ ⁡ ( H ) \psi(H)  is divisible by  2 2  for any graph  H H  by  Lemma   3.1 .
Since  K p − 1 ⊔ { u } K_{p-1}\sqcup\{u\}  is a disconnected graph,  ψ ⁡ ( ( K p − 1 ⊔ { u } ) × Q ′ ) \psi((K_{p-1}\sqcup\{u\})\times Q^{\prime})  is divisible by  4 4  (because  ψ ⁡ ( H ⊔ H ′ ) = ψ ⁡ ( H ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ′ ) \psi(H\sqcup H^{\prime})=\psi(H)\cdot\psi(H^{\prime})  when  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  are node-disjoint graphs).
Besides, if  p = 3 p=3 , then  4 4  divides  ( p − 1 ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( K p − 1 × Q ′ ) (p-1)\cdot\psi\bigl(K_{p-1}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)  as a product of even numbers; whereas if  p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , then  4 4  divides  ( p − 1 ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( K p − 1 × Q ′ ) (p-1)\cdot\psi\bigl(K_{p-1}\times Q^{\prime}\bigr)  by induction on  p p .
This gives the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 3.8 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a graph and  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , then
 ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2})\equiv\psi(G\times Q_{n})\mod 4 . 
 
 
 Moreover, if  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) \psi(G\times K_{2})  is not divisible by  4 4  and if either  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2  or  G G  has an even number of nodes, then  G × Q n G\times Q_{n}  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Consider the two functions on graphs with values in  ℤ / 4 ​ ℤ \mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}  defined by  f : G ↦ ( ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) mod 4 ) f:G\mapsto\bigl(\psi(G\times K_{2})\mod 4\bigr)  and  g : G ↦ ( ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) mod 4 ) g:G\mapsto\bigl(\psi(G\times Q_{n})\mod 4\bigr) .
By  Proposition   3.5 , the functions  f f  and  g g  satisfy the same inductive formula:
 f ( G ) = f ( G / e ) + f ( G ∖ e ) f(G)=f(G/_{e})+f(G\smallsetminus e)  and  g ( G ) = g ( G / e ) + g ( G ∖ e ) g(G)=g(G/_{e})+g(G\smallsetminus e)  for any edge  e e  of  G G .
Hence, to prove that  f f  and  g g  are equal, it is enough to show that they are equal for the initialization graphs,  i.e.  for any graph  G G  which is composed of isolated nodes with no edge. 
 
 
 Let  m m  be the number of nodes of such  G G .
If  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , then both  G × K 2 G\times K_{2}  and  G × Q n G\times Q_{n}  have several connected components, so  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) \psi(G\times K_{2})  and  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) \psi(G\times Q_{n})  are divisible by  4 4  because  ψ ⁡ ( H ⊔ H ′ ) = ψ ⁡ ( H ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ′ ) \psi(H\sqcup H^{\prime})=\psi(H)\cdot\psi(H^{\prime})  when  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  are node-disjoint graphs, and  ψ ⁡ ( H ) \psi(H)  is divisible by  2 2  for any graph  H H  by  Lemma   3.1 .
If  m = 1 m=1 , then  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) = ψ ⁡ ( K 2 ) = 2 \psi(G\times K_{2})=\psi(K_{2})=2 , and, by  Theorem   3.6 ,  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) = ψ ⁡ ( Q n ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(G\times Q_{n})=\psi(Q_{n})\equiv 2\mod 4 .
Thus  f = g f=g  by induction, as claimed. 
 
 
 The “moreover” part boils down to using  Lemma   3.2  together with the first part of the corollary, where the condition that  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2  or  G G  has an even number of nodes ensures that condition ensures that  G × Q n G\times Q_{n}  has an even number of edges.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.9 . 
 
 Let  Q = K m 1 × ⋯ × K m s Q=K_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times K_{m_{s}}  with  m i = 2 k i m_{i}=2^{k_{i}}  for some  k i ≥ 1 k_{i}\geq 1  for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] .
The proof of  Corollary   3.8  suggest that we can explicitly compute  ( ψ ⁡ ( G × Q ) mod 4 ) \bigl(\psi(G\times Q)\mod 4\bigr)  running the deletion-contraction algorithm on  G G  itself, instead of running it on  G × Q G\times Q .
Indeed, the functions with values in  ℤ / 4 ​ ℤ \mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}  defined as  f : G ↦ ( ψ ⁡ ( G × Q ) mod 4 ) f:G\mapsto\bigl(\psi(G\times Q)\mod 4\bigr)  and  g : G ↦ ( ψ ⁡ ( G ) mod 4 ) g:G\mapsto\bigl(\psi(G)\mod 4\bigr)  satisfy the same recursion,  i.e.   f ( G ) = f ( G / e ) + f ( G ∖ e ) f(G)=f(G/_{e})+f(G\smallsetminus e)  and  g ( G ) = g ( G / e ) + g ( G ∖ e ) g(G)=g(G/_{e})+g(G\smallsetminus e)  for any edge  e e  of  G G .
However, it is false to say that  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( G ) mod 4 \psi(G\times Q)\equiv\psi(G)\mod 4  because  f f  and  g g  do not have the same value for initialization graphs:
If  G G  consists in  m m  isolated nodes and no edge, then  f ⁡ ( G ) ≡ f ⁡ ( Q ) f(G)\equiv f(Q)  if  m = 1 m=1  and  f ⁡ ( G ) = 0 f(G)=0  if  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , but  g ⁡ ( G ) = 1 g(G)=1  for all  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 . 
 
 
 For the case  Q = K 2 Q=K_{2} , and more generally for the cube-graph  Q = Q n Q=Q_{n} , one can compute recursively  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2})\mod 4  as follows.
Fix a graph  G G .
 Running  the deletion-contraction algorithm on  G G   until we get forests , means if  G G  is a forest we do nothing, and otherwise we choose an edge  e e  of  G G , then run the deletion-contraction algorithm on  G / e G/_{e}  and on  G ∖ e G\smallsetminus e  until we get forests.
Let  t t  be the number of trees produced by running the deletion-contraction algorithm on  G G  until we obtain forests, then  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) ≡ 2 ⋅ t mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2})\equiv\psi(G\times Q_{n})\equiv 2\cdot t\mod 4 .
Hence,  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) \psi(G\times K_{2})  and  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) \psi(G\times Q_{n})  are divisible by  4 4  if and only if  t t  is even.
To prove this claim, note that, by  Propositions   3.5  and  3.8 , we have  ψ ( G × Q n ) ≡ ψ ( G × K 2 ) ≡ ψ ( ( G / e ) × K 2 ) + ψ ( ( G ∖ e ) × K 2 ) \psi(G\times Q_{n})\equiv\psi(G\times K_{2})\equiv\psi\bigl((G/_{e})\times K_{2}\bigr)+\psi\bigl((G\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}\bigr) , and by  Theorem   3.4 , we have  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2})\equiv 2\mod 4  when  G G  is a tree, while  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2})\equiv 0\mod 4  when  G G  is disconnected. 
 
 
 On the other hand, let  k 1 ≥ 2 k_{1}\geq 2  and  Q = K m 1 × ⋯ × K m s Q=K_{m_{1}}\times\dots\times K_{m_{s}}  with  m i = 2 k i m_{i}=2^{k_{i}}  for some  k i ≥ 1 k_{i}\geq 1  for all  i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 .
By the same reasoning as in the proof of  Corollary   3.8 ,  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q ) \psi(G\times Q)  is equivalent modulo  4 4  to a certain multiple of  ψ ⁡ ( Q ) \psi(Q) .
Since  ψ ⁡ ( Q ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(Q)\equiv 0\mod 4  by  Proposition   3.7 , this implies  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(G\times Q)\equiv 0\mod 4  for all  G G , and yields no conclusion on whether  G × Q G\times Q  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian or not. 
 
 
 
 We can now prove  Theorem   A  by listing classes of graphs which are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Beyond the usual path on  n n  nodes  P n P_{n} , cycle on  n n  nodes  C n C_{n} , star with  n n  outer nodes  K 1 , n K_{1,n} , and complete graph on  n n  nodes  K n K_{n} , we need to define the following two classes of graphs. 
 
 
 The  wheel graph   W n W_{n}  is the cycle  C n C_{n}  together with a  central node  adjacent to all nodes of  C n C_{n} . 
 
 
 For a tree  T = ( [ s ] , E ) T=([s],E)  and numbers  n 1 , … , n s ≥ 3 n_{1},\dots,n_{s}\geq 3 , a  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles  is any graph isomorphic to a  2 2 -sum  H ⊕ 2 C n s H\oplus_{2}C_{n_{s}}  where  H H  is a  ( T ∖ s , n 1 , … , n s − 1 ) (T\smallsetminus s,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s-1}) -tree of cycles (of course for  s = 1 s=1 , a  ( T , n 1 ) (T,\,n_{1}) -tree of cycle is just a cycle graph  C n 1 C_{n_{1}} ). 
 
 
 Corollary 3.10 . 
 
 The following graphs are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Ladder graphs  L n ≔ P n × K 2 L_{n}\coloneqq P_{n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  even, 
 
 (2)  
 
 Book graphs  B n ≔ K 1 , n × K 2 B_{n}\coloneqq K_{1,n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  odd, 
 
 (3)  
 
 Prism graphs  Pr n ≔ C n × K 2 \mathrm{Pr}_{n}\coloneqq C_{n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  even, 
 
 (4)  
 
 Toroidal grid graphs  T 4 , m ≔ C 4 × C m T_{4,m}\coloneqq C_{4}\times C_{m}  for any  m m  even, 
 
 (5)  
 
 Cube graphs  Q n = K 2 × ⋯ × K 2 Q_{n}=K_{2}\times\dots\times K_{2} , with  n n  copies of  K 2 K_{2} , for any  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , 
 
 (6)  
 
 The graph  T × Q n T\times Q_{n}  for any tree  T T  where either  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2  or  T T  has an even number of nodes, 
 
 (7)  
 
 Thick wheel graphs  TW n ≔ W n × K 2 \mathrm{TW}_{n}\coloneqq W_{n}\times K_{2}  for any  n n  odd, 
 
 (8)  
 
 Any  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles for any tree  T T  and any  n 1 , … , n s ≥ 1 n_{1},\dots,n_{s}\geq 1 , if there is some  i ∈ [ d ] i\in[d]  such that  n i n_{i}  is even and if  ∑ i = 1 s ( n i − 1 ) \sum_{i=1}^{s}(n_{i}-1)  is odd. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 ( 1 ) and ( 2 ):
Ladder and book graphs are of the form  T × K 2 T\times K_{2}  where  T T  is a tree (respectively a path and a star), so by  Theorem   3.4 , they are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian when they have an even number of nodes.
Ladder graphs already appear in  [ 37 , Theorem 3.2] . 
 
 
 ( 3 ):
Let  Pr n = C n × K 2 \mathrm{Pr}_{n}=C_{n}\times K_{2} .
We are going to prove by induction that  ψ ⁡ ( Pr n ) \psi(\mathrm{Pr}_{n})  is divisible by  4 4  if  n n  is odd, and not divisible by  4 4  if  n n  is even.
We denote  C n ′ C_{n}^{\prime}  and  C n ′′ C_{n}^{\prime\prime}  the two copies of  C n C_{n}  in  Pr n \mathrm{Pr}_{n} .
Fix any edge  e e  of  C n C_{n} , and let  e ′ e^{\prime}  and  e ′′ e^{\prime\prime}  the copies of  e e  in  C n ′ C_{n}^{\prime}  and  C n ′′ C_{n}^{\prime\prime} .
We denote  H ′ H^{\prime}  (resp.  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime} ) the graph obtained by contracting  e ′ e^{\prime}  and deleting  e ′′ e^{\prime\prime}  (resp. by deleting  e ′ e^{\prime}  and contracting  e ′′ e^{\prime\prime} ) in  Pr n \mathrm{Pr}_{n} .
Note that  H ′ H^{\prime}  and  H ′′ H^{\prime\prime}  are isomorphic.
By deletion-contraction on the edges  e ′ e^{\prime}  and  e ′′ e^{\prime\prime} , we have (using  C n / e ≃ C n − 1 C_{n}/_{e}\simeq C_{n-1} ): 
 

 
 
 ψ ⁡ ( Pr n ) \displaystyle\psi(\mathrm{Pr}_{n}) 
 = ψ ( ( C n ∖ e ) × K 2 ) + ψ ( H ′ ) + ψ ( H ′′ ) + ψ ( ( C n / e ) × K 2 ) \displaystyle=\psi\bigl((C_{n}\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}\bigr)+\psi(H^{\prime})+\psi(H^{\prime\prime})+\psi\bigl((C_{n}/_{e})\times K_{2}\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 = ψ ⁡ ( ( C n ∖ e ) × K 2 ) + 2 ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ′ ) + ψ ⁡ ( Pr n − 1 ) \displaystyle=\psi\bigl((C_{n}\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}\bigr)+2\cdot\psi(H^{\prime})+\psi(\mathrm{Pr}_{n-1}) 
 
 
 
 
 ≡ ψ ⁡ ( ( C n ∖ e ) × K 2 ) + ψ ⁡ ( Pr n − 1 ) mod 4 \displaystyle\equiv\psi\bigl((C_{n}\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}\bigr)+\psi(\mathrm{Pr}_{n-1})\mod 4 
 
 
 
 
 Since  ( C n ∖ e ) × K 2 (C_{n}\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}  is a ladder graph on an even number of nodes,  4 4  does not divide  ψ ⁡ ( ( C n ∖ e ) × K 2 ) \psi\bigl((C_{n}\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}\bigr) , by the proof of ( 1 ), so  ψ ⁡ ( ( C n ∖ e ) × K 2 ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi\bigl((C_{n}\smallsetminus e)\times K_{2}\bigr)\equiv 2\mod 4 .
Hence, by induction,  ψ ⁡ ( Pr n ) ≡ 2 ⋅ ( n − 1 ) mod 4 \psi(\mathrm{Pr}_{n})\equiv 2\cdot(n-1)\mod 4  because  Pr 2 = C 4 \mathrm{Pr}_{2}=C_{4}  with  ψ ⁡ ( C 4 ) = 14 ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(C_{4})=14\equiv 2\mod 4 .
Thus, if  n n  is even,  ψ ⁡ ( Pr n ) \psi(\mathrm{Pr}_{n})  is not divisible by  4 4 .
As the number of edges of  Pr n \mathrm{Pr}_{n}  is  3 ​ n 3n , we get the claim. 
 
 
 ( 4 ):
We just proved that  ψ ⁡ ( C m × K 2 ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(C_{m}\times K_{2})\equiv 2\mod 4 .
Since  C 4 = K 2 × K 2 = Q 2 C_{4}=K_{2}\times K_{2}=Q_{2} , by  Corollary   3.8 , we have  ψ ⁡ ( C m × C 4 ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( C m × K 2 ) ≢ 0 mod 4 \psi(C_{m}\times C_{4})\equiv\psi(C_{m}\times K_{2})\not\equiv 0\mod 4 , so by  Lemma   3.2 , we get that  T 4 , m = C m × C 4 T_{4,m}=C_{m}\times C_{4}  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian for all  m m  even because  T 4 , m T_{4,m}  has an even number of edges. 
 
 
 ( 5 ):
This is  Theorem   3.6 . 
 
 
 ( 6 ):
Combine  Theorems   3.4  and  3.8  where  T × Q n T\times Q_{n}  has an even number of edges if  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2  or if  T T  has an even number of nodes. 
 
 
 ( 7 ):
We use the same decomposition that Savage, Squire and West applied to the wheel graph  W n W_{n}  in the proof of  [ 37 , Theorem 3.1] .
Let  e e  be and edge of  W n W_{n}  which does not contain the central node, and let  G n = W n ∖ e G_{n}=W_{n}\smallsetminus e  and  TG n = G n × K 2 \mathrm{TG}_{n}=G_{n}\times K_{2} .
Note that  W n / e = W n − 1 W_{n}/_{e}=W_{n-1} , hence by deletion-contraction and  Proposition   3.5 , we have:
 ψ ⁡ ( TW n ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( TW n − 1 ) + ψ ⁡ ( TG n ) mod 4 \psi(\mathrm{TW}_{n})\equiv\psi(\mathrm{TW}_{n-1})+\psi(\mathrm{TG}_{n})\mod 4 .
Moreover, let  f f  be and edge of  G n G_{n}  not containing the central node  x x , then  G n / f = G n − 1 G_{n}/_{f}=G_{n-1}  and  G n ∖ f G_{n}\smallsetminus f  is a  1 1 -sum of  G n − 1 G_{n-1}  with a single edge.
In particular, by  Lemma   3.3 ,  ψ ⁡ ( ( G n ∖ f ) × K 2 ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( TG n − 1 ) mod 4 \psi\bigl((G_{n}\smallsetminus f)\times K_{2}\bigr)\equiv\psi(\mathrm{TG}_{n-1})\mod 4  since  ( G n ∖ f ) × K 2 (G_{n}\smallsetminus f)\times K_{2}  is obtained from  TG n − 1 \mathrm{TG}_{n-1}  by gluing a path of length  3 3  on the edge  { x } × K 2 \{x\}\times K_{2} .
Thus, by  Lemmas   3.1  and  3.5 , we get:
 ψ ⁡ ( TG n ) ≡ 2 ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( TG n − 1 ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(\mathrm{TG}_{n})\equiv 2\cdot\psi(\mathrm{TG}_{n-1})\equiv 0\mod 4 .
Consequently,  ψ ⁡ ( TW n ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( TW n − 1 ) mod 4 \psi(\mathrm{TW}_{n})\equiv\psi(\mathrm{TW}_{n-1})\mod 4 , and by induction, since  ψ ⁡ ( TW 1 ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( C 4 ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(\mathrm{TW}_{1})\equiv\psi(C_{4})\equiv 2\mod 4 , we obtain  ψ ⁡ ( TW n ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(\mathrm{TW}_{n})\equiv 2\mod 4 .
As the wheel graph  W n W_{n}  has an even number of edges and  n + 1 n+1  nodes, the thick wheel graph has an even number of edges when  n n  is odd, so we conclude by  Lemma   3.2 . 
 
 
 ( 8 ):
Fix a  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycle  G G  with  n i n_{i}  even for some  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] .
Note that the number of edges of  G G , namely  1 + ∑ i = 1 s ( n i − 1 ) 1+\sum_{i=1}^{s}(n_{i}-1) , is even by hypothesis.
Let  i 1 , … , i s i_{1},\dots,i_{s}  be an enumeration of  [ s ] [s] , with  n i 1 n_{i_{1}}  even, such that  T j = T ⁡ [ { i 1 , … , i j } ] T_{j}=T[\{i_{1},\dots,i_{j}\}]  is a tree.
In particular,  i j i_{j}  is a leaf in  T j T_{j} .
Let also  G j G_{j}  be the  ( T j , n i 1 , … , n i j ) (T_{j},n_{i_{1}},\dots,n_{i_{j}}) -subtree of cycle of  G G .
As  G j G_{j}  is obtained from  G j − 1 G_{j-1}  by gluing a path of length  n j − 1 n_{j}-1  on an edge,  ψ ⁡ ( G j ) \psi(G_{j})  is divisible by  4 4  if and only if  ψ ⁡ ( G j − 1 ) mod 4 \psi(G_{j-1})\mod 4  is divisible by  4 4 , by  Lemma   3.3 .
Since  G 1 G_{1}  is an even cycle with  n i 1 n_{i_{1}}  nodes,  ψ ⁡ ( G 1 ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(G_{1})\equiv 2\mod 4  by  Example   3.16 , so  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  is not divisible by  4 4 .
By  Lemma   3.2 ,  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.11 . 
 
 We have only presented a few possible applications of  Lemmas   3.3  and  3.5  together with the usual deletion-contraction formula.
The reader is invited to try to compute  ψ ⁡ ( G × K 2 ) mod 4 \psi(G\times K_{2})\mod 4  for their favorite (family of) graphs  G G , and conclude on the  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity of  G × K 2 G\times K_{2}  if the number of nodes of  G G  is odd, using  Lemma   3.2 . 
 
 
 Note that any result proven for  G × K 2 G\times K_{2}  immediately extends to  G × Q n G\times Q_{n}  for  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1  thanks to  Corollary   3.8 . 
 
 
 
 We give one last structural result relying on the same ideas.
This can be used to start tackling non- 2 2 -connected graphs. 
 
 
 Proposition 3.12 . 
 
 If  G G  is disconnected, then  ψ ⁡ ( G × Q n ) ≡ ψ ⁡ ( G ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(G\times Q_{n})\equiv\psi(G)\equiv 0\mod 4  for all  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 . 
 
 
 Moreover, for any graph  G G  and any tree  T T , we have for all  n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 : 
 

 
 
 ψ ( ( G ⊕ 1 T ) × Q n ) ≡ ψ ( ( G ⊕ 1 T ) × K 2 ) ≡ ψ ( G × K 2 ) mod 4 . \psi\bigl((G\oplus_{1}T)\times Q_{n}\bigr)\equiv\psi\bigl((G\oplus_{1}T)\times K_{2}\bigr)\equiv\psi(G\times K_{2})\mod 4\,. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The first statement comes from  ψ ⁡ ( H ⊔ H ′ ) = ψ ⁡ ( H ) ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( H ′ ) \psi(H\sqcup H^{\prime})=\psi(H)\cdot\psi(H^{\prime})  for node-disjoint graphs  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime} , and  2 2  divides both  ψ ⁡ ( H ) \psi(H)  and  ψ ⁡ ( H ′ ) \psi(H^{\prime})  by  Lemma   3.1  (note that  ( H ⊔ H ′ ) × Q n (H\sqcup H^{\prime})\times Q_{n}  is also disconnected). 
 
 
 The first equivalence of the second statement is given by  Corollary   3.8 .
For the second one, let  u u  be a leaf of  T T  which is not the one shared by  G G  in  G ⊕ 1 T G\oplus_{1}T , let  v v  be the unique neighbor of  u u  in  T T , and let  T ′ = T ∖ u T^{\prime}=T\smallsetminus u .
Then  ( G ⊕ 1 T ) × K 2 (G\oplus_{1}T)\times K_{2}  is obtained from  ( G ⊕ 1 T ′ ) × K 2 (G\oplus_{1}T^{\prime})\times K_{2}  by gluing a path of length  3 3  on the edge  { v } × K 2 \{v\}\times K_{2} .
By  Lemma   3.3 , we have that  ψ ( ( G ⊕ 1 T ) × K 2 ) ≡ 7 ⋅ ψ ( ( G ⊕ 1 T ′ ) × K 2 ) ≡ ψ ( ( G ⊕ 1 T ′ ) × K 2 ) mod 4 \psi\bigl((G\oplus_{1}T)\times K_{2}\bigr)\equiv 7\cdot\psi\bigl((G\oplus_{1}T^{\prime})\times K_{2}\bigr)\equiv\psi\bigl((G\oplus_{1}T^{\prime})\times K_{2}\bigr)\mod 4 .
Trimming  T T  of its leaves recursively, we get the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We conclude by discussing further possible research angles that use the same core idea of counting the number of acyclic orientations of graphs and checking its divisibility by  4 4 . 
 
 
 Example 3.13 . 
 
 Our computer experiments show that the following graphs  G G  satisfy  ψ ⁡ ( G ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(G)\equiv 2\mod 4 , and hence are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian since they have an even number of edges: 
 
 (1)  
 
 The octahedral graph; 
 
 (2)  
 
 The Franklin graph; 
 
 (3)  
 
 The Herschel graph; 
 
 (4)  
 
 The Möbius–Kantor graph; 
 
 (5)  
 
 The Desargues graph; 
 
 (6)  
 
 The triangular grid graphs  Tr n , m \mathrm{Tr}_{n,m}  for  ( n , m ) ∈ { ( 3 , 3 ) , ( 3 , 5 ) } (n,m)\in\{(3,3),(3,5)\} ; 
 
 (7)  
 
 The graph of the antiprisms  A n A_{n}  for  n ∈ { 3 , 6 } n\in\{3,6\}  but not for  n ∈ { 4 , 5 , 7 , 8 } n\in\{4,5,7,8\} ; 
 
 (8)  
 
 The grid graphs  Gr n , m = P n × P m \mathrm{Gr}_{n,m}=P_{n}\times P_{m}  for  ( n , m ) ∈ { ( 3 , 3 ) , ( 3 , 5 ) , ( 3 , 7 ) } (n,m)\in\{(3,3),(3,5),(3,7)\}  but not for  n = m = 4 n=m=4 ; 
 
 (9)  
 
 The complete tripartite graphs  K m , n , p K_{m,n,p}  for  n = m = 2 n=m=2  and  2 ≤ p ≤ 7 2\leq p\leq 7  and for  ( n , m , p ) = ( 2 , 3 , 4 ) (n,m,p)=(2,3,4) 
 
 (10)  
 
 The generalized Petersen graphs  Pet ⁡ ( n , k ) \mathrm{Pet}(n,k)  for  ( n , k ) ∈ { ( 6 , 1 ) , ( 8 , 1 ) , ( 8 , 3 ) , ( 10 , 1 ) , ( 10 , 3 ) } (n,k)\in\{(6,1),(8,1),(8,3),(10,1),(10,3)\}  but not for  ( n , k ) ∈ { ( 6 , 2 ) , ( 8 , 2 ) , ( 10 , 2 ) , ( 10 , 4 ) } (n,k)\in\{(6,2),(8,2),(10,2),(10,4)\} . 
 
 
 
 
 The reader must be careful: each “but not” means that  ψ ⁡ ( G ) ≡ 0 mod 4 \psi(G)\equiv 0\mod 4 , it does not imply that  G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Note also that the number of acyclic orientations of Chvátal graphs is divisible by  4 4 , yet it is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, see  Table   5 : we will need the notion of acyclic signature studied in  Section   3.3  to understand this case. 
 
 
 
 In view of the previous example list, we conjecture the following.
