 Conjecture 1 . 
 
 For the following graphs  G G , we have  ψ ⁡ ( G ) ≡ 2 mod 4 \psi(G)\equiv 2\mod 4 , and consequently  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian since  G G  has an even number of edges: 
 
 (1)  
 
 The grid graphs  Gr n , m = P n × P m \mathrm{Gr}_{n,m}=P_{n}\times P_{m}  for  m , n ≥ 3 m,n\geq 3  both odd; 
 
 (2)  
 
 The triangular grid graphs  Tr n , m \mathrm{Tr}_{n,m}  for  m , n ≥ 3 m,n\geq 3  both odd; 
 
 (3)  
 
 The generalized Petersen graphs  Pet ⁡ ( n , k ) \mathrm{Pet}(n,k)  for  n n  even and  k k  odd; 
 
 (4)  
 
 The complete tripartite graphs  K m , n , p K_{m,n,p}  for  n n ,  m m ,  p p  not all odd. 
 
 
 
 
 
 We believe that methods similar to the one studied above for path gluing and Cartesian products may be developed in these cases.
Note that the ladder graphs ( i.e.  grid graphs  Gr 2 , m \mathrm{Gr}_{2,m} ) and the complete bipartite graphs were already dealt with by Savage, Squire and West  [ 37 ] . 
 
 
 
 3.2.  The acyclic polynomial in general 

 
 For graphs, using a deletion-contraction recursion proven by Stanley  [ 40 ] , one can compute the number  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  of acyclic orientations of the graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) :
for all  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E , we have  ψ ( G ) = ψ ( G / u ​ v ) + ψ ( G ∖ u v ) \psi(G)=\psi(G/_{uv})+\psi(G\smallsetminus uv) .
This yields a counting algorithm with time complexity  O ⁡ ( φ | N | + | E | ) O\bigl(\varphi^{|N|+|E|}\bigr)  where  φ ≔ 1 + 5 2 \varphi\coloneqq\frac{1+\sqrt{5}}{2} , see  [ 46 , end of Section 2.3] .
Our ultimate goal is to present a similar, although incomplete, algorithm for computing the acyclic signature  σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)  of an orientation of  G G .
This will be done in  Section   3.3 . It is natural to count acyclic orientations according to their rank (with respect to a fixed base orientation). 
 
 
 3.2.1.  Definition and first examples 

 
 For a digraph  D D , the  acyclic polynomial  is defined as 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) ≔ ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) t rk D ⁡ A . {\Psi(D;t)}\coloneqq\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D)}t^{\rank_{D}A}\,. 
 
 
 By convention,  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = 1 \Psi(D;t)=1  if  D D  has no edge.
Clearly, for any orientation  D D  of  G G , one has  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) \psi(G)=\Psi(D;1) , and  σ ⁡ ( D ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) \sigma(D)=\Psi(D;-1) . 
 
 
 We give  22 22  examples of acyclic polynomials for small, well-known graphs in Appendix  D . 
 
 
 Remark 3.14 . 
 
 The coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  are the Whitney numbers of the second kind of the poset of regions of the graphic arrangement with base region given by  D D .
See  [ 42 , Section 3.10] , for the general definition and background.
We will not make use of this perspective, since our goal is to present graph-theoretic proofs of every claim. 
 
 
 
 Example 3.15   (Trees) . 
 
 For a tree  T T  with  m m  edges, recall that all orientations are acyclic.
Hence, for any orientation  D D  of  T T , we get  | 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = | 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = ( m k ) |{\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D)|=|{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)|=\binom{m}{k} .
Thus we get 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∑ k ( m k ) ​ t k = ( 1 + t ) m . \Psi(D;t)=\sum_{k}\binom{m}{k}t^{k}=(1+t)^{m}\,. 
 
 
 We recover that the number of acyclic orientations of  T T  is  ψ ⁡ ( T ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) = 2 m \psi(T)=\Psi(D;1)=2^{m}  while its signature is  σ ⁡ ( D ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) = 0 \sigma(D)=\Psi(D;-1)=0 . 
 
 
 
 Example 3.16   (Cycles) . 
 
 For a cycle  C n = ( [ n ] , E ) C_{n}=([n],E)  all orientations except 2 are acyclic.
Let  D D  be one of the cyclic orientations of  C n C_{n} , hence  D E D^{E}  is the other cyclic orientation of  C n C_{n} . 
 
 
 Thus  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ( 1 + t ) n − 1 − t n . \Psi(D;t)=(1+t)^{n}-1-t^{n}\,. 
We recover that the number of acyclic orientations of  C n C_{n}  is  ψ ⁡ ( C n ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) = 2 n − 2 \psi(C_{n})=\Psi(D;1)=2^{n}-2  while its signature is  σ ⁡ ( D ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) = { 0  if  ​ n ​  is odd − 2  if  ​ n ​  is even \sigma(D)=\Psi(D;-1)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{ if }n\text{ is odd}\\
-2&\text{ if }n\text{ is even}\end{array}\right. . 
 
 
 Changing the base orientation from a cyclic orientation  D D  to some acyclic orientation  A = D X A=D^{X}  with  | X | = q |X|=q , we easily get
 Ψ ⁡ ( A , t ) = ( 1 + t ) n − t q − t n − q , \Psi(A;t)=(1+t)^{n}-t^{q}-t^{n-q}\,,  yielding  σ ⁡ ( A ) = { 0  if  ​ n ​  is odd ( − 1 ) q + 1 ⋅ 2  if  ​ n ​  is even \sigma(A)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{ if }n\text{ is odd}\\
(-1)^{q+1}\cdot 2&\text{ if }n\text{ is even}\end{array}\right. . 
 
 
 
 Example 3.17 . 
 
 As our running example, the complete bipartite graph  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  has  46 46  acyclic orientations, and its acyclic signature is  ± 6 \pm 6  depending on the chosen orientation.
We depict all the possible acyclic polynomials in  Figure   3 .
Out of the  2 6 = 64 2^{6}=64  orientations of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3} , we only get  8 8  different polynomials:
as we will see in  Corollary   3.46 , reversing the directions of all arcs yield the same acyclic polynomial;
and moreover, it is clear that if two base orientations  D D  and  D ′ D^{\prime}  differ by an automorphism of the underlying graph  G G , then their associated acyclic polynomials are identical. 
 
 
 
 Figure 3 .  The  8 8  possible orientations of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  up to symmetry and reversion of the directions of all arcs, with the corresponding acyclic polynomials and their decomposition.
Each polynomial is given as a list of coefficients,  e.g.  “ 1 , 2 , 7 , 4 , 5 , 0 , 0 1,2,7,4,5,0,0 ” means  1 + 2 ​ t + 7 ​ t 2 + 4 ​ t 3 + 5 ​ t 4 + 0 ​ t 5 + 0 ​ t 6 1+2t+7t^{2}+4t^{3}+5t^{4}+0t^{5}+0t^{6} .
Here, we write  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  as a bipartition between on the one side the graph with nodes  u , v u,v  and no edge, and on the other side the remaining edges ( i.e.   K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  itself).
Following the decomposition of  Propositions   3.43  and  3.44 , note that  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  and  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)  are palindromic, contrary to  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t) .
Furthermore,  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is equal to the sum of  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t) , and  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t) , and the reverse of  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t) .
Besides, the constant coefficient (and the coefficient on  t 6 t^{6} ) of  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is non-zero if and only if  D D  is acyclic.
The number of acyclic orientations  ψ ⁡ ( K 2 , 3 ) = 46 \psi(K_{2,3})=46  is the sum of the coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t) , while the acyclic signature  σ ⁡ ( K 2 , 3 ) = 6 \sigma(K_{2,3})=6  is their alternating sum (up to sign). 
 
 
 Remark 3.18 . 
 
 Since we will show that the acyclic polynomial admits a deletion-contraction phenomenon (see  Section   3.2.6 ), one might wonder if  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is a specialization of the famous Tutte–Whitney polynomial of  G G  for some orientation  D D  of the graph  G G .
We conjecture that this is not possible, and we now argue against it. 
 
 
 Let  G = K 2 , 3 G=K_{2,3} , and  D D  the orientation  1 1  of  G G  of  Figure   3 .
The Tutte–Whitney polynomial of  G G  is
 T K 2 , 3 ​ ( x , y ) = x 4 + 2 ​ x 3 + 3 ​ x 2 + 3 ​ x ​ y + y 2 + x + y . T_{K_{2,3}}(x,y)=x^{4}+2x^{3}+3x^{2}+3xy+y^{2}+x+y\,. 
 
 
 On the other hand,  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = 1 + 6 ​ t + 9 ​ t 2 + 14 ​ t 3 + 9 ​ t 4 + 6 ​ t 5 + 1 \Psi(D;t)=1+6t+9t^{2}+14t^{3}+9t^{4}+6t^{5}+1  is of degree  6 6  (since  D D  has been chosen acyclic), as indicated in  Figure   3 .
There are many ways to interpret “being a specialization of  T K 2 , 3 T_{K_{2,3}} ”; we are going to prove that it is impossible that there exist polynomials  A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) A(t),B(t)  with real coefficients, such that  T K 2 , 3 ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) T_{K_{2,3}}\bigl(A(t),B(t)\bigr)=\Psi(D;t) . 
 
 
 Indeed, suppose it is the case, and let  a a  (resp.  b b ) be the degree of  A ⁡ ( t ) A(t)  (resp.  B ⁡ ( t ) B(t) ).
The degree of  T K 2 , 3 ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) T_{K_{2,3}}\bigl(A(t),B(t)\bigr)  depends on which of  4 ​ a 4a  and  2 ​ b 2b  is the greatest (since  a + b < max ⁡ ( 4 ​ a , 2 ​ b ) a+b<\max(4a,2b) ).
In particular, we need to have  max ⁡ ( 4 ​ a , 2 ​ b ) ≥ 6 \max(4a,2b)\geq 6 . 
 
 •  
 
 If  4 ​ a > 2 ​ b 4a>2b , then the degree of  T K 2 , 3 ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) T_{K_{2,3}}\bigl(A(t),B(t)\bigr)  is  4 ​ a 4a  which needs to be equal to  6 6 , but cannot. 
 
 •  
 
 If  4 ​ a < 2 ​ b 4a<2b , then the degree of  T K 2 , 3 ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) T_{K_{2,3}}\bigl(A(t),B(t)\bigr)  is  2 ​ b 2b  which needs to be equal to  6 6 , so  b = 3 b=3  and  a ≤ 1 a\leq 1 .
Hence, writing  A ⁡ ( t ) = α 0 + α 1 ​ t A(t)=\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}t  and  B ⁡ ( t ) = β 0 + β 1 ​ t + β 2 ​ t 2 + β 3 ​ t 3 B(t)=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}t+\beta_{2}t^{2}+\beta_{3}t^{3} , one can develop  T K 2 , 3 ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) T_{K_{2,3}}\bigl(A(t),B(t)\bigr)=\Psi(D;t)  to get  7 7  equations involving the  6 6  variables  α 0 , α 1 , β 0 , β 1 , β 2 , β 3 \alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3} .
A tedious computation (or a solver of polynomial systems) shows that this explicit system has no solutions. 
 
 •  
 
 If  4 ​ a > 6 4a>6  and  2 ​ b > 6 2b>6 , then  4 ​ a = 2 ​ b 4a=2b  and the leading coefficients of  A ​ ( t ) 4 A(t)^{4}  and  B ​ ( t ) 2 B(t)^{2}  compensate each other (otherwise the degree of  T K 2 , 3 ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) T_{K_{2,3}}\bigl(A(t),B(t)\bigr)  would not be  6 6 ).
Yet, since  4 4  and  2 2  are even powers, this is not possible. 
 
 
 
 
 Consequently, there exists a graph  G G  ( e.g.   G = K 2 , 3 G=K_{2,3} ) and an orientation  D D  of  G G  (actually several) such that for all polynomials  A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ∈ ℝ ⁡ [ t ] A(t),B(t)\in\mathbb{R}[t] , we have  T G ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) ≠ Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) T_{G}(A(t),B(t))\neq\Psi(D;t) .
However, this does not rule out the possibility that  T G ​ ( A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) T_{G}(A(t),B(t))=\Psi(D;t)  for some orientation  D D  of some graph  G G  and for some polynomials  A ⁡ ( t ) , B ⁡ ( t ) ∈ ℂ ⁡ [ t ] A(t),B(t)\in\mathbb{C}[t] .
We still believe this cannot happen except in trivial cases, but cannot (un)prove it. 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.19 . 
 
 It is well-known ( e.g.  see  [ 40 ] ) that  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = χ ⁡ ( G , − 1 ) \psi(G)=\chi(G;-1)  where  χ ⁡ ( G , x ) \chi(G;x)  is the chromatic polynomial of  G G ,  i.e.   χ ⁡ ( G , c ) \chi(G;c)  is the number of ways one can color  G G  using  c c  colors, such that no edge has both endpoints of the same color.
The chromatic and acyclic polynomials shall not be confused: in general  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) ≠ χ ⁡ ( D ¯ , − t ) \Psi(D;t)\neq\chi(\underline{D};-t)  for a digraph  D D .
For example, one can compare the results of  Figure   3  with:  χ ⁡ ( K 2 , 3 , x ) = 7 ​ x − 17 ​ x 2 + 15 ​ x 3 − 6 ​ x 4 + x 5 \chi(K_{2,3};x)=7x-17x^{2}+15x^{3}-6x^{4}+x^{5} . 
 
 
 We do not know if there exists a polynomial  Φ ⁡ ( D , t , x ) \Phi(D;t,x) , satisfying a deletion-contraction recursion, from which both  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  and  χ ⁡ ( D ¯ , x ) \chi(\underline{D};x)  can be naturally recovered ( e.g.  such that  Φ ⁡ ( D , t , 1 ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Phi(D;t,1)=\Psi(D;t)  and  Φ ⁡ ( D , 1 , x ) = χ ⁡ ( D ¯ , x ) \Phi(D;1,x)=\chi(\underline{D};x)  for all  x , t x,t ).
We saw in  Remark   3.18  that if such a polynomial were to exist, it could not be a specification of the Tutte–Whitney polynomial. 
 
 
 
 Proposition 3.20 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a graph and  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{O}}(G)  the set of all its orientations, then 
 

 
 
 ∑ D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ( 1 + t ) | E | ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( G ) . \sum_{D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)}\Psi(D;t)=(1+t)^{|E|}\cdot\psi(G). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Note that we have  rk D ⁡ A = rk A ⁡ D \rank_{D}A=\rank_{A}D , by definition (the number of arcs on which  A A  differs from  D D  is equal to the number of arcs on which  D D  differs from  A A ).
We have: 
 

 
 
 ∑ D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∑ D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) t rk D ⁡ A = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ∑ D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) t rk A ⁡ D = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ( 1 + t ) | E | = ( 1 + t ) | E | ⋅ ψ ⁡ ( G ) \sum_{D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)}\Psi(D;t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)\\
A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D}A}=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}\sum_{D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)}t^{\rank_{A}D}=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}(1+t)^{|E|}=(1+t)^{|E|}\cdot\psi(G) 
 
 
 where for any  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)  we have:  ∑ D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) t rk A ⁡ D = ∑ X ⊆ E t rk A ⁡ A X = ∑ X ⊆ E t | X | = ( 1 + t ) | E | \sum_{D\in{\mathcal{O}}(G)}t^{\rank_{A}D}=\sum_{X\subseteq E}t^{\rank_{A}A^{X}}=\sum_{X\subseteq E}t^{|X|}=(1+t)^{|E|} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example 3.21 . 
 
 For the complete bipartite graph  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3} ,  Proposition   3.20  gives  ∑ D ∈ 𝒪 ⁡ ( K 2 , 3 ) Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = 46 ⋅ ( 1 + t ) 6 \sum_{D\in{\mathcal{O}}(K_{2,3})}\Psi(D;t)=46\cdot(1+t)^{6} .
This can be checked by summing the polynomials of  Figure   3 , taking into account the reversion of all the arcs of each orientation and their multiplicities. 
 
 
 
 
 3.2.2.  Disconnected graphs and 1-sums of graphs 

 
 As an easy property of acyclic polynomials, we show that the acyclic polynomial of a disconnected graph is the product of the acyclic polynomials of its connected components, while the acyclic polynomial of a  1 1 -sum of graphs is the product of the acyclic polynomials of the summands. 
 
 
 Proposition 3.22 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a disconnected graph with connected components  N 1 , … , N r N_{1},\dots,N_{r} , and  D D  an orientation of  G G , then: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∏ k = 1 r Ψ ⁡ ( D ⁡ [ N k ] , t ) . \Psi(D;t)=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\Psi(D[N_{k}];t)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It is trivial that the map  A ↦ ( A ⁡ [ N 1 ] , … , A ⁡ [ N r ] ) A\mapsto(A[N_{1}],\dots,A[N_{r}])  is a bijection between  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ⁡ [ N 1 ] ) × ⋯ × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ⁡ [ N r ] ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G[N_{1}])\times\dots\times{\mathcal{AO}}(G[N_{r}]) .
Moreover, given a fixed acyclic orientation  D D  of  G G , we have  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ [ N 1 ] ⁡ A ⁡ [ N 1 ] + ⋯ + rk D ⁡ [ N r ] ⁡ A ⁡ [ N r ] \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D[N_{1}]}A[N_{1}]+\dots+\rank_{D[N_{r}]}A[N_{r}] .
Thus: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) t rk D ⁡ A = ∑ A 1 , … , A r ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ⁡ [ N 1 ] ) × ⋯ × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ⁡ [ N r ] ) ∏ k = 1 r t rk D ⁡ [ N k ] ⁡ A ⁡ [ N k ] = ∏ k = 1 r Ψ ⁡ ( D ⁡ [ N k ] , t ) , \Psi(D;t)=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}t^{\rank_{D}A}=\sum_{A_{1},\dots,A_{r}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G[N_{1}])\times\dots\times{\mathcal{AO}}(G[N_{r}])}\prod_{k=1}^{r}t^{\rank_{D[N_{k}]}A[N_{k}]}=\prod_{k=1}^{r}\Psi(D[N_{k}];t)\,, 
 
 
 where the last equality comes from exchanging the sum with the product and summing over each  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ⁡ [ N k ] ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G[N_{k}])  independently.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proposition 3.23 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊕ 1 H ′ G=H\oplus_{1}H^{\prime}  be a  1 1 -sum of graphs.
For any orientation  D D  of  G G , one naturally has  D = D [ H ] ⊕ 1 D [ H ′ ] D=D[H]\oplus_{1}D[H^{\prime}] , and obtains
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D ⁡ [ H ] , t ) ⋅ Ψ ⁡ ( D ⁡ [ H ′ ] , t ) . \Psi(D;t)=\Psi(D[H];t)\cdot\Psi(D[H^{\prime}];t)\,. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The map  A ↦ ( A ⁡ [ H ] , A ⁡ [ H ′ ] ) A\mapsto(A[H],A[H^{\prime}])  is a bijection between  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  since it is impossible to create a (directed) cycle in  G G  using arcs of both  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime} .
Moreover,  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ [ H ] ⁡ A ⁡ [ H ] + rk D ⁡ [ H ′ ] ⁡ A ⁡ [ H ′ ] \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D[H]}A[H]+\rank_{D[H^{\prime}]}A[H^{\prime}] .
The claim follows.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Consequently, it is enough to compute acyclic polynomials of  2 2 -connected graphs in order to recover the acyclic polynomials of all graphs. 
 
 
 
 3.2.3.  Simplicial nodes, chordal graphs, complete graphs 

 
 A graph  G G  is  chordal  if all its induced cycles have length  3 3 .
On the other hand, a  perfect elimination ordering  of a graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  with  | N | = n |N|=n  is a bijection  θ : [ n ] → N \theta:[n]\to N  such that for all  i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n]  the neighbors of  θ ⁡ ( i ) \theta(i)  in  { θ ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , θ ⁡ ( i − 1 ) } \{\theta(1),\dots,\theta(i-1)\}  form a clique in  G G  ( i.e.  are pairwise connected by an edge in  G G ). We say that  θ ⁡ ( i ) \theta(i)  is  simplicial  in  G ⁡ [ { θ ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , θ ⁡ ( i − 1 ) } ] G[\{\theta(1),\dots,\theta(i-1)\}] .
It is well-known, see  [ 35 ]  that  G G  is chordal if and only if  G G  admits a perfect elimination ordering. 
 
 
 Remark 3.24 . 
 
 For a graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  with an orientation  D D , the coefficients of the acyclic polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  are the Whitney numbers of the second kind of the graphic hyperplane arrangement  ℋ G ≔ ( { 𝒙 ∈ ℝ N ; x i = x j } , i ​ j ∈ E ) {\mathcal{H}}_{G}\coloneqq\bigl(\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}~;~x_{i}=x_{j}\}~;~ij\in E\bigr)  with respect to the base chamber corresponding to  D D ,  i.e.   𝖢 D ≔ { x i ≤ x j ; i ​ j ∈ D } \mathsf{C}_{D}\coloneqq\{x_{i}\leq x_{j}~;~ij\in D\} .
In particular, the graphic hyperplane arrangement of chordal graphs are known to be supersolvable.
In this context, the formula of  Proposition   3.26  is already known, see  [ 5 , Thm 4.4 & Cor. 4.5] . 
 
 
 Moreover, Edelman and Reiner  [ 16 ]  proved that the sequence of exponents of the graphic arrangement associated to a chordal graph is precisely the elimination-degree sequence defined thereafter.
As the sequence of exponents is an invariant of the hyperplane arrangement, the independence from a specific base chamber ( i.e.  from a specific perfect elimination ordering) follows automatically, see  [ 16 , Lemma 3.4] . 
 
 
 Accordingly, we do not pretend that  Propositions   3.26 ,  3.28  and  3.29  are new, but we give graph-theoretical proofs below for the sake of self-containment, and to showcase how to compute acyclic polynomials.
Besides,  Lemmas   3.25 ,  3.31  and  3.32  seem not to appear in the existing literature, although we believe that other authors may have already thought about it.
Finally,  Theorem   3.36  is new, as far as we are aware. 
 
 
 
 In order to prove  Proposition   3.26 , we first state the following more general lemma.
A node is called  simplicial  if its neighbors form a clique. 
 
 
 Lemma 3.25 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a graph with a simplicial node  v ∈ N v\in N  with  b b  neighbors.
Let  D D  be an orientation of  G G  where all edges  u ​ v uv  are directed from  u u  to  v v , and let  D ′ = D ∖ { v } D^{\prime}=D\smallsetminus\{v\} .
Then: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) ⋅ ( 1 + t + ⋯ + t b ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) ⋅ 1 − t b + 1 1 − t . \Psi(D;t)=\Psi(D^{\prime};t)\cdot(1+t+\dots+t^{b})=\Psi(D^{\prime};t)\cdot\frac{1-t^{b+1}}{1-t}\,. 
 
 
 
 
 In particular  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) = ψ ⁡ ( G ∖ { v } ) ⋅ ( b + 1 ) \psi(G)=\Psi(D;1)=\psi(G\smallsetminus\{v\})\cdot(b+1) , and if  b b  is odd then  σ ⁡ ( D ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) = 0 \sigma(D)=\Psi(D;-1)=0 , if  b b  is even, then  σ ⁡ ( D ) = σ ⁡ ( D ′ ) \sigma(D)=\sigma(D^{\prime}) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The rightmost equality is immediate.
The values of  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G)  and  σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)  come from direct evaluations. 
 
 
 To ease notation, let  G ′ ≔ G ∖ { v } G^{\prime}\coloneqq G\smallsetminus\{v\}  and  U ⊆ N U\subseteq N  be the set of neighbors of  v v  in  G G , and recall that  b = | U | b=|U| .
For an acyclic orientation  A A  of  G G , let  A ′ = A ∖ { v } A^{\prime}=A\smallsetminus\{v\}  and let  j j  be the number of neighbors  u u  of  v v  with  u → v u\to v  in  A A .
The acyclic orientation  A A  is fully determined by  ( A ′ , j ) (A^{\prime},j)  because since  A ′ A^{\prime}  defines a total order on  U U  (as  U U  induces a clique in  G G ) the acyclicity of  A A  implies that if  u → v ∈ A u\to v\in A  for some  u ∈ U u\in U , then  u ′ → v ∈ A u^{\prime}\to v\in A  for all  u ′ ∈ U u^{\prime}\in U  with  u ′ → u ∈ A ′ u^{\prime}\to u\in A^{\prime} .
Hence the sole number of arcs pointing towards  v v  fully determines these arcs.
Reciprocally, for all acyclic orientations  A ′ A^{\prime}  of  G ′ G^{\prime}  and all  j ∈ { 0 , … , b } j\in\{0,\dots,b\} , one can construct an acyclic orientation  A A  of  G G  by  A ∖ { v } = A ′ A\smallsetminus\{v\}=A^{\prime}  and orienting  u → v u\to v  for  u ∈ U u\in U  among the  j j  first nodes of  U U  according to the total order of  U U  imposed by  A ′ A^{\prime} , and  v → u v\to u  for all other  u ∈ U u\in U .
Consequently,  A ↦ ( A ′ , j ) A\mapsto(A^{\prime},j)  is a bijection between acyclic orientations of  G G  and pairs of acyclic orientations of  G ′ G^{\prime}  and  j ∈ { 0 , … , b n } j\in\{0,\dots,b_{n}\} .
Finally, we have  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ + ( b − j ) \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}+(b-j)  since  ( b − j ) (b-j)  of the edges  u ​ v uv  for  u ∈ U u\in U  are not oriented from  u u  to  v v  in  A A .
Thus: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) t rk D ⁡ A = ∑ j = 0 b ∑ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ′ ) t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ ⋅ t b − j = Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) ⋅ ( 1 + t + ⋯ + t b ) . ∎ \Psi(D;t)=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}t^{\rank_{D}A}=\sum_{j=0}^{b}\sum_{A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G^{\prime})}t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}}\cdot t^{b-j}=\Psi(D^{\prime};t)\cdot(1+t+\dots+t^{b})\,.~\qed 
 
 
 
 
 
 For a chordal graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  and a perfect elimination ordering  θ \theta , we denote  D θ {D_{\theta}}  the orientation of  G G  induced by  θ \theta , that is the orientation with arcs from  θ ⁡ ( i ) \theta(i)  to  θ ⁡ ( j ) \theta(j)  for all  i < j i<j  such that  θ ⁡ ( i ) ​ θ ​ ( j ) ∈ E \theta(i)\theta(j)\in E .
Besides, we define the  i th i^{\text{th}}  elimination degree  as the size of the clique of previous neighbors of  θ ⁡ ( i ) \theta(i) , that is  b i ≔ | { j ∈ [ i − 1 ] ; θ ⁡ ( i ) ​ θ ​ ( j ) ∈ E } | {b_{i}}\coloneqq\bigl|\{j\in[i-1]~;~\theta(i)\theta(j)\in E\}\bigr| , where by convention  b 1 = 0 b_{1}=0 .
We call the sequence  ( b 1 , … , b n ) \bigr(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\bigr)  the  elimination-degree sequence .
Note that if  G G  is connected, then  b 2 = 1 b_{2}=1 . 
 
 
 Proposition 3.26 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a connected chordal graph with a perfect elimination ordering  θ \theta , and let  D θ D_{\theta}  be its induced orientation, and  ( b 1 , … , b n ) \bigl(b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\bigr)  its elimination-degree sequence.
Then: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) = ∏ i = 1 n ( 1 + t + ⋯ + t b i ) = ∏ i = 1 n 1 − t b i + 1 1 − t . \Psi(D_{\theta};t)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(1+t+\dots+t^{b_{i}}\bigr)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1-t^{b_{i}+1}}{1-t}\,. 
 
 
 
 
 In particular,  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , 1 ) = ∏ i = 1 n ( b i + 1 ) \psi(G)=\Psi(D_{\theta};1)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(b_{i}+1)  and  σ ⁡ ( D θ ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , − 1 ) = 0 \sigma(D_{\theta})=\Psi(D_{\theta};-1)=0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Inductive application of  Lemma   3.25  on the sequence of induced subgraphs on  { θ ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , θ ⁡ ( i ) } \{\theta(1),\dots,\theta(i)\}  in which  θ ⁡ ( i ) \theta(i)  is a simplicial node with  b i b_{i}  neighbors.
For  σ ⁡ ( D θ ) \sigma(D_{\theta}) , note that  b 2 = 1 b_{2}=1  is odd.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example 3.27 . 
 
 In particular, for the complete graph  K n K_{n} , which is a chordal graph, the acyclic orientations are in bijection with the permutations of  [ n ] [n] .
Fixing  D D  to be any acyclic orientation of  K n K_{n} , the number of acyclic orientations of  K n K_{n}  of a given rank  k k  is the number of permutations on  n n  elements with  k k  inversions.
These are counted by the Mahonian numbers  T n , k T_{n,k}   [ 27 , A008302] .
In particular,  Proposition   3.26  helps us to retrieve the famous formula: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∑ k = 0 n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) / 2 T n , k ​ t k = ∏ j = 1 n 1 − t j 1 − t . \Psi(D;t)=\sum_{k=0}^{n(n-1)/2}T_{n,k}\,t^{k}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1-t^{j}}{1-t}\,. 
 
 
 We recover that the number of acyclic orientations of  K n K_{n}  is  ψ ⁡ ( K n ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) = n ! \psi(K_{n})=\Psi(D;1)=n! , while its signature is  σ D ​ ( K n ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) = 0 \sigma_{D}(K_{n})=\Psi(D;-1)=0  for  n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 . 
 
 
 
 One may wonder what happens when we change the chosen perfect elimination ordering of a given graph  G G .
Luckily, the acyclic polynomial remains unchanged. 
 
 
 Proposition 3.28 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a connected chordal graph.
Up to a permutation, the elimination-degree sequence does not depend on the perfect elimination ordering. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 For a given  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , consider the graph  𝒫 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{P}}(G)  whose nodes are the perfect elimination orderings of  G G  and where two perfect elimination orderings  θ \theta  and  θ ′ \theta^{\prime}  are linked by an edge if  θ ′ = θ ∘ ( j ​ j + 1 ) \theta^{\prime}=\theta\circ(j~\,~j\!+\!1)  where  ( j ​ j + 1 ) (j~\,~j\!+\!1)  denotes a transposition of  [ n ] [n]  ( i.e.   𝒫 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{P}}(G)  is the subgraph of the permutahedral graph induced on the perfect elimination orderings of  G G ). 
 
 
 According to  [ 10 ] ,  𝒫 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{P}}(G)  is connected:
the authors even prove that  𝒫 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{P}}(G)  is a Hamiltonian graph by expliciting a Gray code.
Hence, it is enough to prove that if  θ \theta  and  θ ′ \theta^{\prime}  are linked by an edge in  𝒫 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{P}}(G) , then their associated elimination degree sequences are the same up to permutation.
To this end, fix a perfect elimination ordering  θ \theta  of  G G  and  j ∈ [ n − 1 ] j\in[n-1]  such that  θ ′ = θ ∘ ( j ​ j + 1 ) \theta^{\prime}=\theta\circ(j~\,~j\!+\!1)  is also a perfect elimination ordering.
We denote  b i b_{i}  (resp.  b i ′ b^{\prime}_{i} ) the  i th i^{\text{th}}  elimination degree of  θ \theta  (resp. of  θ ′ \theta^{\prime} ).
If  θ ⁡ ( j ) ​ θ ​ ( j + 1 ) ∉ E \theta(j)\theta(j+1)\notin E , then  b i = b i ′ b_{i}=b^{\prime}_{i}  for all  i ∉ { j , j + 1 } i\notin\{j,j+1\} , and  b j ′ = b j + 1 b^{\prime}_{j}=b_{j+1}  and  b j + 1 ′ = b j b^{\prime}_{j+1}=b_{j}  since swapping the order of  θ ⁡ ( j ) \theta(j)  and  θ ⁡ ( j + 1 ) \theta(j+1)  does not change the clique of previous nodes of any node.
If  θ ⁡ ( j ) ​ θ ​ ( j + 1 ) ∈ E \theta(j)\theta(j+1)\in E , then  b i = b i ′ b_{i}=b^{\prime}_{i}  for all  i ∈ [ n ] i\in[n]  since swapping the order of  θ ⁡ ( j ) \theta(j)  and  θ ⁡ ( j + 1 ) \theta(j+1)  only changes the cliques of previous nodes of  θ ⁡ ( j ) \theta(j)  (whose size increases by  1 1 ) and of  θ ⁡ ( j + 1 ) \theta(j+1)  (whose size decreases by  1 1 ) and their sizes have a difference of  1 1 .
In both cases,  b ′ b^{\prime}  is a permutation of  b b , which proves the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 3.29 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a connected chordal graph with two perfect elimination orderings  θ \theta  and  θ ′ \theta^{\prime} .
Then  Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D θ ′ , t ) \Psi(D_{\theta};t)=\Psi(D_{\theta^{\prime}};t) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Direct application of  Propositions   3.26  and  3.28 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.30 . 
 
 For graphic hyperplane arrangements, it is known that the characteristic polynomial of the arrangement coincides with the chromatic polynomial of the graph, see  [ 28 , Section 2.4] .
Thus, we know that  χ ⁡ ( G , x ) = ∏ i = 1 n ( x − b i ) \chi(G,x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(x-b_{i})  according to the Factorization Theorem  [ 28 , Theorem 4.6.21] . In particular, this again shows that the chromatic polynomial and the acyclic polynomials (of perfect elimination orientations) are two different polynomials. 
 
 
 
 We can further explore the distribution of the coefficients of the polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) \Psi(D_{\theta};t)  for  θ \theta  a perfect elimination ordering of a chordal graph  G G . 
 
 
 A sequence of positive integers  𝒂 = ( a 1 , … , a s ) {\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{s})  is  log-concave  if for all  i ∈ [ s − 1 ] i\in[s-1]  we have  a i + 1 2 ≥ a i ⋅ a i + 2 a_{i+1}^{2}\geq a_{i}\cdot a_{i+2} .
Such a sequence is  unimodal  if there exists  m ∈ [ s ] m\in[s] , called the  mode  of  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}} , such that  ( a 1 , … , a m ) (a_{1},\dots,a_{m})  is increasing, while  ( a m , … , a s ) (a_{m},\dots,a_{s})  is decreasing.
A notorious property of log-concave sequences is that they are unimodal.
Furthermore, if the polynomial  ∑ i = 1 s a i ​ t i \sum_{i=1}^{s}a_{i}t^{i}  is  real-rooted  ( i.e.  does not have complex non-real roots), then  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}}  is log-concave, and hence unimodal.
We refer to Brändén’s handbook  [ 7 ]  for all the “well-known” facts on unimodality, log-concavity, real-rootedness, and, later,  𝜸 {\boldsymbol{\gamma}} -vectors. 
 
 
 For chordal graphs endowed with the orientation induced by a perfect elimination ordering, the coefficients of the acyclic polynomial form a log-concave sequence. 
 
 
 Theorem 3.31 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a graph with a simplicial node  v ∈ N v\in N .
Let  D D  be an orientation of  G G  where all edges  u ​ v uv  are directed from  u u  to  v v , and let  D ′ = D ∖ { v } D^{\prime}=D\smallsetminus\{v\} .
If the coefficients of the polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) \Psi(D^{\prime};t)  form a log-concave (hence unimodal) sequence, then so do the coefficients of the polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Recall that if the coefficients of both polynomials  p ⁡ ( t ) p(t)  and  q ⁡ ( t ) q(t)  form log-concave sequences, then the coefficients of the polynomial  p ⁡ ( t ) ⋅ q ⁡ ( t ) p(t)\cdot q(t)  also form a log-concave sequence.
Note that, for all  b b , the coefficients of  1 + t + ⋯ + t b 1+t+\dots+t^{b}  are all  1 1 , hence form a log-concave sequence.
Consequently, by  Lemma   3.25 , if the coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) \Psi(D^{\prime};t)  form a log-concave sequence, then the coefficients of the product  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) ⋅ ( 1 + t + ⋯ + t b ) \Psi(D^{\prime};t)\cdot(1+t+\dots+t^{b})  form a log-concave sequence, whatever the value of  b b .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 3.32 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a connected chordal graph with a perfect elimination ordering  θ \theta .
Then the coefficients of the polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) \Psi(D_{\theta};t)  form a log-concave (hence unimodal) sequence. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Inductive application of  Theorem   3.31  on the induced subgraphs on  { θ ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , θ ⁡ ( i ) } \{\theta(1),\dots,\theta(i)\} .
For  i = 2 i=2 , this induced subgraph is an edge whose acyclic polynomial  1 + t 1+t  has a log-concave sequence of coefficients.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.33 . 
 
 The Mahonian numbers  T n , k T_{n,k}  seen in  Example   3.27  are log-concave (in  k k ) according to  Corollary   3.32  (see  [ 19 ]  for more details), even though the polynomial  ∑ k = 0 n ⁡ ( n − 1 ) / 2 T n , k ​ t k = ∏ j = 1 n 1 − t j 1 − t \sum_{k=0}^{n(n-1)/2}T_{n,k}\,t^{k}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1-t^{j}}{1-t}  is obviously not real-rooted. 
 
 
 However, note that the coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  given by  6 6  of  Figure   3 , where  G G  is the complete bipartite graph  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3} , do not form a log-concave (not even a unimodal) sequence.
This motivates the following question. 
 
 
 
 Question 3.34 . 
 
 Classify the graphs  G G  and orientations  D D  such that  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is real-rooted, and the ones whose coefficients form a log-concave sequence, or a unimodal sequence. 
 
 
 
 Definition 3.35 . 
 
 If  𝒂 = ( a 0 , … , a d ) {\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{0},\dots,a_{d})  is a palindromic sequence of length  d d , then one can rewrite the associated polynomial in the so-called  γ \gamma -basis , that is: 
 

 
 
 ∑ k = 0 d a k ​ t k = ∑ j = 0 ⌊ d 2 ⌋ γ j ​ t j ​ ( 1 + t ) d − 2 ​ j , \sum_{k=0}^{d}a_{k}t^{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor}\gamma_{j}t^{j}(1+t)^{d-2j}\,, 
 
 
 where  𝜸 ≔ ( γ 0 , … , γ ⌊ d 2 ⌋ ) {{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}}\coloneqq(\gamma_{0},\dots,\gamma_{\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor})  is called the  γ \gamma -vector  associated to  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}} . 
 
 
 
 It is well-known that if  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}}  is  γ \gamma -positive , that is if  γ j ≥ 0 \gamma_{j}\geq 0  for all  j ∈ { 0 , … , ⌊ d 2 ⌋ } j\in\{0,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor\} , then  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}}  is unimodal.
Besides, if  ∑ k = 0 d a k ​ t k \sum_{k=0}^{d}a_{k}t^{k}  is real-rooted, then  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}}  is  γ \gamma -positive. 
 
 
 The coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) \Psi(D_{\theta};t)  are palindromic for a chordal graph  G G  with a perfect elimination ordering  θ \theta , as can be seen from  Proposition   3.26 , and we will prove that this actually holds for any directed graph in  Corollary   3.44 .
Hence, one can wonder whether the coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  form a  γ \gamma -positive sequence for some digraph  D D .
We give a negative but surprising answer in the case of chordal graphs:
We say that a sequence  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}}  is  γ \gamma -alternating  if the sign of  ( − 1 ) j ⋅ γ j (-1)^{j}\cdot\gamma_{j}  does not depend on  j ∈ { 0 , … , ⌊ d 2 ⌋ } j\in\{0,\dots,\left\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\right\rfloor\} . 
 
 
 Theorem 3.36 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a connected chordal graph with a perfect elimination ordering  θ \theta .
Then the sequence of coefficients of the polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) \Psi(D_{\theta};t)  is  γ \gamma -alternating. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We first prove that the  γ \gamma -coefficients of the sequence  ( 1 , 1 , … , 1 ) (1,1,\dots,1)  of length  b b  is  γ j = ( − 1 ) j ⋅ ( b − j j ) \gamma_{j}=(-1)^{j}\cdot\binom{b-j}{j}  and that products of  γ \gamma -alternating sequences are  γ \gamma -alternating. Then we conclude the proof using  Proposition   3.26 . 
 
 
 Let  F n ​ ( s , x ) F_{n}(s,x)  be the  n th n^{\text{th}}  bivariate Fibonacci polynomial, that is  F ⁡ ( s , x ) = s ⋅ F n − 1 ​ ( s , x ) + x ⋅ F n − 2 ​ ( s , x ) F(s,x)=s\cdot F_{n-1}(s,x)+x\cdot F_{n-2}(s,x) , and  F 0 ​ ( s , x ) = 0 F_{0}(s,x)=0 ,  F 1 ​ ( s , x ) = 1 F_{1}(s,x)=1 .
Using the Binet formula, we get  F n ​ ( s , x ) = α ​ ( s , x ) n − β ​ ( s , x ) n α ⁡ ( s , x ) − β ⁡ ( s , x ) F_{n}(s,x)=\frac{\alpha(s,x)^{n}-\beta(s,x)^{n}}{\alpha(s,x)-\beta(s,x)}  where  α ⁡ ( s , x ) \alpha(s,x)  and  β ⁡ ( s , x ) \beta(s,x)  are the roots of  Z 2 − s ​ Z − x Z^{2}-sZ-x .
The usual “sum of diagonals” argument on the Fibonacci numbers yields
 F b + 1 ​ ( s , x ) = ∑ j = 0 ⌊ b / 2 ⌋ ( b − j j ) ​ s b − 2 ​ j ​ x j F_{b+1}(s,x)=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor b/2\rfloor}\binom{b-j}{j}s^{b-2j}x^{j} .
In particular 
 

 
 
 ∑ j = 0 ⌊ b / 2 ⌋ ( − 1 ) j ​ ( b − j j ) ​ t j ​ ( 1 + t ) b − 2 ​ j = ( 1 + t ) b ​ ∑ j = 0 ⌊ b / 2 ⌋ ( b − j j ) ​ 1 b − 2 ​ j ​ ( − t ( 1 + t ) 2 ) j = ( 1 + t ) b ​ F b + 1 ​ ( 1 , − t ( 1 + t ) 2 ) . \sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor b/2\rfloor}(-1)^{j}\binom{b-j}{j}t^{j}(1+t)^{b-2j}=(1+t)^{b}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor b/2\rfloor}\binom{b-j}{j}1^{b-2j}\left(\frac{-t}{(1+t)^{2}}\right)^{j}=(1+t)^{b}F_{b+1}\left(1,\frac{-t}{(1+t)^{2}}\right). 
 
 
 
 
 Using  α ⁡ ( 1 , − x ) = 1 + 1 − 4 ​ x 2 \alpha(1,-x)=\frac{1+\sqrt{1-4x}}{2}  and  β ⁡ ( 1 , − x ) = 1 − 1 − 4 ​ x 2 \beta(1,-x)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4x}}{2} , and evaluating for  x = t ( 1 + t ) 2 x=\frac{t}{(1+t)^{2}} , we get (after several simplifications):
 ( 1 + t ) b ​ F b + 1 ​ ( 1 , − t ( 1 + t ) 2 ) = 1 − t b + 1 1 − t = 1 + t + t 2 + ⋯ + t b (1+t)^{b}F_{b+1}\left(1,\frac{-t}{(1+t)^{2}}\right)=\frac{1-t^{b+1}}{1-t}=1+t+t^{2}+\dots+t^{b} .
Hence,  γ j = ( − 1 ) j ⋅ ( b − j j ) \gamma_{j}=(-1)^{j}\cdot\binom{b-j}{j}  is indeed the  γ \gamma -vector of the sequence  ( 1 , 1 , … , 1 ) (1,1,\dots,1)  of length  b b . This sequence is thus  γ \gamma -alternating. 
 
 
 Besides, if  ( − 1 ) j ​ γ j ≥ 0 (-1)^{j}\gamma_{j}\geq 0  and  ( − 1 ) k ​ δ k ≥ 0 (-1)^{k}\delta_{k}\geq 0  for all  j , k ≥ 0 j,k\geq 0 , then 
 

 
 
 ( ∑ j γ j ​ t j ​ ( 1 + t ) b − 2 ​ j ) ⋅ ( ∑ k γ k ​ t k ​ ( 1 + t ) c − 2 ​ k ) = ∑ p ( ∑ j = 0 p γ j ⋅ δ p − j ) ​ t p ​ ( 1 + t ) b + c − 2 ​ p , \left(\sum_{j}\gamma_{j}t^{j}(1+t)^{b-2j}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{k}\gamma_{k}t^{k}(1+t)^{c-2k}\right)=\sum_{p}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{p}\gamma_{j}\cdot\delta_{p-j}\right)t^{p}(1+t)^{b+c-2p}\,, 
 
 
 where for all  j ∈ { 0 , … , p } j\in\{0,\dots,p\} ,  ( − 1 ) p ​ γ j ⋅ δ p − j ≥ 0 (-1)^{p}\gamma_{j}\cdot\delta_{p-j}\geq 0 , so  ( − 1 ) p ⋅ ( ∑ j = 0 p γ j ⋅ δ p − j ) ≥ 0 (-1)^{p}\cdot\left(\sum_{j=0}^{p}\gamma_{j}\cdot\delta_{p-j}\right)\geq 0 .
Hence if two sequences are  γ \gamma -alternating, then their product is also  γ \gamma -alternating. 
 
 
 By  Proposition   3.26 , the sequence of coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D θ , t ) \Psi(D_{\theta};t)  is a product of  γ \gamma -alternating sequences:
it is  γ \gamma -alternating.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.37 . 
 
 Note that we are not the first to describe a  γ \gamma -alternating behavior:
in  [ 14 , Corollary 7.10] , the authors show that for a  ( d − 1 ) (d-1) -dimensional Cohen–Macaulay sphere  Δ \Delta  with positive  h h -vectors, the ring  R Δ R_{\Delta}  they define is  γ \gamma -alternating. 
 
 
 
 Question 3.38 . 
 
 Classify the graphs  G G  and orientations  D D  such that the coefficients of  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  form a  γ \gamma -positive or a  γ \gamma -alternating sequence. 
 
 
 
 
 3.2.4.  Decomposition of the acyclic polynomial and reversion of the direction of all arcs 

 
 In order to propose some deletion-contraction formulas, we will need to split the acyclic polynomial according to the following notion: 
 
 
 Definition 3.39 . 
 
 For two distinct nodes   u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  in a graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , and an acyclic orientation  A A  of  G G , we define the  direction  between  u u  and  v v  in  A A  as: 
 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( u , v ) ≔ { + 1  if there exists a directed path from  ​ u ​  to  ​ v ​  in  ​ A , − 1  if there exists a directed path from  ​ v ​  to  ​ u ​  in  ​ A , 0  otherwise  {A(u,v)}\coloneqq\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}+1&\text{ if there exists a directed path from }u\text{ to }v\text{ in }A,\\
-1&\text{ if there exists a directed path from }v\text{ to }u\text{ in }A,\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{array}\right. 
 
 
 
 
 We allow such a directed path to be composed of a single edge.
Besides:  A ⁡ ( v , u ) = − A ⁡ ( u , v ) A(v,u)=-A(u,v) .
For a sequence of acyclic orientations  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A r ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{r}) , let  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) ≔ ( A 1 ​ ( u , v ) , … , A r ​ ( u , v ) ) {{\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)}\coloneqq\bigl(A_{1}(u,v),\dots,A_{r}(u,v)\bigr) . 
 
 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of the graph  G G .
Fix an orientation of  G G , say  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  where  D D  is an orientation of  H H , and  D ′ D^{\prime}  is an orientation of  H ′ H^{\prime} .
If  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  is acyclic, then both  D D  and  D ′ D^{\prime}  are acyclic.
Moreover, if  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  is acyclic, then for any nodes  u , v u,v  of  G G , we have  D ⁡ ( u , v ) ⋅ D ′ ​ ( u , v ) ≠ − 1 D(u,v)\cdot D^{\prime}(u,v)\neq-1 .
The reciprocal is straightforward: 
 
 
 Lemma 3.40 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of the graph  G G .
The map  ( A , A ′ ) ↦ A ⊔ A ′ (A,A^{\prime})\mapsto A\sqcup A^{\prime}  is a bijection from the pairs of acyclic orientations of  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) ⋅ A ′ ​ ( u , v ) ≠ − 1 A(u,v)\cdot A^{\prime}(u,v)\neq-1  for all nodes  u , v u,v  of  G G , and the acyclic orientations of  G G . 
 
 
 Moreover,  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is the subgraph of the Cartesian product  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  induced on these pairs of acyclic orientations, that is  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  and  B ⊔ B ′ B\sqcup B^{\prime}  are adjacent in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  if and only if either  A = B A=B , and  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  are adjacent in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}) , or  A ′ = B ′ A^{\prime}=B^{\prime} , and  A A  and  B B  are adjacent in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Clearly,  ( A , A ′ ) ↦ A ⊔ A ′ (A,A^{\prime})\mapsto A\sqcup A^{\prime}  is an injection, since this is just a standard operation between sets.
Moreover,  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  is acyclic if and only if for all  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  there is no path from  u u  to  v v  and from  v v  to  u u  in  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime} , which is equivalent to: for all  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  there is no path from  u u  to  v v  and from  v v  to  u u  both in  A A  ( i.e.   A A  is acyclic), and there is no path from  u u  to  v v  and from  v v  to  u u  both in  A ′ A^{\prime}  ( i.e.   A ′ A^{\prime}  is acyclic), and there is no path from  u u  to  v v  and from  v v  to  u u , one in  A A  and the other in  A ′ A^{\prime}  ( i.e.   A ⁡ ( u , v ) ⋅ A ′ ​ ( u , v ) ≠ − 1 A(u,v)\cdot A^{\prime}(u,v)\neq-1 ).
This proves the bijection. 
 
 
 Besides,  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  and  B ⊔ B ′ B\sqcup B^{\prime}  are adjacent if and only if they differ on exactly 1 edge: due to the bipartition of the edges of  G G , this edge is either in  H H  or in  H ′ H^{\prime}  not in both.
This proves the adjacency relation.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Some fundamental properties of the acyclic polynomial come from understanding what happens when we reverse the direction of all the arcs of an orientation at once.
We will often use the following straightforward lemma: 
 
 
 Lemma 3.41 . 
 
 Fix a (di)graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , any  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N , and any orientation  D D  of  G G .
The map  X ↦ E ∖ X X\mapsto E\smallsetminus X  satisfies  rk D ⁡ D E ∖ X = | E | − rk D ⁡ D X \rank_{D}D^{E\smallsetminus X}=|E|-\rank_{D}D^{X} , and it is: 
 
 (1)  
 
 an involution without fixed point on  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{O}}(G) , and 
 
 (2)  
 
 an involution without fixed point on  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) , and 
 
 (3)  
 
 an involution without fixed point on  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~A(u,v)=0\} , and 
 
 (4)  
 
 a bijection between  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; A ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~A(u,v)=+1\}  and  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; A ⁡ ( u , v ) = − 1 } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~A(u,v)=-1\} , and 
 
 (5)  
 
 if  | E | |E|  is even, an involution without fixed point on  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A ​  is even } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A\text{ is even}\} , and an involution without fixed point on  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A ​  is odd } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A\text{ is odd}\} , and 
 
 (6)  
 
 if  | E | |E|  is odd, a bijection between the set  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A ​  is even } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A\text{ is even}\}  and the set { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A ​  is odd } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A\text{ is odd}\} . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It is immediate that  X ↦ E ∖ X X\mapsto E\smallsetminus X  is an involution of  2 E 2^{E} .
For ( 1 ), note that  𝒪 ⁡ ( G ) {\mathcal{O}}(G)  is isomorphic to  2 E 2^{E} .
For ( 2 ), note that reversing the directions of all the arcs at once does not change whether the orientation is acyclic or not.
For ( 3 ) and ( 4 ), note that reversing the directions of all the arcs at once changes a directed path from  u u  to  v v  into a directed path from  v v  to  u u ,  i.e.   D E ∖ X ​ ( u , v ) = − D X ​ ( u , v ) D^{E\smallsetminus X}(u,v)=-D^{X}(u,v)  for all orientations  D D  of  G G . 
 
 
 The claimed rank equality is by definition:
 rk D ⁡ D E ∖ X = | E ∖ X | = | E | − | X | = | E | − rk D ⁡ D X \rank_{D}D^{E\smallsetminus X}=|E\smallsetminus X|=|E|-|X|=|E|-\rank_{D}D^{X} .
This immediately implies ( 5 ) and ( 6 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Using the fundamental involution, we can re-prove one of the powerful results of  [ 37 ]  that they repeatedly use in order to show that some graphs are not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Our proof is essentially the same as theirs, but written with our language. 
 
 
 Theorem 3.42   ( [ 37 , Lemma 3.1] ) . 
 
 If a graph with an even number of edges is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then its number of acyclic orientations is divisible by  4 4 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  G G  be such a graph, and  D D  some orientation of  G G . Since  | E | |E|  is even,  Lemma   3.41  ( 5 ) shows that  X ↦ E ∖ X X\mapsto E\smallsetminus X  induces an involution without fixed point on  { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A ​  is even } \{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A\text{ is even}\} .
This implies that the latter set is of even size. 
 
 
 By  Lemma   2.9 , we have  σ ⁡ ( D ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) = 0 \sigma(D)=\Psi(D;-1)=0 .
So writing  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = ∑ k = 0 d p k ​ t k \Psi(D;t)=\sum_{k=0}^{d}p_{k}t^{k}  we get that  ∑ k ​  even p k = ∑ k ​  odd p k \sum_{k\text{ even}}p_{k}=\sum_{k\text{ odd}}p_{k} .
Since  ∑ k ​  even p k = | { A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ; rk D ⁡ A ​  is even } | \sum_{k\text{ even}}p_{k}=\bigl|\{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)~;~\rank_{D}A\text{ is even}\}\bigr|  is even, we finally get that  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) = ∑ k p k \psi(G)=\Psi(D;1)=\sum_{k}p_{k}  is divisible by  4 4 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Thanks to the notion of direction and the fundamental involution, we can isolate two sub-polynomials hidden inside the acyclic polynomial.
The core idea is to split the edges of  G G  between two subgraphs  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  which meet on exactly two nodes  u u  and  v v :
in an acyclic orientation of  G G , either there is no directed path between  u u  and  v v  inside the induced orientation of  H H , or there is a directed path from  u u  to  v v , or from  v v  to  u u .
The set of possible acyclic orientations of  H ′ H^{\prime}  depends only on which of these three cases we are in, not on the exact acyclic orientation of  H H .
In  Figure   3 , we illustrate the particular case where  H ′ H^{\prime}  is the subgraph of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  with nodes  u , v u,v  and no edge. 
 
 
 Proposition 3.43 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\}  for two nodes  u , v u,v .
Then:
 Ψ ⁡ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) + Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) + t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 t ) \Psi(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)+\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)+t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};\tfrac{1}{t}) , where: 
 

 
 
 Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) ≔ ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′  and  Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) ≔ ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ {\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ and }\hskip 14.22636pt{\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Clearly, for any  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime} , and  t t , we have: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ + ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ + ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = − 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ \Psi(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}~+~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}~+~\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=-1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}} 
 
 
 
 
 Thus, we only need to prove that  ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = − 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 t ) \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=-1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};\frac{1}{t}) .
Using the bijection  X ↦ E ∖ X X\mapsto E\smallsetminus X  on the graph  G G  from  Lemma   3.41  ( 4 ), we get: 
 

 
 
 ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = − 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 t | E | − rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = t | E | ⋅ ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 ( 1 t ) rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 t ) ∎ \begin{array}[]{rcl}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=-1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}&=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=+1\end{subarray}}t^{|E|-\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}\\
&=&t^{|E|}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=+1\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right)^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}\\
&=&t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};\tfrac{1}{t})~\qed\end{array} 
 
 
 
 
 
 As a special case, we define the  partial acyclic polynomials   Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) {\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)}  and  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) {\Psi^{u\to v}(D;t)}  to the polynomials associated to the bipartition of edges  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  where  H ′ H^{\prime}  is the graph with nodes  u , v u,v  and no edge between them.
The polynomial  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)  (resp.  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t) ) counts the number of acyclic orientations of  G G  containing no directed path from  u u  to  v v  nor from  v v  to  u u  (resp. containing a directed path from  u u  to  v v ), according to their rank with respect to the orientation  D D . 
 
 
 Recall that a polynomial  P ⁡ ( t ) = ∑ k = 0 d p k ​ t k P(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{d}p_{k}t^{k}  is  palindromic of length  d d  if its coefficients satisfy  p k = p d − k p_{k}=p_{d-k}  for all  k ∈ [ d ] k\in[d] , or equivalently if  P ⁡ ( t ) = t d ⋅ P ⁡ ( 1 t ) P(t)=t^{d}\cdot P(\tfrac{1}{t}) . 
 
 
 Corollary 3.44 . 
 
 The acyclic polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is palindromic of length  | E | |E| . 
 
 
 Furthermore, if  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  is a bipartition of the edges of  G G  with  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , then the polynomial  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)  is palindromic of length  | E | |E| . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Using the involution  X ↦ E ∖ X X\mapsto E\smallsetminus X  from  Lemma   3.41  ( 3 ), we get: 
 

 
 
 Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t | E | − rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = t | E | ⋅ Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{|E|-\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};\tfrac{1}{t}) 
 
 
 
 
 Hence, the polynomial  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)  is palindromic.
Consequently, by  Proposition   3.43 ,  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is also palindromic, as it is the sum of a palindromic polynomial  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)  with another palindromic polynomial  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) + t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D , 1 t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t)+t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D;\tfrac{1}{t}) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.45 . 
 
 Note that  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is not necessarily of degree  | E | |E|  (nor does it need to have a non-zero constant term), especially when  D D  is not acyclic, see the example of cycles  C n C_{n}  in  Example   3.16 , or of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  in  Examples   3.17  and  3 . 
 
 
 
 For two orientations  D D  and  D ′ D^{\prime}  of the same graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , it is not evident how to determine  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) \Psi(D^{\prime};t)  given  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t) .
However, for an orientation  D D , we can easily express the acyclic polynomial of the reverse orientation  D E D^{E} : 
 
 
 Corollary 3.46 . 
 
 For any digraph  D = ( N , E ) D=(N,E) , we have:
 Ψ ⁡ ( D E , t ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D^{E};t)=\Psi(D;t) . 
 
 
 Furthermore, if  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  is a bipartition of the edges of  G G  with  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , then we have  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( ( D ⊔ D ′ ) E , t ) = Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}((D\sqcup D^{\prime})^{E};t)=\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t) , and also  Ψ u → v ​ ( ( D ⊔ D ′ ) E , t ) = t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 t ) \Psi^{u\to v}((D\sqcup D^{\prime})^{E};t)=t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};\tfrac{1}{t}) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Note that  rk D E ⁡ D X = | E ∖ X | = | E | − rk D ⁡ D X \rank_{D^{E}}D^{X}=|E\smallsetminus X|=|E|-\rank_{D}D^{X} .
Using the involution  X ↦ E ∖ X X\mapsto E\smallsetminus X  from  Lemma   3.41  ( 2 ), we get 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D E , t ) = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D E ) t rk D E ⁡ A = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) t | E | − rk D ⁡ A = t | E | ⋅ Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 t ) . \Psi(D^{E};t)=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D^{E})}t^{\rank_{D^{E}}A}=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D)}t^{|E|-\rank_{D}A}=t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi(D;\tfrac{1}{t}). 
 
 
 
 
 Since  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is palindromic of length  | E | |E|  by  Corollary   3.44 , the latter is equal to  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t) . 
 
 
 The proof is the same for  Ψ u ↮ v \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v} .
From the equality on  Ψ \Psi  and on  Ψ u ↮ v \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v} , we deduce the one of  Ψ u → v \Psi^{u\to v}  via the decomposition of  Proposition   3.43 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example 3.47 . 
 
 By  Corollary   3.46 , we can deduce from  Figure   3  the acyclic polynomials (and their decomposition) of the orientations obtained by reversing the direction of all the arcs in the drawn orientations. 
 
 
 
 
 3.2.5.  Automorphisms of (di)graphs and palindromicity of  Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t) 

 
 It is straightforward to show that if two orientations  D D  and  D ′ D^{\prime}  of  G G  are isomorphic (as digraphs,  i.e.  if there exists  φ : N → N \varphi:N\to N  such that  x ​ y ∈ D xy\in D  if and only if  φ ⁡ ( x ) ​ φ ​ ( y ) ∈ D ′ \varphi(x)\varphi(y)\in D^{\prime} ), then their acyclic polynomials are the same.
We can go one step further by considering automorphisms of digraphs. 
 
 
 Definition 3.48 . 
 
 For a (directed) graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , an  automorphism  is a bijection  φ : N → N \varphi:N\to N  such that  G G  has the (directed) edge  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E  if and only if  G G  has the (directed) edge  φ ⁡ ( u ) ​ φ ​ ( v ) \varphi(u)\varphi(v) . 
 
 
 
 The set of automorphisms of a (directed) graph together with the composition of functions forms a group, which is denoted  Aut ⁡ ( G ) {\Aut(G)} . 
 
 
 Theorem 3.49 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a graph with a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , and  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  an orientation of  G G .
If the directed graph  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  admits an automorphism  φ \varphi  which induces an automorphism of  D D  and such that  φ ⁡ ( u ) = v \varphi(u)=v  and  φ ⁡ ( v ) = u \varphi(v)=u , then  Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)  is palindromic of length  | E | |E| . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Fix  G G ,  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime} ,  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  and  φ \varphi  as above.
For an acyclic orientation  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  of  G G , we denote  φ ⁡ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) \varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})  the orientation whose arcs are from  φ ⁡ ( x ) \varphi(x)  to  φ ⁡ ( y ) \varphi(y)  for all arcs from  x x  to  y y  in  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime} . 
 
 
 First, if there is a directed path  x 0 → x 1 → … → x ℓ x_{0}\to x_{1}\to\dots\to x_{\ell}  in  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime} , then there is a directed path  φ ⁡ ( x 0 ) → φ ⁡ ( x 1 ) → … → φ ⁡ ( x ℓ ) \varphi(x_{0})\to\varphi(x_{1})\to\dots\to\varphi(x_{\ell})  in  φ ⁡ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) \varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime}) .
Hence, since  φ \varphi  is a bijection,  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  is acyclic if and only if  φ ⁡ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) \varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})  is acyclic.
Moreover, since  φ ⁡ ( u ) = v \varphi(u)=v  and  φ ⁡ ( v ) = u \varphi(v)=u , there is a path from  u u  to  v v  in  A A  if and only if there is a path from  v v  to  u u  in  φ ⁡ ( A ) \varphi(A)  because  φ \varphi  is also an automorphism of  D D .
Second, let  X ⊆ E X\subseteq E  be such that  A ⊔ A ′ = ( D ⊔ D ′ ) X A\sqcup A^{\prime}=(D\sqcup D^{\prime})^{X} ,  i.e.   X X  is the set of edges  x ​ y ∈ E xy\in E  such that we have an arc from  x x  to  y y  in  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  but an arc from  y y  to  x x  in  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime} .
For such  x ​ y ∈ E xy\in E , we have  φ ⁡ ( x ) → φ ⁡ ( y ) \varphi(x)\to\varphi(y)  in  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  because  φ \varphi  is an automorphism of  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime} , and  φ ⁡ ( x ) → φ ⁡ ( y ) \varphi(x)\to\varphi(y)  in  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  by definition.
Hence, if  A ⊔ A ′ = ( D ⊔ D ′ ) X A\sqcup A^{\prime}=(D\sqcup D^{\prime})^{X} , then  φ ⁡ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) = ( D ⊔ D ′ ) { φ ⁡ ( x ) ​ φ ​ ( y ) ; x ​ y ∈ E } \varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})=(D\sqcup D^{\prime})^{\{\varphi(x)\varphi(y)~;~xy\in E\}} .
In particular, we get:  rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ φ ⁡ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) = rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ \rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}\varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})=\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime} . 
 
 
 Now, we mix both  φ \varphi  and the reversion of all arcs.
Note that if  A A  admits a directed path from  u u  to  v v , then  φ ⁡ ( A ) \varphi(A)  admits a directed path from  v v  to  u u , so  φ ​ ( A ) E \varphi(A)^{E}  admits a directed path from  u u  to  v v  again.
Consequently, the map  A ⊔ A ′ ↦ φ ​ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) E A\sqcup A^{\prime}\mapsto\varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})^{E}  defines a bijection (whose reciprocal is  A ⊔ A ′ ↦ φ − 1 ​ ( ( A ⊔ A ′ ) E ) A\sqcup A^{\prime}\mapsto\varphi^{-1}((A\sqcup A^{\prime})^{E}) ) of the set of acyclic orientations of  G G  with a directed path from  u u  to  v v  in  D D , such that  rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ φ ​ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) E = | E | − rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ φ ⁡ ( A ⊔ A ′ ) = | E | − rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ \rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}\varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})^{E}=|E|-\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}\varphi(A\sqcup A^{\prime})=|E|-\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime} .
This bijection yields the following transformation of the sum: 
 

 
 
 Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = ∑ B ⊔ B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) B ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ φ − 1 ​ ( B ⊔ B ′ ) E = t | E | ⋅ ∑ B ⊔ B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) B ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 t − rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ B ⊔ B ′ = t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 t ) \begin{array}[]{rcl}\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)&=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=+1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}\vskip 7.11317pt=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\sqcup B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
B(u,v)=+1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}\varphi^{-1}(B\sqcup B^{\prime})^{E}}\\
&=&t^{|E|}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\sqcup B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D)\\
B(u,v)=+1\end{subarray}}t^{-\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}B\sqcup B^{\prime}}\vskip 7.11317pt=t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};\tfrac{1}{t})\end{array} 
 
 
 This ensures that  Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)  is palindromic of length  | E | |E| .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example 3.50 . 
 
 For the complete bipartite graph  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  depicted in  Figure   3 , the orientations          1           ,         3           \hbox to11.67pt{\vbox to13.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-6.55522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

These are the only orientations of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  to admit an automorphism that exchanges  u u  and  v v , and are indeed the only orientations for which  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t)  is palindromic of length  | E | = 6 |E|=6 . 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.51 . 
 
 One has to be careful not to confuse automorphisms of graphs and of digraphs.
For instance, let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a path on  ℓ \ell  nodes, with  ℓ \ell  odd, denoted  x 1 , … , x ℓ x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}  with  x i ​ x i + 1 ∈ E x_{i}x_{i+1}\in E .
Let  u = x 1 u=x_{1}  and  v = x ℓ v=x_{\ell}  be the endpoints of this path.
The graph  G G  admits an automorphism that exchanges  u u  and  v v , namely  φ : x i ↦ x ℓ − i \varphi:x_{i}\mapsto x_{\ell-i}  for  i ∈ [ ℓ ] i\in[\ell] .
Moreover, this is the only automorphism of  G G . 
 
 
 Consequently, there is no orientation  D D  of  G G  which admits an automorphism that exchanges  u u  and  v v .
Indeed, such an automorphism of  D D  would induce an automorphism of  G G , so it would have to be the above  φ \varphi , yet since  ℓ \ell  is odd, there is an edge  x ( ℓ − 1 ) / 2 ​ x ( ℓ + 1 ) / 2 x_{(\ell-1)/2}x_{(\ell+1)/2}  in  G G  whose image by  φ \varphi  is  x ( ℓ + 1 ) / 2 ​ x ( ℓ − 1 ) / 2 x_{(\ell+1)/2}x_{(\ell-1)/2} :
whatever how we orient this edge in  D D , its image by  φ \varphi  would be the same edge but with the reverse orientation, so it would not be an arc of  D D . 
 
 
 
 
 3.2.6.  Deletion-contraction phenomenon for the acyclic polynomial 

 
 By means of the decomposition of  Proposition   3.43 , we can present a deletion-contraction phenomenon.
It will be incomplete since it will fail to handle pairs of nodes which share a neighbor which is of degree 3 or more (see  Remark   3.57 ), but it will still enable us to compute the acyclic signature of new graphs, such as the multipaths (see  Section   3.3.3 ). We start with contraction. 
 
 
 Definition 3.52 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a graph with a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} .
We say that the nodes  u u  and  v v  are  suspended over  H H  if all the common neighbors of  u u  and  v v  in  H H  are of degree  2 2 .
Moreover, for an orientation  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  of  G G , if  u u  and  v v  are suspended over  H H , then we denote by  x x  the number of directed paths of length  2 2  from  u u  to  v v  or from  v v  to  u u  in  D D , and let  y y  be the number of paths of length  2 2  of  H H  which are not directed paths in  D D , and we denote by  D ~ / u ​ v \tilde{D}/_{uv}  the contraction on  { u , v } \{u,v\}  of the orientation  D D  with the common neighbors of  u u  and  v v  deleted. 
 
 
 
 Example 3.53 . 
 
 In  Figure   3 , the nodes  u u  and  v v  are suspended over  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3} , while any pair among the three other nodes is not suspended over  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3} .
For orientation  5 5 , we have  x = 2 x=2  and  y = 1 y=1 .
The orientation  D ~ / u ​ v \tilde{D}/_{uv}  is just a digraph with a single node, whatever the chosen orientation of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3} . 
 
 
 
 Proposition 3.54 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a graph with a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , and  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  an orientation of  G G .
Let also  ε = 1 \varepsilon=1  if  u ​ v ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) uv\in E(H)  and  ε = 0 \varepsilon=0  if  u ​ v ∉ E ⁡ ( H ) uv\notin E(H) .
If  u u  and  v v  do not have a common neighbor in  H H , then 
 

 
 
 Ψ u ↮ v ( D ⊔ D ′ ; t ) = ( 1 − ε ) ⋅ Ψ ( D ′ ; t ) ⋅ Ψ ( D / u ​ v ; t ) . \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\Psi(D^{\prime};t)\cdot\Psi(D/_{uv};t)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 More generally, if  u u  and  v v  are suspended over  H H , then with the notations of  Definition   3.52 : 
 

 
 
 Ψ u ↮ v ( D ⊔ D ′ ; t ) = ( 1 − ε ) ⋅ ( 2 t ) x ⋅ ( 1 + t 2 ) y ⋅ Ψ ( D ′ ; t ) ⋅ Ψ ( D ~ / u ​ v ; t ) . \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot(2t)^{x}\cdot(1+t^{2})^{y}\cdot\Psi(D^{\prime};t)\cdot\Psi(\tilde{D}/_{uv};t)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The second formula implies the first when  x = y = 0 x=y=0 , hence, we only prove the second. 
 
 
 If there is an edge between  u u  and  v v  in  H H , then all acyclic orientations  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  of  G = D ⊔ D ′ G=D\sqcup D^{\prime}  satisfy  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = ± 1 A(u,v)=\pm 1 , so  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = 0 \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=0 .
The right-hand side for the formula is also  0 0  since  ( 1 − ε ) = 0 (1-\varepsilon)=0  in this case.
Hence, we assume that there is no edge  u ​ v ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) uv\in E(H)  in the following. 
 
 
 The set of acyclic orientations  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  of  G = D ⊔ D ′ G=D\sqcup D^{\prime}  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 A(u,v)=0  is in bijection with the set of pairs  ( A , A ′ ) ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) (A,A^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 A(u,v)=0 .
Consequently, since  t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = t rk D ⁡ A ⋅ t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=t^{\rank_{D}A}\cdot t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}} : 
 

 
 
 Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = ( ∑ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ′ ) t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ ) ⋅ ( ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ⁡ A ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=\left(\sum_{A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D^{\prime})}t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D}A}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 The left parenthesis is  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) \Psi(D^{\prime};t) . 
 
 
 For the right parenthesis, consider the map  A ↦ A ~ / u ​ v A\mapsto\tilde{A}/_{uv} .
This maps an acyclic orientation of  D D  to an orientation of  D ~ / u ​ v \tilde{D}/_{uv} .
Moreover, if  A A  is acyclic and  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 A(u,v)=0 , then  A ~ / u ​ v \tilde{A}/_{uv}  is acyclic too (because a directed path between  u u  and  v v  in  A ~ / u ​ v \tilde{A}/_{uv}  yields a directed path between  u u  and  v v  in  A A ).
Besides, two orientations  A A  and  B B  of  D D  satisfy  A ~ / u ​ v = B ~ / u ​ v \tilde{A}/_{uv}=\tilde{B}/_{uv}  if and only if they only differ on the orientation of the paths of length  2 2  between  u u  and  v v  (if there exist any).
Let  W ⊆ N W\subseteq N  be the set of nodes  w w  such that there is a path  ( u , w , v ) (u,w,v)  in  H H , and let  W x W_{x}  (resp.  W y W_{y} ) be the set of all nodes  w w  such that  ( u , w , v ) (u,w,v)  is a path in  H H  and is (resp. is not) a directed path in  D D . 
 
 
 Fix  w ∈ W w\in W :
the orientation  A A  satisfies  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 A(u,v)=0  if and only if  w w  is either a source or a sink in  ( u , w , v ) (u,w,v) .
Hence for a given acyclic orientation  a a  of  H ~ / u ​ v \tilde{H}/_{uv} , the set of acyclic orientations  A A  of  H H  such that  A ~ / u ​ v = a \tilde{A}/_{uv}=a  is in bijection with partitions  I ⊔ J = W I\sqcup J=W , namely  ( I , J ) (I,J)  is associated to the orientation of  H H  given by the arcs of  a a  together with the orientation of  ( u , w , v ) (u,w,v)  where  w w  is a source if  w ∈ I w\in I  (resp. a sink if  w ∈ J w\in J ).
Note that this set of orientations admits an involution  η \eta  without fixed point that exchanges the roles of  I I  and  J J .
We will sum over these possible partitions for a fixed  a ∈ D ~ / u ​ v a\in\tilde{D}/_{uv} , it remains to compute  rk D ⁡ A \rank_{D}A  from  rk D ~ / u ​ v a \rank_{\tilde{D}/_{uv}}a  and  I , J I,J . 
 
 
 Let  A A  be an acyclic orientation of  H H  where  w ∈ W w\in W  is either a source or a sink. 
 
 
 
 (1)  
 
 If  w ∈ W x w\in W_{x} , then  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ ( A ∖ w ) + 1 \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D}(A\smallsetminus w)+1 ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 If  w ∈ I w\in I  (resp.  w ∈ J w\in J ) and  w w  is a sink in  D D , then  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ ( A ∖ w ) + 2 \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D}(A\smallsetminus w)+2  (resp.  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ ( A ∖ w ) \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D}(A\smallsetminus w) ); 
 
 (3)  
 
 If  w ∈ I w\in I  (resp.  w ∈ J w\in J ) and  w w  is a source in  D D , then  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ ( A ∖ w ) \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D}(A\smallsetminus w)  (resp.  rk D ⁡ A = rk D ⁡ ( A ∖ w ) + 2 \rank_{D}A=\rank_{D}(A\smallsetminus w)+2 ). 
 
 
 
 
 Consequently, up to using the involution  η \eta , we assume without loss of generality that all  w ∈ W y w\in W_{y}  are sinks in  D D .
Therefore, for a fixed  a ∈ 𝒜 𝒪 ( D ~ / u ​ v ) a\in{\mathcal{AO}}(\tilde{D}/_{uv}) , we get: 
 

 
 
 rk D A = ( rk D ~ / u ​ v a ) + 1 ⋅ | W x | + 0 ⋅ | I ∩ W y | + 2 ⋅ | J ∩ W y | = ( rk D ~ / u ​ v a ) + x + 2 ⋅ | J ∩ W y | . \rank_{D}A=(\rank_{\tilde{D}/_{uv}}a)+1\cdot|W_{x}|+0\cdot|I\cap W_{y}|+2\cdot|J\cap W_{y}|=(\rank_{\tilde{D}/_{uv}}a)+x+2\cdot|J\cap W_{y}|. 
 
 
 Finally, we obtain (remember that  | W x | = x |W_{x}|=x ,  | W y | = y |W_{y}|=y  and  | W | = x + y |W|=x+y ): 
 

 
 
 ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ⁡ A = ∑ a ∈ 𝒜 𝒪 ( D ~ / u ​ v ) t rk D ~ / u ​ v a ⋅ t x ⋅ ( ∑ J ⊆ W t 2 ​ | J ∩ W y | ) = t x ⋅ ∑ a ∈ 𝒜 𝒪 ( D ~ / u ​ v ) t rk D ~ / u ​ v a ⋅ ( ∑ J ⊆ W y t 2 ​ | J | ⋅ 2 | W | − | W y | ) = t x ⋅ ( 1 + t 2 ) | W y | ⋅ 2 x ⋅ ∑ a ∈ 𝒜 𝒪 ( D ~ / u ​ v ) t rk D ~ / u ​ v a = ( 2 t ) x ⋅ ( 1 + t 2 ) y ⋅ Ψ ( D ~ / u ​ v ; t ) ∎ \begin{array}[]{rclr}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D)\\
A(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D}A}&=&\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{AO}}(\tilde{D}/_{uv})}t^{\rank_{\tilde{D}/_{uv}}a}\cdot t^{x}\cdot\left(\sum_{J\subseteq W}t^{2|J\cap W_{y}|}\right)&\\
&=&t^{x}\cdot\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{AO}}(\tilde{D}/_{uv})}t^{\rank_{\tilde{D}/_{uv}}a}\cdot\left(\sum_{J\subseteq W_{y}}t^{2|J|}\cdot 2^{|W|-|W_{y}|}\right)&\\
&=&t^{x}\cdot(1+t^{2})^{|W_{y}|}\cdot 2^{x}\cdot\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{AO}}(\tilde{D}/_{uv})}t^{\rank_{\tilde{D}/_{uv}}a}&\\
&=&(2t)^{x}\cdot(1+t^{2})^{y}\cdot\Psi(\tilde{D}/_{uv};t)&\qed\end{array} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.55 . 
 
 As typical use cases of  Proposition   3.54 , note that if  H ′ = ( { u , v } , ∅ ) H^{\prime}=(\{u,v\},\varnothing) , then  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) = 1 \Psi(D^{\prime};t)=1 ; while if  H ′ H^{\prime}  is only the edge between  u u  and  v v , then  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) = 1 + t \Psi(D^{\prime};t)=1+t . 
 
 
 
 Example 3.56 . 
 
 In  Figure   3 , with the bipartition  K 2 , 3 = H ⊔ H ′ K_{2,3}=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  where  H ′ H^{\prime}  is the graph with nodes  u , v u,v  and no edge, the polynomial  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)  can be deduced directly using the second formula of  Proposition   3.54 :
for each orientation, denoting  x x  the number of directed paths between  u u  and  v v  in  D D , we get  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) = ( 2 ​ t ) x ​ ( 1 + t 2 ) 3 − x \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)=(2t)^{x}(1+t^{2})^{3-x}  whose value is either  8 ​ t 3 8t^{3} , or  4 ​ t 2 + 4 ​ t 4 4t^{2}+4t^{4} , or  2 ​ t + 4 ​ t 3 + 2 ​ t 5 2t+4t^{3}+2t^{5} , or  1 + 3 ​ t 2 + 3 ​ t 4 + t 6 1+3t^{2}+3t^{4}+t^{6} . 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.57 . 
 
 In  Proposition   3.54 , the condition that  u u  and  v v  are suspended over  H H  is not artificial.
Consider the map  δ : A ↦ A / u ​ v \delta:A\mapsto A/_{uv}  from the acyclic orientations of  H H  to the acyclic orientations of  H / u ​ v H/_{uv} .
If all common neighbors of  u , v u,v  are of degree  2 2 , then the proof of  Proposition   3.54  essentially relies on the fact that the fibers  δ − 1 ​ ( a ) \delta^{-1}(a)  for  a ∈ 𝒜 𝒪 ( H / u ​ v ) a\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H/_{uv})  are well-behaved:
they have the same size, and the rank of the pre-images can be controlled easily.
However, if there exists a common neighbor to  u , v u,v  which is connected to other nodes of  H H  (possibly to other common neighbors of  u u  and  v v ), then nothing guarantees that the fibers of  δ \delta  are well-behaved. 
 
 
 As illustrated in  Figure   4 , is not even true in general that the polynomial  Ψ ( D ~ / u ​ v ; t ) \Psi(\tilde{D}/_{uv};t)  divides the polynomial  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t) . 
 
 
 
 Figure 4 .  A digraph  D D  (left) and its contraction on  u , v u,v  (right), together with their respective (partial) acyclic polynomials.
Both polynomials are factorized (over  ℤ \mathbb{Z} ), so the right one does not divide the left one. 
 
 
 We now turn our attention to deletion: in what follows, the digraphs  D D  and  D ′ D^{\prime}  should be thought as deletions from the digraph  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime} .
The result is easier to understand than the contraction of  Proposition   3.54  but only allows us to express  partial  acyclic polynomials  Ψ u → v \Psi^{u\to v}  using  partial  acyclic polynomials of smaller digraphs. 
 
 
 Proposition 3.58 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a graph with a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , and  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  an orientation of  G G .
Then 
 

 
 
 Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = ( Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ′ , t ) + Ψ u → v ​ ( D ′ , t ) ) ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) = ( Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , t ) − t | E | ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ′ , 1 t ) ) ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) . \begin{array}[]{rcl}\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)&=&\bigl(\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D^{\prime};t)+\Psi^{u\to v}(D^{\prime};t)\bigr)\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D;t)\\
&=&\bigl(\Psi(D^{\prime};t)-t^{|E|}\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D^{\prime};\tfrac{1}{t})\bigr)\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D;t).\end{array} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The set of acyclic orientations  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  of  G = D ⊔ D ′ G=D\sqcup D^{\prime}  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 1 A(u,v)=1  is in bijection with the set of pairs  ( A , A ′ ) ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) (A,A^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 1 A(u,v)=1  and  A ′ ​ ( u , v ) ≠ − 1 A^{\prime}(u,v)\neq-1 .
Consequently, since  t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = t rk D ⁡ A ⋅ t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=t^{\rank_{D}A}\cdot t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}} : 
 

 
 
 Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) = ∑ A ⊔ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 1 t rk D ⊔ D ′ ⁡ A ⊔ A ′ = ( ∑ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ′ ) A ′ ​ ( u , v ) ∈ { 0 , 1 } t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ ) ⋅ ( ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ) A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 1 t rk D ⁡ A ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\sqcup A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)\\
A(u,v)=1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D\sqcup D^{\prime}}A\sqcup A^{\prime}}=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D^{\prime})\\
A^{\prime}(u,v)\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}}\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D)\\
A(u,v)=1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D}A}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 The rightmost parenthesis is  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t) , while the left parenthesis is 
 

 
 
 ∑ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ′ ) A ′ ​ ( u , v ) = 0 t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ + ∑ A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( D ′ ) A ′ ​ ( u , v ) = 1 t rk D ′ ⁡ A ′ = Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ′ , t ) + Ψ u → v ​ ( D ′ , t ) . \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D^{\prime})\\
A^{\prime}(u,v)=0\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(D^{\prime})\\
A^{\prime}(u,v)=1\end{subarray}}t^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A^{\prime}}=\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D^{\prime};t)+\Psi^{u\to v}(D^{\prime};t). 
 
 
 Using  Proposition   3.43 , we get the second formula.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.59 . 
 
 Sadly,  Propositions   3.54  and  3.58  do not allow us to compute  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  for any digraph  D D  via a deletion-contraction recursion.
Not only is the choice of the bipartition  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  slightly restrictive, but also, although the formula for  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D;t)  only involves  Ψ \Psi  of smaller digraphs, the formula for  Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};t)  involves some  Ψ u → v \Psi^{u\to v}  for smaller digraphs, and we provide no method (except exhaustive enumeration of acyclic orientations) to compute such polynomials.
Note that the polynomial  Ψ u → v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;t)  cannot be recovered knowing  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  and  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D , t ) \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;t)  since it is not palindromic. 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.60 . 
 
 In  [ 40 ] , Stanley proved that  ψ ( G ) = ψ ( G / u ​ v ) + ψ ( G ∖ u v ) \psi(G)=\psi(G/_{uv})+\psi(G\smallsetminus uv)  for all graphs  G G  and all edges  u ​ v uv  of  G G , where  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) \psi(G)=\Psi(D;1)  for any orientation  D D  of  G G .
Considering the bipartition of edges  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  where  H ′ H^{\prime}  is a single edge between  u u  and  v v , one can almost recover Stanley’s deletion-contraction recursion.
Indeed, if  u , v u,v  have no common neighbors in  G G , then evaluating  Propositions   3.43 ,  3.54  and  3.58  at  t = 1 t=1  yields: 
 

 
 
 Ψ ⁡ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 ) = Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 ) + 2 ⋅ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , 1 ) = Ψ ( D ′ ; 1 ) ⋅ Ψ ( D / u ​ v ; 1 ) + 2 ⋅ ( Ψ u ↮ v ( D ′ ; 1 ) + Ψ u → v ( D ′ ; 1 ) ) ⋅ Ψ u → v ( D ; 1 ) \begin{array}[]{rcl}\Psi(D\sqcup D^{\prime};1)&=&\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};1)+2\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};1)\\
&=&\Psi(D^{\prime};1)\cdot\Psi(D/_{uv};1)+2\cdot\bigl(\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D^{\prime};1)+\Psi^{u\to v}(D^{\prime};1)\bigr)\cdot\Psi^{u\to v}(D;1)\end{array} 
 
 
 where  Ψ ⁡ ( D ′ , 1 ) = 2 \Psi(D^{\prime};1)=2 ,  Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ′ , 1 ) = 0 \Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D^{\prime};1)=0 , and  Ψ u → v ​ ( D ′ , 1 ) = 1 \Psi^{u\to v}(D^{\prime};1)=1 , since  D ′ D^{\prime}  is only an (oriented) edge.
Now, applying  Propositions   3.43  and  3.54  to  D D  itself gives:
 Ψ u → v ( D ; 1 ) = 1 2 Ψ ( D ; 1 ) − 1 2 Ψ u ↮ v ( D ; 1 ) = 1 2 Ψ ( D ; 1 ) − 1 2 Ψ ( D / u ​ v ; 1 ) \Psi^{u\to v}(D;1)=\frac{1}{2}\Psi(D;1)-\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D;1)=\frac{1}{2}\Psi(D;1)-\frac{1}{2}\Psi(D/_{uv};1) .
Substituting in the above, we obtain:
 Ψ ( D ⊔ D ′ ; 1 ) = Ψ ( D / u ​ v ; 1 ) + Ψ ( D ; 1 ) \Psi(D\sqcup D^{\prime};1)=\Psi(D/_{uv};1)+\Psi(D;1) , which is precisely Stanley’s recursion, as desired. 
 
 
 However, as our  Proposition   3.54  does not apply if  u u  and  v v  are not suspended over  H H , one still needs to prove Stanley’s recursion in these cases, and cannot recover it from the deletion-contraction phenomenon that we presented. 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.3.  Acyclic signature 

 
 We use the knowledge on the acyclic polynomial to compute the acyclic signature  σ ⁡ ( D ) ≔ Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) \sigma(D)\coloneqq\Psi(D;-1)  of (di)graphs.
The actual value of the signature is not very important for the problem of Hamiltonicity of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) :
we only care about whether  σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)  is  0 0  or not.
Indeed, by the parity argument (see  [ 37 ]  or  Lemma   2.9 ), if  σ ⁡ ( D ) ≠ 0 \sigma(D)\neq 0  for some orientation  D D  or  G G , then  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
