The reciprocal is false, yet it seems non-trivial to produce an interesting  2 2 -connected graph with an even number of edges that would help to understand how far from characterizing  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity the acyclic signature is. 
 
 
 3.3.1.  Definition for (di)graphs, first examples and first properties 

 
 We have seen in  Figure   3  that, up to a sign, the signature  σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)  for  D D  an orientation of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  does not depend on  D D .
This is actually a general fact: 
 
 
 Proposition 3.61 . 
 
 Let  D D  and  D ′ D^{\prime}  be two orientations of a graph  G G , then:  σ ⁡ ( D ′ ) = ( − 1 ) rk D ⁡ D ′ ⋅ σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D^{\prime})=(-1)^{\rank_{D}D^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma(D) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Suppose that  D ′ = D Y D^{\prime}=D^{Y}  for some  Y ⊆ 2 E Y\subseteq 2^{E} , and let  A = D X A=D^{X}  be another orientation of  G G .
Thus, we have  A = ( D ′ ) X ​ △ ​ Y A=(D^{\prime})^{X\triangle Y}  where  △ \triangle  denotes the symmetric difference operator.
This implies that  rk D ′ ⁡ A = | X ​ △ ​ Y | = | X | + | Y ​ | − 2 | ​ X ∩ Y | = rk D ⁡ D ′ + rk D ⁡ A − 2 ​ | X ∩ Y | \rank_{D^{\prime}}A=|X\triangle Y|=|X|+|Y|-2|X\cap Y|=\rank_{D}D^{\prime}+\rank_{D}A-2|X\cap Y|  which has the same parity as  rk D ⁡ D ′ + rk D ⁡ A \rank_{D}D^{\prime}+\rank_{D}A ,  i.e.   ( − 1 ) rk D ′ ⁡ A = ( − 1 ) rk D ⁡ D ′ ⋅ ( − 1 ) rk D ⁡ A (-1)^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A}=(-1)^{\rank_{D}D^{\prime}}\cdot(-1)^{\rank_{D}A} .
Consequently: 
 

 
 
 σ ⁡ ( D ′ ) = ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ( − 1 ) rk D ′ ⁡ A = ( − 1 ) rk D ⁡ D ′ ⋅ ∑ A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) ( − 1 ) rk D ⁡ A = ( − 1 ) rk D ⁡ D ′ ⋅ σ ⁡ ( D ) . ∎ \sigma(D^{\prime})=\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}(-1)^{\rank_{D^{\prime}}A}=(-1)^{\rank_{D}D^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)}(-1)^{\rank_{D}A}=(-1)^{\rank_{D}D^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma(D).~\qed 
 
 
 
 
 
 In view of  Proposition   3.61 , we define  σ ⁡ ( G ) {\sigma(G)}  to be the absolute value of  σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)  for any orientation  D D  of the graph  G G .
We can directly use the explicit formulas given in  Section   3.2  for  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  to obtain formulas for  σ ⁡ ( G ) \sigma(G) , see  Table   1 . 
 
 
 
 3.3.2.  Towards a deletion-contraction algorithm 

 
 Looking at the formulas we developed for  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  in  Section   3.2 , one can see that some of them simplify drastically when evaluated at  t = 1 t=1 :
this allows for recovering many key properties of the number of acyclic orientations  ψ ⁡ ( G ) = Ψ ⁡ ( D , 1 ) \psi(G)=\Psi(D;1) , as we did in  Remark   3.60 .
Likewise, some of these formulas also simplify when evaluated at  t = − 1 t=-1 , yielding properties of the acyclic signature  σ ⁡ ( G ) = | Ψ ⁡ ( D , − 1 ) | \sigma(G)=\bigl|\Psi(D;-1)\bigr| . 
 
 
 Corollary 3.62 . 
 
 If  G G  has an odd number of edges, then  σ ⁡ ( G ) = 0 \sigma(G)=0 . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By  Corollary   3.44 ,  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  is palindromic of length  | E | |E|  for any orientation  D D  of  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) ,  i.e.   Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) = t | E | ​ Ψ ​ ( D , 1 t ) \Psi(D;t)=t^{|E|}\Psi(D;\tfrac{1}{t}) .
Evaluating at  t = − 1 t=-1  for  | E | |E|  odd yields  σ ⁡ ( D ) = − σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)=-\sigma(D) , so  σ ⁡ ( D ) = 0 \sigma(D)=0 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 For the case of graphs with an even number of edges, we can use the decomposition of  Proposition   3.43  and the deletion-contraction phenomenon described in  Propositions   3.54  and  3.58 , but we need to work with  σ ⁡ ( D ) \sigma(D)  instead of  σ ⁡ ( G ) \sigma(G)  to keep track of signs. 
 
 
 Definition 3.63 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a graph with a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , and  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  an orientation of  G G .
We define the  partial signatures  as  σ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) ≔ Ψ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , − 1 ) {\sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime})}\coloneqq\Psi^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};-1)  and  σ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) ≔ Ψ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ , − 1 ) {\sigma^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime})}\coloneqq\Psi^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime};-1) . 
 
 
 
 Theorem 3.64 . 
 
 For  G G  a graph with an even number of edges,
let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a graph with a bipartition of the edges of  G G  such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} , and  D ⊔ D ′ D\sqcup D^{\prime}  an orientation of  G G .
Then: 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 σ ⁡ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) = σ u ↮ v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) + 2 ⋅ σ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) \sigma(D\sqcup D^{\prime})=\sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime})+2\cdot\sigma^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime}) 
 
 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 σ u → v ​ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) = ( σ ⁡ ( D ′ ) − σ u → v ​ ( D ′ ) ) ⋅ σ u → v ​ ( D ) \sigma^{u\to v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime})=\bigl(\sigma(D^{\prime})-\sigma^{u\to v}(D^{\prime})\bigr)\cdot\sigma^{u\to v}(D) 
 
 
 
 
 Moreover, if  u u  and  v v  are suspended over  H H , then with the notations of  Definition   3.52 , and  ε = 1 \varepsilon=1  if  u ​ v ∈ E ⁡ ( H ) uv\in E(H)  and  ε = 0 \varepsilon=0  if  u ​ v ∉ E ⁡ ( H ) uv\notin E(H) , we get: 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 σ u ↮ v ( D ⊔ D ′ ) = ( 1 − ε ) ⋅ ( − 1 ) x ⋅ 2 x + y ⋅ σ ( D ′ ) ⋅ σ ( D ~ / u ​ v ) \sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D\sqcup D^{\prime})=(1-\varepsilon)\cdot(-1)^{x}\cdot 2^{x+y}\cdot\sigma(D^{\prime})\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}/_{uv}) 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Direct applications of  Propositions   3.43 ,  3.54  and  3.58  evaluated at  t = − 1 t=-1  and  | E | |E|  even.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma 3.65 . 
 
 Let  G G  be a graph with nodes  u u  and  v v  which are suspended over  G G .
Then, we have 
 

 
 (3.5) 
 
 σ u → v ( D ) = 1 2 ( σ ( D ) − ( − 1 ) x ⋅ 2 x + y ⋅ σ ( D ~ / u ​ v ) ) . \sigma^{u\to v}(D)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D)-(-1)^{x}\cdot 2^{x+y}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}/_{uv})\bigr)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Write the bipartition of edges  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  where  H ′ H^{\prime}  is the graph with nodes  u , v u,v  and no edge between them, by  Theorem   3.64  ( 3.2 ), we get:
 σ ⁡ ( D ) = σ u ↮ v ​ ( D ) + 2 ⋅ σ u → v ​ ( D ) \sigma(D)=\sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D)+2\cdot\sigma^{u\to v}(D) , so we have  σ u → v ​ ( D ) = 1 2 ​ ( σ ⁡ ( D ) − σ u ↮ v ​ ( D ) ) \sigma^{u\to v}(D)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D)-\sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D)\bigr) .
Besides, by ( 3.4 )
 σ u ↮ v ( D ) = ( − 1 ) x ⋅ 2 x + y ⋅ 1 ⋅ σ ( D ~ / u ​ v ) \sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D)=(-1)^{x}\cdot 2^{x+y}\cdot 1\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}/_{uv}) .
Thus, 
 

 
 
 σ u → v ( D ) = 1 2 ( σ ( D ) − ( − 1 ) x ⋅ 2 x + y ⋅ σ ( D ~ / u ​ v ) ) ∎ \sigma^{u\to v}(D)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D)-(-1)^{x}\cdot 2^{x+y}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}/_{uv})\bigr)~~\qed 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.66   (Algorithm to compute the acyclic signature) . 
 
 Constructing an algorithm to compute the acyclic signature of a graph  G G  is not always possible with our current formulas, but we describe how to do it for certain graphs. 
 
 
 First of all, if  G G  is not connected or not  2 2 -connected  Table   1  indicates that  σ ⁡ ( G ) \sigma(G)  can be written as a product of the signatures of the  2 2 -connected components of  G G .
Secondly, if  G G  has an odd number of edges, then  σ ⁡ ( G ) = 0 \sigma(G)=0  by  Corollary   3.62 . 
 
 
 Now, assume that  G G  is  2 2 -connected with an even number of edges.
Suppose there is a bipartition  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  of its edges such that  N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) = { u , v } N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime})=\{u,v\} ,  | E ⁡ ( H ) | |E(H)|  and  | E ⁡ ( H ′ ) | |E(H^{\prime})|  are both even, and  u u  and  v v  are suspended over  H H , then, combining  Equations   3.2 ,  3.3 ,  3.4  and  3.5  yields: 
 

 
 (3.6) 
 
 σ ⁡ ( D ⊔ D ′ ) = c ⋅ b + 1 2 ⋅ ( c − d ) ⋅ ( a + b ) \sigma(D\sqcup D^{\prime})=c\cdot b+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(c-d)\cdot(a+b) 
 
 
 where, with the notations of  Definition   3.52  for  x , y , D ~ x,y,\tilde{D}  and  x ′ , y ′ , D ′ ~ x^{\prime},y^{\prime},\tilde{D^{\prime}} : 
 

 
 
 a = σ ⁡ ( D ) , b = ( − 1 ) x ⋅ 2 x + y ⋅ σ ( D ~ / u ​ v ) , c = σ ⁡ ( D ′ ) ​ , and  ​ d = ( − 1 ) x ′ ⋅ 2 x ′ + y ′ ⋅ σ ( D ′ ~ / u ​ v ) . \begin{array}[]{rclrclrclrcl}a&=&\sigma(D),~~b&=&(-1)^{x}\cdot 2^{x+y}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}/_{uv}),~~c&=&\sigma(D^{\prime})\text{, and }d&=&(-1)^{x^{\prime}}\cdot 2^{x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D^{\prime}}/_{uv})\end{array}\,. 
 
 
 
 
 Note that  Equation   3.6  expresses the acyclic signature of  G G  using acyclic signatures of smaller (di)graphs, not using any partial signature.
Hence, this is a genuine induction step.
In particular, one can try dealing with the signatures  a a ,  b b ,  c c  and  d d  using the same method, or computing them directly via  Table   1  or by exhaustive enumeration of acyclic orientations if the involved digraphs are tame enough.
Note that the common neighbors of  u u  and  v v  are taken care of in this induction step. 
 
 
 
 
 3.3.3.  Acyclic signature of multipaths 

 
 Definition 3.67 . 
 
 A  path   P ℓ {P_{\ell}}  of  length   ℓ ≥ 1 \ell\geq 1  is a graph composed of  ℓ + 1 \ell+1  nodes   i 1 ​ … , i ℓ + 1 i_{1}\dots,i_{\ell+1} , and edges  i p ​ i p + 1 i_{p}i_{p+1}  for  p ∈ [ ℓ ] p\in[\ell] .
A  multipath  is a graph composed of several (internally) disjoint paths between the same endpoints  u u  and  v v , see  Figure   5  for an illustration.
Precisely, for a sequence of integers  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s})  with  ℓ 1 ≥ 1 \ell_{1}\geq 1  and  ℓ i ≥ 2 \ell_{i}\geq 2  for all  i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 , the  ℓ {\boldsymbol{\ell}} -multipath   P ℓ {P_{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}}  is the graph obtained as the contraction of paths  P ℓ 1 , … , P ℓ s P_{\ell_{1}},\dots,P_{\ell_{s}}  at their endpoints,  i.e.  the graph with nodes  u u ,  v v  and  i 1 , 2 , … , i 1 , ℓ 1 , … , i s , 2 , … , i s , ℓ s i_{1,2},\dots,i_{1,\ell_{1}},\dots,i_{s,2},\dots,i_{s,\ell_{s}} , and edges  u ​ i p , 2 ui_{p,2} ,  i p , ℓ p ​ v i_{p,\ell_{p}}v  for all  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s]  and  i p , m ​ i p , m + 1 i_{p,m}i_{p,m+1}  for all  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s]  and  m ∈ [ 2 , ℓ p ] m\in[2,\ell_{p}] . 
 
 
 We denote  D u → v ℓ {D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}}  the acyclic orientation of  P ℓ P_{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  “from  u u  to  v v ”,  i.e.  with arcs  u ​ i p , 2 ui_{p,2} ,  i p , ℓ p ​ v i_{p,\ell_{p}}v  for all  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s]  and  i p , m ​ i p , m + 1 i_{p,m}i_{p,m+1}  for all  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s]  and  m ∈ [ 2 , ℓ p ] m\in[2,\ell_{p}] . 
 
 
 
 Figure 5 .  
For  ℓ = ( 1 , 2 , 2 , 3 , 3 , 4 , 5 ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(1,2,2,3,3,4,5) , the multipath  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  (left) and its orientation  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}  (right).
By  Theorem   3.69 , since  ∑ i ℓ i \sum_{i}\ell_{i}  is even and some  ℓ i \ell_{i}  are odd, we have  σ ⁡ ( P ℓ ) = − 2 ≠ 0 \sigma(P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}})=-2\neq 0 .
 
 
 
 Example 3.68 . 
 
 The graph  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  is the multipath  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  with  ℓ = ( 2 , 2 , 2 ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(2,2,2) . Orientation  6 6  of  Figure   3  is the orientation  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v} , and we have  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = + 6 = ( − 1 ) 3 + 1 ⋅ ( 2 3 − 2 ) \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})=+6=(-1)^{3+1}\cdot(2^{3}-2) . 
 
 
 
 The goal of this subsection is to compute the acyclic signature of multipaths.
The proof should be read as a showcase of the algorithm of  Remark   3.66 . 
 
 
 Theorem 3.69 . 
 
 Fix any  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s})  with  ℓ 1 ≥ 1 \ell_{1}\geq 1  and  ℓ i ≥ 2 \ell_{i}\geq 2  for all  i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 .
If  ∑ i ℓ i \sum_{i}\ell_{i}  is odd, then  σ ⁡ ( P ℓ ) = 0 \sigma(P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}})=0 .
If  ∑ i ℓ i \sum_{i}\ell_{i}  is even, then denoting  r r  the number of  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s]  such that  ℓ i \ell_{i}  is even, we have: 
 

 
 
 σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = { ( − 1 ) s + 1 ⋅ ( 2 s − 2 )  if  ​ ℓ i ​  is even for all  ​ i ∈ [ s ] ( − 1 ) r ⋅ 2  otherwise . \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(-1)^{s+1}\cdot(2^{s}-2)&\text{ if }\ell_{i}\text{ is even for all }i\in[s]\\
(-1)^{r}\cdot 2&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.\,. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 If  ∑ i ℓ i \sum_{i}\ell_{i}  is odd, then  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  has an odd number of edges, so by  Corollary   3.62 , we get  σ ⁡ ( P ℓ ) = 0 \sigma(P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}})=0 . 
 
 
 Suppose all  ℓ i \ell_{i}  are even for  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] , let  u u  and  v v  be the two common endpoints of the multipath  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}} , and let  ℓ ′ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}) .
Assume by induction on  s s  that  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) = ( − 1 ) s ⋅ ( 2 s − 1 − 2 ) \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})=(-1)^{s}\cdot(2^{s-1}-2) , which holds true for  s = 1 s=1  (where  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  is a path, hence a tree) and  s = 2 s=2  (where  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  is an even cycle) by  Table   1 .
Let  x x  be the number of  i ∈ [ s − 1 ] i\in[s-1]  such that  ℓ i = 2 \ell_{i}=2 .
Fix the bipartition  P ℓ = P ℓ ′ ⊔ P ℓ s P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}=P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}\sqcup P^{\ell_{s}} .
The deletion-contraction argument of  Theorem   3.64  (see also  Remark   3.66 ) gives: 
 

 
 
 σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = σ u ↮ v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) + 2 ⋅ σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) σ u ↮ v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) = ( − 2 ) x ⋅ σ ( D ℓ s u → v ) ⋅ σ ( D ~ ℓ u → v / u ​ v ) σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) = ( σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ s ) − σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ s ) ) ⋅ σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) \begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})&=&\sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})+2\cdot\sigma^{u\to v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})\\
\sigma^{u\not\leftrightarrow v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})&=&(-2)^{x}\cdot\sigma(D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v})\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})\\
\sigma^{u\to v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})&=&\bigl(\sigma(D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v})-\sigma^{u\to v}(D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v})\bigr)\cdot\sigma^{u\to v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})\end{array} 
 
 
 
 
 Since  P ℓ s P^{\ell_{s}}  is a path, it is a tree, so  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ s ) = 0 \sigma(D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v})=0  by  Table   1 .
Moreover, there is a unique acyclic orientation of  P ℓ s P^{\ell_{s}}  which contains a directed path from  u u  to  v v , namely  D u → v ℓ s D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v} , so  Ψ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ s , t ) = 1 \Psi^{u\to v}(D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v};t)=1 , and in particular  σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ s ) = 1 \sigma^{u\to v}(D^{\ell_{s}}_{u\to v})=1 .
It remains to compute  σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) \sigma^{u\to v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})  which can be done using  Lemma   3.65  (where  y = 0 y=0  by construction) because  u u  and  v v  have no common neighbors in  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  except on paths of length  2 2 :
 σ u → v ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) = 1 2 ( σ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) − ( − 2 ) x ⋅ σ ( D ~ u → v ℓ ′ / u ​ v ) ) \sigma^{u\to v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})-(-2)^{x}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})\bigr) . 
 
 
 The graph  D ~ ℓ u → v / u ​ v \tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}/_{uv}  is a  1 1 -sum of several cycles directed in a cyclic way of lengths  ℓ j \ell_{j}  for  j ∈ [ s − 1 ] j\in[s-1]  such that  ℓ j ≠ 2 \ell_{j}\neq 2 :
these are  s − 1 − x s-1-x  even cycles.
Thus  σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) = ( − 2 ) s − 1 − x \sigma^{u\to v}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})=(-2)^{s-1-x}  by  Table   1 .
Putting everything back together, we get 
 

 
 
 σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = 0 + ( 0 − 1 ) ⋅ 1 2 ​ ( σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) − ( − 2 ) x ⋅ ( − 2 ) s − 1 − x ) . \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})=0+(0-1)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})-(-2)^{x}\cdot(-2)^{s-1-x}\bigr). 
 
 
 As we have assumed by induction that  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) = ( − 1 ) s ⋅ ( 2 s − 1 − 2 ) \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})=(-1)^{s}\cdot(2^{s-1}-2) , we indeed get  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = ( − 1 ) s + 1 ⋅ ( 2 s − 2 ) \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})=(-1)^{s+1}\cdot(2^{s}-2)  if  ℓ i \ell_{i}  is even for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] . 
 
 
 Last but not least, if there is some  j ∈ [ s ] j\in[s]  such that  ℓ j \ell_{j}  is odd, then, since  ∑ i ℓ i \sum_{i}\ell_{i}  is even, it implies that there exists another index  k ∈ [ s ] k\in[s] ,  k ≠ j k\neq j  such that  ℓ k \ell_{k}  is odd.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that  ℓ s − 1 \ell_{s-1}  and  ℓ s \ell_{s}  are odd, and if there exists some  j ∈ [ s ] j\in[s]  such that  ℓ j = 1 \ell_{j}=1  then  ℓ s = 1 \ell_{s}=1 .
We denote  ℓ ′ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s − 2 ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s-2}) .
We consider the bipartition  P ℓ = P ℓ ′ ⊔ P ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}=P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}\sqcup P^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})} , and compute the corresponding (partial) signatures in a similar manner.
Note that  D u → v ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) D^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v}  is a cycle of even length whose orientation differs on the direction of an odd number of arcs (namely  ℓ s \ell_{s} ) from the cyclic orientation, so  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) ) = + 2 \sigma(D^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v})=+2 .
Besides,  D ~ ℓ ′ u → v / u ​ v \tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv}  is a  1 1 -sum of cycles directed in a cyclic way:
if there is  j ∈ [ s − 2 ] j\in[s-2]  such that  ℓ j \ell_{j}  is odd, then one of these cycles is of odd length, so  σ ( D ~ u → v ℓ ′ / u ​ v ) = 0 \sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})=0 , otherwise,  σ ( D ~ u → v ℓ ′ / u ​ v ) = ( − 2 ) s − x − 2 \sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})=(-2)^{s-x-2} .
We could compute  σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) ) \sigma^{u\to v}(D^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v})  directly, but note that  u u  and  v v  have no common neighbors in  D u → v ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) D^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v} , so we can instead apply  Lemma   3.65 , where  D ~ ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) u → v / u ​ v \tilde{D}^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v}/_{uv}  is a  1 1 -sum of two odd cycles, so  σ ( D ~ u → v ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) / u ​ v ) = 0 \sigma(\tilde{D}^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v}/_{uv})=0 .
We now have all the pieces: 
 

 
 
 σ u → v ​ ( D u → v ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) ) = 1 2 ( σ ( D ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) u → v ) − σ ( D ~ ( ℓ s − 1 , ℓ s ) u → v / u ​ v ) ) = 1 2 ⋅ ( 2 − 0 ) = 1 σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = ( − 2 ) x ⋅ 2 ⋅ σ ( D ~ ℓ ′ u → v / u ​ v ) + 2 ⋅ ( 2 − 1 ) ⋅ 1 2 ( σ ( D ℓ ′ u → v ) − ( − 2 ) x ⋅ σ ( D ~ ℓ ′ u → v / u ​ v ) ) = ( − 2 ) x ⋅ σ ( D ~ ℓ ′ u → v / u ​ v ) + σ ( D ℓ ′ u → v ) \begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma^{u\to v}(D^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v})&=&\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v})-\sigma(\tilde{D}^{(\ell_{s-1},\ell_{s})}_{u\to v}/_{uv})\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(2-0)=1\\
\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})&=&(-2)^{x}\cdot 2\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})+2\cdot\bigl(2-1\bigr)\cdot\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})-(-2)^{x}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})\bigr)\\
&=&(-2)^{x}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})+\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})\end{array} 
 
 
 To finish, if  ℓ ′ {\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}  contains only even elements, then  r = s − 2 r=s-2 ,  σ ( D ~ u → v ℓ ′ / u ​ v ) = ( − 2 ) s − x − 2 = ( − 2 ) r − x \sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})=(-2)^{s-x-2}=(-2)^{r-x} , and  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) = ( − 1 ) r + 1 ​ ( 2 r − 2 ) \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})=(-1)^{r+1}(2^{r}-2)  by induction:
hence  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = ( − 2 ) r + ( − 1 ) r + 1 ​ ( 2 r − 2 ) = ( − 1 ) r ⋅ 2 \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})=(-2)^{r}+(-1)^{r+1}(2^{r}-2)=(-1)^{r}\cdot 2 .
If  ℓ ′ {\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}  contains some odd elements, then  σ ( D ~ u → v ℓ ′ / u ​ v ) = 0 \sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})=0 , and  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ′ ) = ( − 1 ) r ⋅ 2 \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}^{\prime}}_{u\to v})=(-1)^{r}\cdot 2  by induction:
hence  σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) = ( − 1 ) r ⋅ 2 \sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})=(-1)^{r}\cdot 2 .
This concludes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 3.70 . 
 
 For any  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s})  with  ℓ 1 ≥ 1 \ell_{1}\geq 1  and  ℓ i ≥ 2 \ell_{i}\geq 2  for all  i ≥ 2 i\geq 2 , if  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then  ∑ i = 1 s ℓ i \sum_{i=1}^{s}\ell_{i}  is odd. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Direct consequence of  Theorems   3.69  and  2.9 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.71 . 
 
 One could decide whether the acyclic signature of multipaths is  0 0  or not via a more direct count of the number of acyclic orientations of multipaths.
We prefer the proof of  Theorem   3.69  since it emphasizes the properties of the acyclic signature and hints at ways to compute it for larger families of graphs, but the interested reader may have a look at Appendix  C . 
 
 
 
 Remark 3.72 . 
 
 Let  H H  be a graph with two nodes  u , v u,v , and  H ′ = P ℓ H^{\prime}=P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  a multipath with endpoints  u , v u,v  such that  ∑ i ℓ i \sum_{i}\ell_{i}  is even.
One can deduce the acyclic signature of  H ⊔ H ′ H\sqcup H^{\prime}  from the acyclic signature of  H H  if  H H  has an even number of edges, and  u u  and  v v  do not have a common neighbor in  H H  except for nodes on induced paths of length  2 2  between  u u  and  v v .
Indeed, in this context, we can use  Equation   3.6 , where  c = σ ⁡ ( D u → v ℓ ) c=\sigma(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})  is known from  Theorem   3.69  and  d = ( − 2 ) x ′ ⋅ σ ( D ~ u → v ℓ / u ​ v ) d=(-2)^{x^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma(\tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}/_{uv})  is equal to  0 0  if there is some  j ∈ [ s ] j\in[s]  such that  ℓ j \ell_{j}  is odd, and is equal to  ( − 2 ) s (-2)^{s}  otherwise (since  D ~ ℓ u → v / u ​ v \tilde{D}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}/_{uv}  is a  1 1 -sum of cycles directed in a cyclic way). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity: (multi)path gluing, and simplicial nodes 

 
 Our goal in this section is to construct a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  where  G G  is obtained from an  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph  H H  by adding a multipath between two nodes of  H H . 
 
 
 We give an overview of the strategy for proving Hamiltonicity of graphs in a constructive way, in  Section   4.1 .
We begin with a key element in the proof of Hamiltonicity of the graph of regions of supersolvable arrangements in  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII ] . 
 
 
 Definition 4.1 . 
 
 Denoting  P ℓ P_{\ell}  the path on  ℓ \ell  nodes with endpoints  u u  and  v v  for  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 , a graph  G G  is  ℓ \ell -suspended  over a graph  H = ( N , E ) H=(N,E)  if  G G  is a spanning graph of the Cartesian product  H × P ℓ H\times P_{\ell} , such that  G ⁡ [ { x } × P ℓ ] ≃ P ℓ G[\{x\}\times P_{\ell}]\simeq P_{\ell}  for all  x ∈ N x\in N  and  G ⁡ [ N × { u } ] ≃ G ⁡ [ N × { v } ] ≃ H G[N\times\{u\}]\simeq G[N\times\{v\}]\simeq H . 
 
 
 
 This graph  G G  is well-behaved in terms of Hamiltonicity if the graph  H H  was Hamiltonian (see  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII , Lemma 15] ).
This is illustrated in  Figure   6 . 
 
 
 Figure 6 .  (Left) The Cartesian product  P 4 × H P_{4}\times H  where the graph  H H  in  cyan .
(Right) An  4 4 -suspended graph  G G  over  H H .
In  red , a Hamiltonian cycle in  G G .
In general, there might be some nodes missing in the copies of  H H  (see the nodes labeled as a red star  ⋆ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\star}  in a blue circle  ∘ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\circ}  in  Figure   7 ) and thus, it is inevitable to use some of the black edges in the right picture to construct a Hamiltonian cycle. 
 
 
 The proof for supersolvable arrangements takes advantage of the fact that if we add new hyperplanes according to the modular chain witnessing supersovability, we subdivide each of the existing chambers in a very regular way, identical to all chambers.
Thus, we only need to specify how to walk the new chambers subdividing the existing ones, and then zigzag through the existing chambers through the graphic suspension. 
 
 
 We want to analyze which operations on graphs we can perform, such that if the base graph is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, the resulting graph is also  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. This means that we generalize this approach of graphic suspension to a case where the two graphs  G ⁡ [ N × { 0 } ] G[N\times\{0\}]  and  G ⁡ [ N × { ℓ } ] G[N\times\{\ell\}]  need not be copies of the same graph, but rather instances of graphs of the subdivisions of neighboring chambers of the original arrangement through the new hyperplanes. In this case, we find that it can be necessary to use the edges between two graphs  G ⁡ [ { v } × i ] G[\{v\}\times i]  and  G ⁡ [ { w } × i ] G[\{w\}\times i] , where  { v , w } \{v,w\}  is an edge in the original graph  H H . 
 
 
 For our problem, what the authors of  [ BrennerCardinalMcConvilleMerinoMutze25-CombinatorialGenerationVII ]  proved implies that if  G G  is obtained from  H H  by adding a simplicial node on a clique of size  b b , then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is  b b -suspended over  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) , so the zigzag method guarantees that if  H H  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then  G G  is also  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
We will discuss this idea in  Section   4.3  since we need more conditions on the nodes and certain sequences associated to them. 
 
 
 The question we are concerned with is what to do when  G G  is obtained from  H H  by an operation more complicated than adding a simplicial node, especially when  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is not suspended over  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) .
We propose two generalizations of the zigzag method, namely  Corollaries   4.4  and  4.5 .
Both are based on the following core idea:
if  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  is a bipartition of the edges of  G G , where  H ′ H^{\prime}  shall be thought of as the edges we add to  H H , then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is an induced subgraph of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  (see  Lemma   3.40 ).
For each acyclic orientation  A A  of  H H , we consider the graph of acyclic orientations of  G G  of the form  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  for all admissible  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}) :
if we find a Hamiltonian path  P A P_{A}  through these acyclic orientations and if we manage to guarantee that we can “string together” the  ( P A , A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) ) (P_{A}~;~A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H)) , then we would have a Hamiltonian path (or cycle) of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
This is the idea of  Theorem   4.5 . 
 
 
 Yet, such a straightforward construction is usually not possible.
Therefore, instead of just one single orientation, we consider entire sequences  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  of adjacent acyclic orientations of  H H  ( i.e.  directed paths in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) ), and find a Hamiltonian path in the subgraph of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  induced by all the acyclic orientations of the form  A i ⊔ A ′ A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}  for  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s]  and  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}) .
We call such a Hamiltonian path a  replacing pattern  for  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s}) .
To be able to “string together” these replacing patterns in order to construct a Hamiltonian path (or cycle) for  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) , we develop a theory of  lacing  patterns, leading to  Propositions   4.3  and  4.4 . 
 
 
 We formalize these ideas, and apply them to gluing multipaths in  Sections   4.2.2 ,  4.2.1  and  4.2.3 . 
 
 
 4.1.  How to lace patterns 

 
 Recall from  Definition   3.39  that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) A(u,v)  is the  direction  induced by the acyclic orientation  A A  on the nodes  u , v u,v  ( i.e.   ± 1 \pm 1  if there is a directed path from  u u  to  v v  or from  v v  to  u u  in  A A , and  0 0  otherwise), and that we denote  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) ≔ ( A 1 ​ ( u , v ) , … , A r ​ ( u , v ) ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)\coloneqq\bigl(A_{1}(u,v),\dots,A_{r}(u,v)\bigr)  for a sequence of acyclic orientations  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A r ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{r}) . 
 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of the graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) .
We have seen in  Lemma   3.40  that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is the subgraph of the Cartesian product  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  induced on the pairs of orientations  ( A , A ′ ) (A,A^{\prime})  with  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H)  and  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) ⋅ A ′ ​ ( u , v ) ≠ − 1 A(u,v)\cdot A^{\prime}(u,v)\neq-1  for all  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N .
Hence, for an acyclic orientation  A A  of  H H , we denote by  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A ) {{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A)}  the set of acyclic orientations  A ′ A^{\prime}  of  H ′ H^{\prime}  such that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) ⋅ A ′ ​ ( u , v ) ≠ − 1 A(u,v)\cdot A^{\prime}(u,v)\neq-1  for all  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N . 
 
 
 Let  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  be a directed path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) .
We denote by  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | 𝑨 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|{\boldsymbol{A}})  the subgraph of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  induced on all the orientations  A j ⊔ A k ′ A_{j}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{k}  of  G G  for  A k ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A j ) A^{\prime}_{k}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{j})  with  1 ≤ j ≤ s 1\leq j\leq s . 
 
 
 Definition 4.2 . 
 
 A  replacing pattern   𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  for  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a (directed) Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | 𝑨 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|{\boldsymbol{A}})  that starts at some  A 1 ⊔ A ′ A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}  for some  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1})  and ends at some  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  for some  B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s}) . 
 
 
 
 We denote by  ω i 𝑷 {\omega_{i}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}}  the total order on  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})  ( i.e.  the bijection from  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})  to  [ m ] [m]  with  m ≔ | 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) | m\coloneqq|{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})| ) induced by the replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} , that is  ω i 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ≤ ω i 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) \omega_{i}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(A^{\prime})\leq\omega_{i}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(B^{\prime})  if  A i ⊔ A ′ A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}  appears before  A i ⊔ B ′ A_{i}\sqcup B^{\prime}  in the directed path  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} . 
 
 
 For a replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  on  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  and  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s] , we define the  consecutive locus   cons i ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) {\cons_{i}({\boldsymbol{P}})}  to be the set of indices  j j  such that for the orientation  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})  with  ω i 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) = j \omega_{i}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(A^{\prime})=j , it holds that the node in  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  after  A i ⊔ A ′ A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}  is of the form  A i ⊔ B ′ A_{i}\sqcup B^{\prime}  for some  B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i}) . 
 
 
 Denoting  m ≔ | 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) | m\coloneqq|{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s})| , two replacing patterns  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  on  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  on  ( A s , … , A t ) (A_{s},\dots,A_{t})   can lace  if  ω s 𝑷 = ω s 𝑸 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}=\omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}  and  cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) ∪ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑸 ) = [ m ] \cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}})\cup\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{Q}})=[m] .
If they can lace,
we write  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  as the concatenation of directed paths  𝑷 0 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝑷 m − 1 {{\boldsymbol{P}}_{0}}\cup\dots\cup{{\boldsymbol{P}}_{m-1}}  where each  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  is the directed sub-path from the node  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  with  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) = j \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(A^{\prime})=j  to the node  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  with  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = j + 1 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(B^{\prime})=j+1  (by convention,  𝑷 0 {\boldsymbol{P}}_{0}  starts at the starting node of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} ).
Similarly, we write  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  as the concatenation of directed paths  𝑸 1 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝑸 m {{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{1}}\cup\dots\cup{{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{m}}  where each  𝑸 j {\boldsymbol{Q}}_{j}  is the directed sub-path from the node  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  with  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( A ′ ) = j \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}(A^{\prime})=j  to the node  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  with  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( B ′ ) = j + 1 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}(B^{\prime})=j+1  (by convention,  𝑸 m {\boldsymbol{Q}}_{m}  ends at the ending node of  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}} ).
Then, the  lacing  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}}  is the directed path obtained as the concatenation of  𝑷 0 ∪ 𝑿 1 ∪ ⋯ ∪ 𝑿 m − 1 ∪ 𝑸 m {\boldsymbol{P}}_{0}\cup{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1}\cup\dots\cup{\boldsymbol{X}}_{m-1}\cup{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{m}  where: 
 

 
 
 𝑿 j = { 𝑷 j  if  ​ j ∈ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑸 ) 𝑸 j  otherwise . {\boldsymbol{X}}_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}{\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}&\text{ if }j\in\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{Q}})\\
{\boldsymbol{Q}}_{j}&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.. 
 
 
 Note that the condition  cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) ∪ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑸 ) = [ m ] \cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}})\cup\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{Q}})=[m]  ensures that the “otherwise” case implies  j ∈ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) j\in\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}}) . 
 
 
 Proposition 4.3 . 
 
 Let  𝐏 {\boldsymbol{P}}  (resp.  𝐐 {\boldsymbol{Q}} ) be a replacing pattern for the subsequence  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  (resp.  ( A s , … , A t ) (A_{s},\dots,A_{t}) ).
If  𝐏 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝐐 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  can lace, then their lacing  𝐏 ∧ 𝐐 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}}  is a replacing pattern for  ( A 1 , … , A t ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{t}) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  can lace, we have  ω s 𝑷 = ω s 𝑸 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}=\omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}} , so  𝑿 j {\boldsymbol{X}}_{j}  and  𝑿 j + 1 {\boldsymbol{X}}_{j+1}  meet on the node  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  with  ω s ​ 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) = j + 1 = ω s 𝑸 ​ ( A ′ ) \omega_{s}{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(A^{\prime})=j+1=\omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}(A^{\prime}) .
Hence, by construction of  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}} , it is a directed path inside  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … , A s , … , A t ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots,A_{s},\dots,A_{t}) .
Moreover,  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}}  starts at some  A 1 ⊔ A ′ A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}  for some  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1})  (because its starting node is the same as the one of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} ), and ends at some  A t ⊔ B ′ A_{t}\sqcup B^{\prime}  for some  B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A t ) B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{t})  (because its ending node is the same as the one of  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}} ).
Hence, it is enough to prove that all nodes of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … , A s , … , A t ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots,A_{s},\dots,A_{t})  appear in  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}} . 
 
 
 We use the notations presented in the definition of the lacing.
Consider the restriction of  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}}  to  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … , A s ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots,A_{s}) : by construction, if  𝑷 j ⊄ 𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}\not\subset{\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}} , then  j ∈ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) j\in\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}}) , so  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  is composed of a single edge from  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  to  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  for some  A ′ , B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s}) .
Note that these two nodes  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  to  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  are in the path  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}}  since they belong to  𝑸 j {\boldsymbol{Q}}_{j} , only the edge is not there.
Hence, all the nodes appearing in  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  (resp.  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}} ) also appear in  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}} .
As  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  (resp.  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}} ) is a Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … , A s ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  (resp. in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s , … , A t ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s},\dots,A_{t}) ), we indeed get that  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}}  is a Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … , A s , … , A t ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots,A_{s},\dots,A_{t}) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Finally, a replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  for a directed cycle  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  with  A s = A 1 A_{s}=A_{1}   can self-lace  if  cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) = { 1 , … , | 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) | } \cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}})=\{1,\dots,|{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s})|\}  and for the orientation  A ∘ ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) A^{\prime}_{\circ}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s})  such that  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ∘ ′ ) = 1 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(A^{\prime}_{\circ})=1  we also have  ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( A ∘ ′ ) = 1 \omega_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}(A^{\prime}_{\circ})=1 .
The  self-lacing  of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} , denoted  ∧ 𝑷 {\wedge{\boldsymbol{P}}} , is defined as  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  without its edges from  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  to  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  for some  A ′ , B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s}) . 
 
 
 Corollary 4.4 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of  G G .
If  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  and  𝐏 {\boldsymbol{P}}  a replacing pattern for  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  that can self-lace, then  ∧ 𝐏 \wedge{\boldsymbol{P}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  ω s 𝑷 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}  and  ω 1 𝑷 \omega_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}  have the same first element  A ∘ ′ A^{\prime}_{\circ} , the first node of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  is equal to the last node of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  without its edges from  A s ⊔ A ′ A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}  to  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  for some  A ′ , B ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) A^{\prime},B^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s}) .
Hence  ∧ 𝑷 \wedge{\boldsymbol{P}}  is a (directed) cycle.
Moreover, all the nodes of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … ​ A s − 1 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots A_{s-1})  appear in  ∧ 𝑷 \wedge{\boldsymbol{P}} , so because  A 1 = A s A_{1}=A_{s} , the cycle  ∧ 𝑷 \wedge{\boldsymbol{P}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 One can generalize  Propositions   4.3  and  4.4  in order to weaken their hypotheses.
As it makes the hypotheses less concise and handy, we keep both as stated above, for the more general statement see Appendix  A.1 . 
 
 
 Theorem 4.5 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of  G G .
Suppose that there exists  X ′ , Y ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  and a Hamiltonian path  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  such that: 
 
 •  
 
 X ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) X^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})  and  Y ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})  for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s]  (equivalently,  X ′ ​ ( u , v ) = Y ′ ​ ( u , v ) = 0 X^{\prime}(u,v)=Y^{\prime}(u,v)=0  for all nodes  u , v u,v  connected in  H H ), and 
 
 •  
 
 One can split the sequence  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  into  splitting subsequences   𝑨 j ≔ ( A i j , A i j + 1 , … , A i j + 1 − 1 ) {\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}\coloneqq(A_{i_{j}},A_{i_{j}+1},\dots,A_{i_{j+1}-1})  for  j ∈ [ r ] j\in[r]  with  i 1 = 1 i_{1}=1  and  i r − 1 = s i_{r}-1=s  such that for each  j ∈ [ r ] j\in[r] , the sequence  𝑨 j {\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}  admits either: 
 
 –  
 
 a replacing pattern which starts at  A i j ⊔ A j ′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup A_{j}^{\prime}  and ends at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ B j ′ A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup B_{j}^{\prime}  either for  { A j ′ , B j ′ } = { X ′ , Y ′ } \{A_{j}^{\prime},B_{j}^{\prime}\}=\{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}\} , or 
 
 –  
 
 two replacing patterns which start at  A i j ⊔ A j ′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup A_{j}^{\prime}  and end at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ B j ′ A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup B_{j}^{\prime} , one for  A j ′ = B j ′ = X ′ A_{j}^{\prime}=B_{j}^{\prime}=X^{\prime}  and one for  A j ′ = B j ′ = Y ′ A_{j}^{\prime}=B_{j}^{\prime}=Y^{\prime} , 
 
 
 
 
 then there exists a Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  obtained as a concatenation of some of these replacing patterns. 
 
 
 Moreover, if  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) , and  # ⁡ { j ∈ [ r ] ; A j ′ ≠ B j ′ } \#\{j\in[r]~;~A^{\prime}_{j}\neq B^{\prime}_{j}\}  is even, then  G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 We will concatenate replacing patterns.
Note that one can reverse the direction of every arc in a replacing pattern to exchange its starting and ending nodes. 
 
 
 Let  ς j = # ⁡ { k ∈ [ j − 1 ] ; A k ′ ≠ B k ′ } \varsigma_{j}=\#\{k\in[j-1]~;~A^{\prime}_{k}\neq B^{\prime}_{k}\} .
We choose the replacing pattern  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  as follows: 
 
 •  
 
 if  A j ′ ≠ B j ′ A^{\prime}_{j}\neq B^{\prime}_{j}  and  ς j \varsigma_{j}  is even, then  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  starts at  A i j ⊔ X ′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup X^{\prime}  and ends at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ Y ′ A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup Y^{\prime} ; 
 
 •  
 
 if  A j ′ ≠ B j ′ A^{\prime}_{j}\neq B^{\prime}_{j}  and  ς j \varsigma_{j}  is odd, then  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  starts at  A i j ⊔ Y ′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup Y^{\prime}  and ends at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ X ′ A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup X^{\prime} ; 
 
 •  
 
 if  A j ′ = B j ′ A^{\prime}_{j}=B^{\prime}_{j}  and  ς j \varsigma_{j}  is odd, then  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  starts at  A i j ⊔ Y ′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup Y^{\prime}  and ends at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ Y ′ A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup Y^{\prime} ; 
 
 •  
 
 if  A j ′ = B j ′ A^{\prime}_{j}=B^{\prime}_{j}  and  ς j \varsigma_{j}  is even, then  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  starts at  A i j ⊔ X ′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup X^{\prime}  and ends at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ X ′ A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup X^{\prime} . 
 
 
 For all  j ∈ [ r − 1 ] j\in[r-1] , by construction of  ς j \varsigma_{j} , the orientation of  H H  of the last node of  𝑷 j {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j}  is  A i j + 1 − 1 A_{i_{j+1}-1}  and the orientation of  H H  of the first node of  𝑷 j + 1 {\boldsymbol{P}}_{j+1}  is  A i j + 1 A_{i_{j+1}}  and the orientation of  H ′ H^{\prime}  is the same (either  X ′ X^{\prime}  or  Y ′ Y^{\prime} ), so these two nodes are adjacent in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
In particular, the concatenation  ( 𝑷 1 , 𝑷 2 , … , 𝑷 r ) ({\boldsymbol{P}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{P}}_{2},\dots,{\boldsymbol{P}}_{r})  is a Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) . 
 
 
 Moreover, if  # ⁡ { j ∈ [ r ] ; A j ′ ≠ B j ′ } \#\{j\in[r]~;~A^{\prime}_{j}\neq B^{\prime}_{j}\}  is even, then either  ς r \varsigma_{r}  is odd and  A j ′ ≠ B j ′ A^{\prime}_{j}\neq B^{\prime}_{j} , so its last node is  A s ⊔ X ′ A_{s}\sqcup X^{\prime} , or  ς r \varsigma_{r}  is odd and  A j ′ = B j ′ A^{\prime}_{j}=B^{\prime}_{j} , so its last node is  A s ⊔ X ′ A_{s}\sqcup X^{\prime} .
If, in addition,  A s A_{s}  and  A 1 A_{1}  are adjacent in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  ( i.e.  if  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H) ), then the last node of  𝑷 r {\boldsymbol{P}}_{r}  and the first node of  𝑷 1 {\boldsymbol{P}}_{1}  are adjacent in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  so  ( 𝑷 1 , 𝑷 2 , … , 𝑷 r ) ({\boldsymbol{P}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{P}}_{2},\dots,{\boldsymbol{P}}_{r})  is a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 As before, one can generalize  Theorem   4.5  in order to weaken its hypotheses, for the more general statement, see Appendix  A.2 . 
 
 
 
 4.2.  Multipath gluing and 0-even sequences 

 
 For a graph  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  and two nodes   u , v ∈ N u,v\in N , the  path gluing  of length  ℓ \ell  at  u , v u,v , denoted  ⊕ u , v ℓ G {\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G} , is the graph obtained by adding  ℓ − 1 \ell-1  nodes  to  G G  and constructing a path of length  ℓ \ell  between  u u  and  v v , supported on these new nodes.
Formally, using the notation we have introduced for gluings in  Section   2.4 , let  P ℓ P_{\ell}  be a path of length  ℓ \ell  with end nodes  x x  and  y y , and let  φ 1 : { u } → { x } \varphi_{1}:\{u\}\to\{x\}  and  φ 2 : { v } → { y } \varphi_{2}:\{v\}\to\{y\} , then  ⊕ u , v ℓ G ≔ G ⊕ φ 1 , φ 2 P ℓ \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G\coloneqq G\oplus_{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}}P_{\ell} . 
 
 
 The  multipath gluing  of length  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ m ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m})  at  u , v u,v  is then defined recursively for  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  as the graph  ⊕ u , v ℓ G ≔ ⊕ u , v ℓ m ( ⊕ u , v ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ m − 1 ) G ) {\oplus_{u,v}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}G}\coloneqq\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{m}}\bigl(\oplus_{u,v}^{(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m-1})}G\bigr) . 
 
 
 Definition 4.6 . 
 
 A sequence of integers  𝒂 = ( a 1 , … , a r ) {\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})  is  0 0 -even  if, between any two non-zero entries of  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}} , there is an even number of zeros in  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}} . 
 
 
 
 4.2.1.  Gluing a path of even length 

 
 Our main objective in this section is to prove  Theorem   4.7 .
This motivates us to define special subsequences as well as a proposition on regularity of replacing patterns (see Proposition  4.8 ).
The proof is delayed to page  4.2.1 . 
 
 
 Theorem 4.7 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
For  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  in the same connected component of  G G  and an even number  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 , if  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even, then  ⊕ u , v ℓ G \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Moreover, under these assumptions, there exists a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 = ( B 1 , … , B t ) {\boldsymbol{B}}=(B_{1},\dots,B_{t})  in  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G)  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Our proof will explicitly construct a new Hamiltonian cycle  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  for  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G)  with  ℓ \ell  even, from any Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  (such that  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even) and any Hamiltonian cycle of the cube-graph  Q ℓ Q_{\ell} . 
 
 
 For the cube-graph  Q ℓ Q_{\ell} , fix a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑺 ≔ ( S 1 , … , S 2 ℓ ) {{\boldsymbol{S}}}\coloneqq(S_{1},\dots,S_{2^{\ell}})  of  Q ℓ ≃ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P ℓ ) Q_{\ell}\simeq{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{\ell})  (see  Example   2.4  for its existence), where we interpret each  S p S_{p}  as a subset of  E ⁡ ( P ℓ ) E(P_{\ell})  (instead of a subset of  [ ℓ ] [\ell] ).
Without loss of generality, we can suppose that  S 2 ℓ = [ ℓ ] ≃ E ⁡ ( P ℓ ) {S_{2^{\ell}}=[\ell]\simeq E(P_{\ell})} ,  i.e.   S 2 ℓ ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 S_{2^{\ell}}(u,v)=-1 ; and we denote by  μ {\mu}  the index in  [ 2 ℓ ] [2^{\ell}]  such that  S μ = ∅ {S_{\mu}=\varnothing} ,  i.e.   S μ ​ ( u , v ) = 1 S_{\mu}(u,v)=1 .
Note that  Q ℓ Q_{\ell}  is bipartite:
 ∅ \varnothing  and  E ⁡ ( P ℓ ) E(P_{\ell})  belong to the same part if and only if  ℓ \ell  is even.
Consequently, as we have supposed  ℓ \ell  even, this implies that  μ \mu  is even and not  0 0 . 
 
 
 Let  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  be a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  such that  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even.
Without loss of generality, we can suppose that  A 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{1}(u,v)=1 .
For  a , b ∈ { − 1 , + 1 } a,b\in\{-1,+1\} , a  ( a → b ) (a\to b) -subsequence  of  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a sequence  ( A p , A p + 1 , … , A q ) (A_{p},A_{p+1},\dots,A_{q})  for some  p ≤ q p\leq q  considered modulo  r r  such that  A p ​ ( u , v ) = a A_{p}(u,v)=a  and  A q ​ ( u , v ) = b A_{q}(u,v)=b  and  A j ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{j}(u,v)=0  for all  p < j < q p<j<q .
If  p = q − 1 p=q-1 , then we call it a  degenerate   ( a → b ) (a\to b) -subsequence of  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}} . This is the case if and only if the edge between  u u  and  v v  exists in  G G . 
 
 
 Proposition 4.8 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
For  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  in the same connected component of  G G  and an even number  ℓ ≥ 2 \ell\geq 2 , if  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even, then for any  ( a → b ) (a\to b) -subsequence  ( A p , … , A q ) (A_{p},\dots,A_{q})  of  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  with  a , b ∈ { − 1 , + 1 } a,b\in\{-1,+1\} , there exists a replacing pattern  𝐏 {\boldsymbol{P}}  such that: 
 
 (a)  
 
 𝑷 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{P}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even, and 
 
 (b)  
 
 if  b = 1 b=1  (resp.  b = − 1 b=-1 ), then  ω q 𝑷 = 1 2 ​ … ​ 2 ℓ − 1 \omega_{q}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}=1\,2\,\dots 2^{\ell}-1  (resp.  ω q 𝑷 = μ + 1 ​ μ + 2 ​ … ​ μ − 1 \omega_{q}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}=\mu+1\,\mu+2\,\dots\mu-1 ), and 
 
 (c)  
 
 cons p ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) ⊇ { S 2 ​ k ; k ∈ [ 2 ℓ ] } \cons_{p}({\boldsymbol{P}})\supseteq\{S_{2k}~;~k\in[2^{\ell}]\}  and  cons q ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) ⊇ { S 2 ​ k + 1 ; k ∈ [ 2 ℓ ] } \cons_{q}({\boldsymbol{P}})\supseteq\{S_{2k+1}~;~k\in[2^{\ell}]\} , and 
 
 (d)  
 
 if  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is not degenerate and  A q ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{q}(u,v)=1  (resp.  A q ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 A_{q}(u,v)=-1 ), then  cons q ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) = [ 2 ℓ ] ∖ { 2 ℓ } \cons_{q}({\boldsymbol{P}})=[2^{\ell}]\smallsetminus\{2^{\ell}\}  (resp.  cons q ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) = [ 2 ℓ ] ∖ { μ } \cons_{q}({\boldsymbol{P}})=[2^{\ell}]\smallsetminus\{\mu\} ). 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is by sketching the relevant replacing patterns with the claimed properties. 
 
 
 For a non-degenerate  ( 1 → 1 ) (1\to 1) -subsequence,  Figure   7  illustrates a replacing pattern with the claimed properties.
The node in the  i th i^{\text{th}}  row and the  j th j^{\text{th}}  column represents the orientation  A i ⊔ S j A_{i}\sqcup S_{j}  for  i ∈ [ p , q ] i\in[p,q]  and  j ∈ [ 2 ℓ ] j\in[2^{\ell}] .
A node is drawn with a red star  ⋆ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\star}  if  A i ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{i}(u,v)=1  or  S j ​ ( u , v ) = 1 S_{j}(u,v)=1 ;
it is drawn with a blue circle  ∘ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\circ}  if  A i ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 A_{i}(u,v)=-1  or  S j ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 S_{j}(u,v)=-1 ;
otherwise, it is drawn with a black bullet  ∙ \bullet .
Hence,  A i ⊔ S j A_{i}\sqcup S_{j}  is an acyclic orientation of  ⊕ u , v ℓ G \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G  if and only if its associated node is not both a red star  ⋆ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\star}  and a blue circle  ∘ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\circ} .
As  𝑨 = ( A p , … , A q ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{p},\dots,A_{q})  is a path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) , and  ( S 1 , … , S 2 ℓ ) (S_{1},\dots,S_{2^{\ell}})  is a cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P ℓ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(P_{\ell}) , the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | 𝑨 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|{\boldsymbol{A}})  contains (at least) edges between vertically adjacent nodes, and edges between horizontally adjacent nodes, and edges between the rightmost and the leftmost nodes of each row (which we represent by a dotted line). 
 
 
 We have chosen and oriented some of these edges in order to construct a replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} .
A vertical arrow coming from above the first row indicates the starting node of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}} , while a vertical arrow going below the last row indicates its ending node.
To prove that this pattern is  0 0 -even, it is enough to show that each (maximal) path of consecutive black bullets  ∙ \bullet  contains an even number of such black bullets  ∙ \bullet .
This is ensured by the fact that  μ \mu  is even.
To determine  ω p 𝑷 \omega_{p}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}  (resp.  ω q 𝑷 \omega_{q}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}} ), one follows along the directed path  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and each time one sees a new node of the first (resp. last) row, one records the number of its column.
To determine  cons p ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \cons_{p}({\boldsymbol{P}})  (resp.  cons q ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \cons_{q}({\boldsymbol{P}}) ), one follows along the nodes of the first (resp. last) row in the order given by  ω p 𝑷 \omega_{p}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}  (resp.  ω q 𝑷 \omega_{q}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}} ),  i.e.  left-to-right starting from the first column, and one records which node has an edge going horizontally to its right. 
 
 
 Figure 7 .  Replacing pattern for a  ( 1 → 1 ) (1\to 1) -subsequence.
 
 
 
 For a  ( 1 → − 1 ) (1\to-1) -subsequence, the drawing is given in  Figure   8 , with the same conventions.
Note that, here,  cons q ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \cons_{q}({\boldsymbol{P}})  is determined following along the nodes of the last row from left to right starting from the  ( μ + 1 ) th (\mu+1)^{\text{th}}  column, and recording which node has an edge going horizontally to its right. 
 
 
 Figure 8 .  Replacing pattern for a  ( 1 → − 1 ) (1\to-1) -subsequence. 
 
 
 For degenerate  ( 1 → 1 ) (1\to 1) -subsequences and degenerate  ( 1 → − 1 ) (1\to-1) -subsequences, the drawings are given in  Figures   9  and  10 , with the same conventions. 
 
 
 Figure 9 .  Replacing pattern for a degenerate  ( 1 → 1 ) (1\to 1) -subsequence. 
 
 
 Figure 10 .  Replacing pattern for a degenerate  ( 1 → − 1 ) (1\to-1) -subsequence. 
 
 
 The drawings for the (degenerate)  ( − 1 → − 1 ) (-1\to-1) -subsequences and for the (degenerate)  ( − 1 → 1 ) (-1\to 1) -subsequences can be obtained from the ones of  Figures   7 ,  8 ,  9  and  10  by swapping red stars  ⋆ {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\star}  and blue circles  ∘ {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\circ} .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of  Theorem   4.7 . 
 
 Fix a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  with  A 1 = A s A_{1}=A_{s}  such that  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 On the one hand, we suppose that there exists  j ∈ [ s ] j\in[s]  such that  A j ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{j}(u,v)=0 .
Without loss of generality, we can consider that  A 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{1}(u,v)=1  and  A s − 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{s-1}(u,v)=0 .
Let  p 1 , … , p r p_{1},\dots,p_{r}  be such that the  ( a → b ) (a\to b) -subsequences of  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  for all possible  a , b ∈ { − 1 , + 1 } a,b\in\{-1,+1\}  are  ( A p i , A p i + 1 , … , A p i + 1 ) (A_{p_{i}},A_{p_{i}+1},\dots,A_{p_{i+1}})  for  i ∈ [ r ] i\in[r]  (in particular  p 1 = 1 = p r + 1 p_{1}=1=p_{r+1} ). 
 
 
 Fix  i ∈ [ r ] i\in[r] .
Let  𝑷 i {\boldsymbol{P}}_{i}  be a replacing pattern for  ( A p i , A p i + 1 , … , A p i + 1 ) (A_{p_{i}},A_{p_{i}+1},\dots,A_{p_{i+1}})  given by  Proposition   4.8 .
If  A p i + 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{p_{i+1}}(u,v)=1  (resp.  A p i + 1 ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 A_{p_{i+1}}(u,v)=-1 ), then
by ( b ) we get  ω p i + 1 𝑷 i = ω p i + 1 𝑷 i + 1 \omega_{p_{i+1}}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}_{i}}=\omega_{p_{i+1}}^{{\boldsymbol{P}}_{i+1}} , and
by ( c ) we get  cons p i + 1 ⁡ ( 𝑷 i ) ∪ cons p i + 1 ⁡ ( 𝑷 i + 1 ) = [ 2 ℓ ] ∖ { 2 ℓ } \cons_{p_{i+1}}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{i})\cup\cons_{p_{i+1}}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{i+1})=[2^{\ell}]\smallsetminus\{2^{\ell}\}  (resp.  cons p i + 1 ⁡ ( 𝑷 i ) ∪ cons p i + 1 ⁡ ( 𝑷 i + 1 ) = [ 2 ℓ ] ∖ { μ } \cons_{p_{i+1}}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{i})\cup\cons_{p_{i+1}}({\boldsymbol{P}}_{i+1})=[2^{\ell}]\smallsetminus\{\mu\} ). 
 
 
 Thus by  Proposition   4.3 ,  𝑷 i {\boldsymbol{P}}_{i}  and  𝑷 i + 1 {\boldsymbol{P}}_{i+1}  can lace for all  i ∈ [ r ] i\in[r] .
Thus  𝑩 ≔ 𝑷 1 ∧ ⋯ ∧ 𝑷 r {\boldsymbol{B}}\coloneqq{\boldsymbol{P}}_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge{\boldsymbol{P}}_{r}  is a replacing pattern for  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}} .
It remains to prove that  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  can self-lace.
As we have assumed that  A 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{1}(u,v)=1  and  A s − 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{s-1}(u,v)=0 , the last  ( a → 1 ) (a\to 1) -subsequence  ( A p r − 1 , … , A p r ) (A_{p_{r-1}},\dots,A_{p_{r}})  is not degenerate.
Thus by ( d ), we have  cons s ⁡ ( 𝑩 ) = [ 2 ℓ ] ∖ { 2 ℓ } \cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{B}})=[2^{\ell}]\smallsetminus\{2^{\ell}\} .
Combining this with  ω s 𝑩 = 1 2 ​ … ​  2 ℓ − 1 \omega_{s}^{{\boldsymbol{B}}}=1\,2\,\dots\,2^{\ell}-1  proves that  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  can self-lace.
By  Corollary   4.4 , we get that  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G) . 
 
 
 On the other hand, assume that  A j ​ ( u , v ) ≠ 0 A_{j}(u,v)\neq 0  for all  j ∈ [ s ] j\in[s]  (equivalently, that  G G  contains the edge between  u u  and  v v ).
Suppose that there are two consecutive  A i ​ ( u , v ) A_{i}(u,v)  and  A i + 1 ​ ( u , v ) A_{i+1}(u,v)  of the same sign, so we can assume that  A 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{1}(u,v)=1  and  A s − 2 ​ ( u , v ) = A s − 1 ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 A_{s-2}(u,v)=A_{s-1}(u,v)=-1 . 
 
 
 The reasoning is very similar, as consecutive (degenerate)  ( a → b ) (a\to b) -subsequences can lace according to  Proposition   4.8 .
However, we need to modify the last step of the chosen replacing patterns so that the resulting  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  can self-lace and yield a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G) .
We swap the last replacing pattern for  ( A s − 2 , A s − 1 ) (A_{s-2},A_{s-1})  by the one drawn in  Figure   11  where the last vertical edge connects  A s − 1 ⊔ S 1 A_{s-1}\sqcup S_{1}  to  A 1 ⊔ S 1 A_{1}\sqcup S_{1} , closing the Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G) . 
 
 
 Figure 11 .  Alternative replacing pattern for a degenerate  ( − → − 1 ) (-1\!\to\!-1) -subsequence. 
 
 
 Otherwise,  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  alternates between  + 1 +1  and  − 1 -1  say  A 2 ​ k ​ ( u , v ) = 1 A_{2k}(u,v)=1  and  A 2 ​ k + 1 ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 A_{2k+1}(u,v)=-1 , then we can replace each  ( A 2 ​ k , A 2 ​ k + 1 ) (A_{2k},A_{2k+1})  by the replacing pattern of  Figure   12  to directly get a cycle in  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell}G)  (no lacing is needed). 
 
 
 Figure 12 .  Alternative replacing pattern for a degenerate  ( 1 → − 1 ) (1\to-1) -subsequence. 
 
 
 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.2.2.  Gluing two paths of odd lengths 

 
 The main objective of this section is to prove  Theorem   4.9 .
As before we state it here, and delay the proof to page  4.2.2 .
The proof is very similar to  Theorem   4.7 , except that it is conceptually easier since no lacing is needed; but the sketches are more intricate since the replacing patterns are larger. 
 
 
 Theorem 4.9 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
For  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  in the same connected component of  G G  and two odd numbers  ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 ≥ 2 \ell_{1},\ell_{2}\geq 2 , if  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even, then  ⊕ u , v ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 G \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Moreover, under these assumptions, there exists a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 = ( B 1 , … , B t ) {\boldsymbol{B}}=(B_{1},\dots,B_{t})  in  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}G)  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Our proof will explicitly construct a new Hamiltonian cycle  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  for  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}G)  with  ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 \ell_{1},\ell_{2}  odd, from any Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  (such that  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even) and almost any Hamiltonian cycle of the cube-graphs  Q ℓ 1 Q_{\ell_{1}}  and  Q ℓ 2 Q_{\ell_{2}} . 
 
 
 For the cube-graph  Q ℓ Q_{\ell}  for  ℓ \ell  odd, fix a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑺 ≔ ( S 1 , … , S 2 ℓ ) {{\boldsymbol{S}}}\coloneqq(S_{1},\dots,S_{2^{\ell}})  of  Q ℓ ≃ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P ℓ ) Q_{\ell}\simeq{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{\ell})  (see  Example   2.4  for its existence), where we interpret each  S p S_{p}  as a subset of  E ⁡ ( P ℓ ) E(P_{\ell})  (instead of a subset of  [ ℓ ] [\ell] ).
Without loss of generality, we can suppose that  S 2 ℓ = [ ℓ ] ≃ E ⁡ ( P ℓ ) {S_{2^{\ell}}=[\ell]\simeq E(P_{\ell})} ,  i.e.   S 2 ℓ ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 S_{2^{\ell}}(u,v)=-1 ; and we denote  μ ∈ [ 2 ℓ ] {\mu}\in[2^{\ell}]  such that  S μ = ∅ {S_{\mu}=\varnothing} ,  i.e.   S μ ​ ( u , v ) = 1 S_{\mu}(u,v)=1 .
Note that  Q ℓ Q_{\ell}  is bipartite:
 ∅ \varnothing  and  E ⁡ ( P ℓ ) E(P_{\ell})  do not belong to the same part if and only if  ℓ \ell  is odd.
Consequently, as we have supposed  ℓ \ell  odd, this implies that  μ \mu  is odd.
Note also that  μ ∉ { 1 , 2 ℓ − 1 } \mu\notin\{1,2^{\ell}-1\}  since  ∅ \varnothing  and  [ 2 ℓ ] [2^{\ell}]  are not adjacent in the cube-graph  Q ℓ Q_{\ell} . 
 
 
 For given odd  ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 \ell_{1},\ell_{2} , we denote  𝑺 ′ = ( S 1 ′ , … , S μ 1 ′ , … , S 2 ℓ 1 ′ ) {\boldsymbol{S}}^{\prime}=(S^{\prime}_{1},\dots,S^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}},\dots,S^{\prime}_{2^{\ell_{1}}})  the chosen Hamiltonian cycle of  Q ℓ 1 Q_{\ell_{1}} , and denote  𝑺 ′′ = ( S 2 ′′ , … , S μ 2 ′′ , … , S 2 ℓ 2 ′′ ) {\boldsymbol{S}}^{\prime\prime}=(S^{\prime\prime}_{2},\dots,S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu_{2}},\dots,S^{\prime\prime}_{2^{\ell_{2}}})  the chosen Hamiltonian cycle of  Q ℓ 2 Q_{\ell_{2}} , where  μ 1 \mu_{1}  and  μ 2 \mu_{2}  are odd with  μ 1 , μ 2 ∉ { 1 , 2 ℓ − 1 } \mu_{1},\mu_{2}\notin\{1,2^{\ell}-1\} . 
 
 
 Proposition 4.10 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with two nodes  u , v u,v  in the same connected component of  G G , and let  ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 ≥ 3 \ell_{1},\ell_{2}\geq 3  be odd numbers.
We denote  A ⊔ A ′ ⊔ A ′′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}\sqcup A^{\prime\prime}  an orientation of  ⊕ u , v ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 G \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}G  where  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G) ,  A ′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P ℓ 1 ) A^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{\ell_{1}})  and  A ′′ ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P ℓ 2 ) A^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{\ell_{2}}) . 
 
 (1)  
 
 If  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 A(u,v)=+1  (resp.  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = − 1 A(u,v)=-1 ), there exists a  0 0 -even replacing pattern 1 1

Note that the replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  is  0 0 -even, and starts at  A 1 ⊔ S μ 1 + 1 ′ ⊔ S μ 2 − 1 ′′ A_{1}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}+1}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu_{2}-1}  and ends at  A 2 ⊔ S μ 1 + 1 ′ ⊔ S μ 2 − 1 ′′ A_{2}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{\mu_{1}+1}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu_{2}-1} .

 Proof of  Theorem   4.9 . 
 
 For odd  ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 ≥ 3 \ell_{1},\ell_{2}\geq 3 , this is a direct consequence of  Theorems   4.5  and  4.10  using the splitting  i j + 1 = i j i_{j+1}=i_{j}  if  A i j ​ ( u , v ) ≠ 0 A_{i_{j}}(u,v)\neq 0 , and  i j + 1 = i j + 1 i_{j+1}=i_{j}+1  if  A i j = 0 A_{i_{j}}=0 .
As the number of acyclic orientations  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)  with  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = + 1 A(u,v)=+1  is equal to the number of acyclic orientations  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)  with  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = − 1 A(u,v)=-1  by  Lemma   3.41  ( 4 ), the quantity  # ⁡ { j ∈ [ r ] ; A j ′ ≠ B j ′ } \#\{j\in[r]~;~A^{\prime}_{j}\neq B^{\prime}_{j}\}  of  Theorem   4.5  is even.
Thus, we get a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ 1 , ℓ 2 G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}G) .
The  0 0 -even-ness of this cycle is ensured by the  0 0 -even-ness of each replacing pattern of  Proposition   4.10 . 
 
 
 The remaining case is  ℓ 1 = 1 \ell_{1}=1  and  ℓ 2 ≥ 3 \ell_{2}\geq 3  is odd.
For readability, we call  S + ′ S^{\prime}_{+}  and  S − ′ S^{\prime}_{-}  the two orientations of  P 1 P_{1} , where  S + ′ ​ ( u , v ) = + 1 S^{\prime}_{+}(u,v)=+1  and  S − ′ ​ ( u , v ) = − 1 S^{\prime}_{-}(u,v)=-1 .
Also, we rename  ℓ 2 \ell_{2}  into simply  ℓ \ell , and  μ 2 \mu_{2}  into  μ \mu .
The proof is mainly via the drawings of  Figure   15  where the conventions are the same as in  Figures   13  and  14 .
We cannot directly apply  Theorem   4.5 , but we can concatenate paths joining four families of orientations, namely  A ⊔ S + ′ ⊔ S μ − 1 ′′ A\sqcup S_{+}^{\prime}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu-1} , and  A ⊔ S + ′ ⊔ S μ + 1 ′′ A\sqcup S_{+}^{\prime}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu+1} , and  A ⊔ S − ′ ⊔ S 1 ′′ A\sqcup S_{-}^{\prime}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{1} , and  A ⊔ S − ′ ⊔ S 2 ℓ − 1 ′′ A\sqcup S_{-}^{\prime}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{2^{\ell}-1}  for  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G) . 
 
 
 We again use the splitting subsequences of  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  defined by  i j + 1 = i j i_{j+1}=i_{j}  if  A i j ​ ( u , v ) ≠ 0 A_{i_{j}}(u,v)\neq 0 , and  i j + 1 = i j + 1 i_{j+1}=i_{j}+1  if  A i j ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{i_{j}}(u,v)=0 .
Since we are gluing the edge  u ​ v uv , we are sure that  u ​ v ∉ E ⁡ ( G ) uv\notin E(G) , so we can consider that  A 1 ​ ( u , v ) = + 1 A_{1}(u,v)=+1  and  A s ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{s}(u,v)=0  without loss of generality.
By  Figure   15  (bottom left & right) and symmetries, if  A i j ​ ( u , v ) = A i j + 1 ​ ( u , v ) = 0 A_{i_{j}}(u,v)=A_{i_{j+1}}(u,v)=0  there exist replacing patterns for  ( A i j , A i j + 1 ) (A_{i_{j}},A_{i_{j+1}})  that start at  A i j ⊔ S ε ′ ⊔ S x ′′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{x}  and ends at  A i j + 1 ⊔ S η ′ ⊔ S y ′′ A_{i_{j+1}}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{\eta}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{y}  for all  ε , η ∈ { − , + } \varepsilon,\eta\in\{-,+\}  and all possible  x , y ∈ { μ − 1 , μ + 1 , 1 , 2 ℓ − 1 } x,y\in\{\mu-1,\mu+1,1,2^{\ell}-1\} .
On the other hand, by  Figure   15  (top left & right), if  A i j ​ ( u , v ) ≠ 0 A_{i_{j}}(u,v)\neq 0 , then there exists a replacing pattern for  ( A i j ) (A_{i_{j}})  that starts at  A i j ⊔ S ε ′ ⊔ S x ′′ A_{i_{j}}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{x}  and ends at  A i j + 1 ⊔ S η ′ ⊔ S y ′′ A_{i_{j+1}}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{\eta}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{y}  for all possible  ε , η ∈ { − , + } \varepsilon,\eta\in\{-,+\}  and all possible  x , y ∈ { μ − 1 , μ + 1 , 1 , 2 ℓ − 1 } x,y\in\{\mu-1,\mu+1,1,2^{\ell}-1\} .
The concatenation of these paths immediately yields a Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
Moreover, we can choose the replacing pattern of  A 1 A_{1}  to start at  A 1 ⊔ S + ′ ⊔ S μ − 1 ′′ A_{1}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{+}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu-1} , and choose the replacing pattern of  A s A_{s}  to end at  A s ⊔ S + ′ ⊔ S μ − 1 ′′ A_{s}\sqcup S^{\prime}_{+}\sqcup S^{\prime\prime}_{\mu-1} :
Thus,  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  admits a Hamiltonian cycle.

 ∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.2.3.  Gluing a multipath of even total length 

 
 Using  Theorems   4.7  and  4.9 , we can prove the main result of this section. 
 
 
 Corollary 4.11 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
For  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  in the same connected component of  G G  and integers  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ m ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m})  for  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , if: 
 
 •  
 
 ∑ i = 1 m ℓ i \sum_{i=1}^{m}\ell_{i}  is even (i.e. the number of odd  ℓ i \ell_{i} s is even), and 
 
 •  
 
 𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even, 
 
 
 then  ⊕ u , v ℓ G \oplus_{u,v}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 Moreover, under these assumptions, there exists a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 = ( B 1 , … , B t ) {\boldsymbol{B}}=(B_{1},\dots,B_{t})  in  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊕ u , v ℓ G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\oplus_{u,v}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}G)  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Inductive application of  Theorems   4.7  and  4.9 :
since each Theorem yields a Hamiltonian cycle whose sequence of directions on  ( u , v ) (u,v)  is  0 0 -even, the induction runs.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary   4.11  has several interesting consequences. First of all, the  0 0 -even-ness can be dropped if  u ​ v uv  is an edge of  G G , since it is impossible that  A ⁡ ( u , v ) = 0 A(u,v)=0  in this case. 
 
 
 Corollary 4.12 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph, and let  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E .
For numbers  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s})  if  ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p \sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}  is odd, then  ⊕ u , v ℓ G \oplus_{u,v}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 As  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E , for any acyclic orientation  A A  of  G G , there is a directed path from  u u  to  v v  or from  v v  to  u u ,  i.e.   A ⁡ ( u , v ) ≠ 0 A(u,v)\neq 0 .
Hence, for any Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A r ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{r})  of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) , the sequence  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even.
Applying  Corollary   4.11  yields the claim.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Secondly, since a single path is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, we can finish our characterization of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian multipaths, proving  Theorem   B . 
 
 
 Corollary 4.13 . 
 
 The  ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) (\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}) -multipath is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if  ∑ p ℓ p \sum_{p}\ell_{p}  is odd. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The “only if” direction is proven in  Corollary   3.70 .
For the “if” direction, consider the graph  G ∘ = ( { u , v } , ∅ ) G_{\circ}=\bigl(\{u,v\},\varnothing\bigr)  composed of 2 isolated nodes: Its  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is composed of a single node.
Even if one might not consider this  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  to be Hamiltonian, is it enough for applying  Corollary   4.11 : if  ∑ p ℓ p \sum_{p}\ell_{p}  is odd, then the  ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) (\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}) -multipath  ⊕ u , v ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s G ∘ \oplus_{u,v}^{\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}}G_{\circ}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Thirdly, we can characterize  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graphs among certain families of graphs: 
 
 
 Example 4.14 . 
 
 As an immediate consequence of  Corollaries   4.12 ,  4.11  and  4.13 , we get: 
 
 (1)  
 
 As proven in  [ 37 , Lem. 3.1 & Thm. 4.2] ,
the cycle graph  C n C_{n}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if  n n  is odd: Indeed  C n C_{n}  is isomorphic to  ( n − 1 ,  1 ) (n-1,\,1) -multipath. 
 
 (2)  
 
 Any  2 2 -sum of two cycle graphs  C n C_{n}  and  C m C_{m}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if  n + m n+m  is even: Indeed  C n ⊕ 2 C m C_{n}\oplus_{2}C_{m}  is isomorphic to the  ( n − 1 , m − 1 ,  1 ) (n-1,\,m-1,\,1) -multipath. 
 
 (3)  
 
 For a tree  T = ( [ s ] , E ) T=([s],E)  and numbers  n 1 , … , n s ≥ 3 n_{1},\dots,n_{s}\geq 3 , a  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles  is any graph isomorphic to a  2 2 -sum  H ⊕ 2 C n s H\oplus_{2}C_{n_{s}}  where  H H  is a  ( T ∖ s , n 1 , … , n s − 1 ) (T\smallsetminus s,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s-1}) -tree of cycles (of course for  s = 1 s=1 , a  ( T , n 1 ) (T,\,n_{1}) -tree of cycle is just a cycle graph  C n 1 C_{n_{1}} ).
By consecutive applications of  Corollary   4.12 , one shows that if  n p n_{p}  is odd for all  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s] , then any  ( T , n 1 , … , n s ) (T,\,n_{1},\dots,n_{s}) -tree of cycles is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, for any tree  T T . 
 
 (4)  
 
 If  u ​ v ∈ E uv\in E  is an edge of  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E) , and  C n C_{n}  is a cycle graph, then the  2 2 -sum  G ⊕ 2 C n G\oplus_{2}C_{n}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if  n n  is odd: Indeed this is the case  s = 1 s=1  of  Corollary   4.12 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.3.  Adding a simplicial node and 0-even sequences 

 
 Recall that a node  x x  in a graph  G G  is simplicial if the neighbors of  x x  form a clique, that is if every pair of neighbors of  x x  share an edge in  G G .
If  K K  is a clique in  G G , the  simplicial augmentation  above  K K , denoted  ⊙ K G {\odot_{K}G} , is the graph obtained by adding a new (simplicial) node to  G G  connected to all the nodes of  K K ,  i.e.   ⊙ K G ≔ G ⊕ n K n + 1 \odot_{K}G\coloneqq G\oplus_{n}K_{n+1}  where  n = | K | n=|K|  and  K n + 1 K_{n+1}  is the clique on  [ n + 1 ] [n+1] . 
 
 
 In the details of the proof of  [ 37 , Theorem 4.1]  that chordal graphs are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, they prove the first sentence of the upcoming  Theorem   4.15 .
We use the Steinhaus–Johnson–Trotter algorithm (see  Example   2.5 ) in a similar way but we keep track of  0 0 -evenness: 
 
 
 Theorem 4.15 . 
 
 If  G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, and  K K  is a clique of  G G , then  ⊙ K G \odot_{K}G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
Moreover, for any nodes  u , v u,v  of  G G , if  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and either  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even or if  | K | |K|  is odd, then  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊙ K G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\odot_{K}G)  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 {\boldsymbol{B}}  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  K K  be a clique of  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  with  n = | K | n=|K| , and let  v ∘ v_{\circ}  be the added node. Since  K K  is a clique, any acyclic orientation  A A  of  G G  induces a total order on  K K , denoted  σ A : K → [ n ] \sigma_{A}:K\to[n] .
Reciprocally, to a total order  τ \tau  on  K ⊔ { v ∘ } K\sqcup\{v_{\circ}\} , we associate the orientation  𝒪 ⁡ ( τ ) {\mathcal{O}}(\tau)  of the neighboring edges of  v ∘ v_{\circ}  where we orient from  v ∘ v_{\circ}  to  u ∈ K u\in K  if  τ ⁡ ( v ∘ ) < τ ⁡ ( u ) \tau(v_{\circ})<\tau(u) , and conversely from  u u  to  v ∘ v_{\circ}  if  τ ⁡ ( u ) < τ ⁡ ( v ∘ ) \tau(u)<\tau(v_{\circ}) . 
 
 
 As in  Example   2.5 , for  i ∈ [ n + 1 ] i\in[n+1] , we denote  σ A ( i ) \sigma_{A}^{(i)}  the total order on  K ⊔ { v ∘ } K\sqcup\{v_{\circ}\}  defined by 
 

 
 
 σ A ( i ) ​ ( u ) = { σ A ​ ( u ) if  ​ σ A ​ ( u ) < i i if  ​ u = v ∘ σ A ​ ( u ) + 1 if  ​ σ A ​ ( u ) > i  for  ​ u ∈ K ⊔ { v ∘ } . \sigma_{A}^{(i)}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{A}(u)&\text{if }\sigma_{A}(u)<i\\
i&\text{if }u=v_{\circ}\\
\sigma_{A}(u)+1&\text{if }\sigma_{A}(u)>i\end{array}\right.\qquad\text{ for }u\in K\sqcup\{v_{\circ}\}. 
 
 
 
 
 There is a bijection between acyclic orientations  A ′ A^{\prime}  of  ⊙ K G \odot_{K}G  and pairs  ( A , i ) (A,i)  with  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and  i ∈ [ n + 1 ] i\in[n+1]  given by  ( A , i ) ↦ A ⊔ 𝒪 ⁡ ( σ A ( i ) ) (A,i)\mapsto A\sqcup{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\sigma_{A}^{(i)}\bigr) .
Indeed, the edges of  ⊙ K G \odot_{K}G  are bipartite between the edges of  G G  and the edges adjacent to  v ∘ v_{\circ} , and the orientation  A ⊔ A ′ A\sqcup A^{\prime}  of  ⊙ K G \odot_{K}G  (split according to this bipartition) is acyclic if and only if the total order induced by  A ′ A^{\prime}  on  K ⊔ { v ∘ } K\sqcup\{v_{\circ}\}  which agrees on  K K  with the total order induced by  A A  on  K K .
Moreover,  A ⊔ 𝒪 ⁡ ( σ A ( i ) ) A\sqcup{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\sigma_{A}^{(i)}\bigr)  is adjacent to  B ⊔ 𝒪 ⁡ ( σ B ( j ) ) B\sqcup{\mathcal{O}}\bigl(\sigma_{B}^{(j)}\bigr)  in  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊙ K G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\odot_{K}G)  if and only if either  A A  is adjacent to  B B  in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and  i = j i=j , either  A = B A=B  and  j ∈ { i − 1 , i + 1 } j\in\{i-1,i+1\} . 
 
 
 Consequently, given a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  (recall that  s s  is even by  Lemma   3.41 ), the following is mapped to a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  of  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊙ K G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\odot_{K}G) : 
 

 
 
 ( ( A 1 , n + 1 ) , … , ( A 1 , 1 ) , ( A 2 , 1 ) , … , ( A 2 , n + 1 ) , ( A 3 , n + 1 ) , … , ( A 3 , 1 ) , … , ( A s , 1 ) , … , ( A s , n + 1 ) ) . \bigl((A_{1},n+1),\dots,(A_{1},1),(A_{2},1),\dots,(A_{2},n+1),(A_{3},n+1),\dots,(A_{3},1),\dots,(A_{s},1),\dots,(A_{s},n+1)\bigr)\,. 
 
 
 
 
 Note that in  ( A k , i ) (A_{k},i)  there is no directed path between  u u  and  v v  if and only if there is no directed path between  u u  and  v v  in  A k A_{k} .
Hence, by construction, the number of non-zero entries of  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  between two non-zero entries is always  n + 1 n+1  times the corresponding number for  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v) .
Thus, if  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even or if  n n  is odd, then  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Example 4.16 . 
 
 Consider the complete graph  K 3 K_{3}  and let  K = { 2 , 3 } K=\{2,3\}  be a clique of  K 3 K_{3} .
The graph  ⊙ 23 K 3 \odot_{23}K_{3}  is drawn in  Figure   16 .
A Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 3 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{3})  is given by  𝑨 = ( 123,132,312,321,231 , 213 ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(123,132,312,321,\linebreak 231,213)  where  i ​ j ​ k ijk  correspond to the orientation of  K 3 K_{3}  with arcs  i → j i\to j ,  j → k j\to k  and  i → k i\to k .
