 Remark 4.17 . 
 
 For  K K  a clique of the graph  G G , and any Hamiltonian cycle  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  of  𝒜 𝒪 ( ⊙ K G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(\odot_{K}G) , note that  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , x ∘ ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,x_{\circ})  is  0 0 -even for all  u ∈ K u\in K , where  x ∘ x_{\circ}  denotes the added simplicial node.
Indeed, there is an edge between  u u  and  x ∘ x_{\circ}  in  ⊙ K G \odot_{K}G , so  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , x ∘ ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,x_{\circ})  contains no  0 0 . 
 
 
 
 We can combine  Corollaries   4.11  and  4.15  into a single corollary.
To this end, we introduce  simplicial multi-augmentation  of a graph  G G  above a clique-sequence  𝑲 = ( K 1 , … , K s ) {\boldsymbol{K}}=(K_{1},\dots,K_{s}) , defined as  G G  if  s = 0 s=0 , otherwise as  ⊙ 𝑲 G ≔ ⊙ K s ( ⊙ ( K 1 , … , K s − 1 ) G ) \odot_{{\boldsymbol{K}}}G\coloneqq\odot_{K_{s}}\bigl(\odot_{(K_{1},\dots,K_{s-1})}G\bigr)  where  K s K_{s}  is a clique of  ⊙ ( K 1 , … , K s − 1 ) G \odot_{(K_{1},\dots,K_{s-1})}G . 
 
 
 We are going to construct a sequence of graphs where we alternate between gluing a multipath and adding a simplicial node.
To ease notation, we consider that we start by gluing a multipath, but one can easily start by adding a simplicial node instead.
In each step, we just need to state a condition ensuring that we glue the multipath between two nodes where we can guarantee that the Hamiltonian cycle constructed thus far is  0 0 -even.
This way we prove (the general version of)  Theorem   C . 
 
 
 Corollary 4.18 . 
 
 Let  G 0 , … , G p G_{0},\dots,G_{p}  be a sequence of graphs such that  G 2 ​ i + 1 = ⊕ ℓ i u i , v i G 2 ​ i G_{2i+1}=\oplus^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}_{i}}_{u_{i},v_{i}}G_{2i}  for some  u i , v i ∈ N ⁡ ( G 2 ​ i ) u_{i},v_{i}\in N(G_{2i})  and some  ℓ i ∈ ℕ ≥ 1 r i {\boldsymbol{\ell}}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}^{r_{i}}  with  r i ≥ 1 r_{i}\geq 1  where  ℓ i {\boldsymbol{\ell}}_{i}  contains at most one  1 1 , and  G 2 ​ i + 2 = ⊙ 𝐊 i G 2 ​ i + 1 G_{2i+2}=\odot_{{\boldsymbol{K}}_{i}}G_{2i+1}  for some clique-sequence  𝐊 i {\boldsymbol{K}}_{i}  of length  s i ≥ 0 s_{i}\geq 0  on  G 2 ​ i + 1 G_{2i+1} .
If 
 
 (1)  
 
 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G 0 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G_{0})  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  where  𝑨 ⁡ ( u 0 , v 0 ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u_{0},v_{0})  is  0 0 -even; and 
 
 (2)  
 
 for all  i i  we have that  ∑ j = 1 r i ℓ i , j \sum_{j=1}^{r_{i}}\ell_{i,j}  is even, that  u i u_{i}  and  v i v_{i}  are in the same connected component of  G 2 ​ i G_{2i} ; and either: 
 
 •  
 
 u i ​ v i ∈ E ⁡ ( G 2 ​ i ) u_{i}v_{i}\in E(G_{2i}) ; or 
 
 •  
 
 s i ≥ 1 s_{i}\geq 1  and there is  j j  such that  | K i , j | |K_{i,j}|  is odd, and, with  m i = max ⁡ { j ; | K i , j | ​  is odd } m_{i}=\max\{j~;~|K_{i,j}|\text{ is odd}\}  and  ( x i , 1 , … , x i , s i ) (x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,s_{i}})  the simplicial nodes added in  ⊙ 𝑲 i G 2 ​ i + 1 \odot_{{\boldsymbol{K}}_{i}}G_{2i+1} , we have  u i , v i ∈ N ⁡ ( G 2 ​ i ) ∖ { x i , m i , x i , m i + 1 , … , x i , s i } u_{i},v_{i}\in N(G_{2i})\smallsetminus\{x_{i,m_{i}},x_{i,m_{i}+1},\dots,x_{i,s_{i}}\} ; 
 
 
 
 
 then  G p G_{p}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Direct combination of  Corollaries   4.11 ,  4.12  and  4.15 :
the conditions ensure that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G i ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G_{i})  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝑨 i {\boldsymbol{A}}_{i}  such that  𝑨 2 ​ i ​ ( u i , v i ) {\boldsymbol{A}}_{2i}(u_{i},v_{i})  is  0 0 -even for all  i i .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.4.  Disconnected graphs, 1-sums, strongly even and internally even sequences 

 
 It is well-known that if  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  are Hamiltonian graphs, then the Cartesian product  H × H ′ H\times H^{\prime}  is also Hamiltonian:
for instance, it is the first sentence of  [ 43 ] .
Consequently, if  G G  has several connected components, each inducing a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph, then  G G  itself is also  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian because it is straightforward to show that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  is the Cartesian product of the graph of acyclic orientations of its connected components.
Similarly, if  G = H ⊕ 1 H ′ G=H\oplus_{1}H^{\prime}  is a  1 1 -sum, then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) = 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)={\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}) , so if  H H  and  H ′ H^{\prime}  are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then  G G  is also  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 We can go further and keep track of the  0 0 -evenness.
For disconnected graphs, the question is trivial by definition:
if  u u  and  v v  are not in the same connected component, then  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) = ( 0 , 0 , … , 0 ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)=(0,0,\dots,0)  for any sequence of orientations  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}} , since no orientation can induce a directed path between  u u  and  v v .
(Note however that  Corollary   4.11  does not apply if  u u  and  v v  are not in the same connected component.)
The case of  1 1 -sums is, strangely, richer.
We need two notions to further control evenness. 
 
 
 Definition 4.19 . 
 
 A sequence of integers  𝒂 = ( a 1 , … , a r ) {\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})  is  strongly even  if for all  x ∈ { a 1 , … , a r } x\in\{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\}  with  x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 : 
 
 •  
 
 if  1 ≤ i < j ≤ r 1\leq i<j\leq r  are such that  a i = a j ≠ 0 a_{i}=a_{j}\neq 0  and  a k ≠ a i a_{k}\neq a_{i}  for all  i < k < j i<k<j , then  i i  and  j j  have the same parity; and 
 
 •  
 
 min ⁡ { i ; a i = x } \min\{i~;~a_{i}=x\}  is odd and  max ⁡ { i ; a i = x } \max\{i~;~a_{i}=x\}  is even. 
 
 
 
 
 
 Definition 4.20 . 
 
 A sequence of integers  𝒂 = ( a 1 , … , a r ) {\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})  is  internally even  if: 
 
 •  
 
 for any  x ∈ { a 1 , … , a r } x\in\{a_{1},\dots,a_{r}\}  with  x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 , if  1 ≤ i < j ≤ r 1\leq i<j\leq r  are such that  a i = a j ≠ 0 a_{i}=a_{j}\neq 0  and  a k ≠ a i a_{k}\neq a_{i}  for all  i < k < j i<k<j , then  i i  and  j j  have the same parity; and 
 
 •  
 
 the parity of  min ⁡ { i ; a i = x } \min\{i~;~a_{i}=x\}  does not depend on  x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 ; and the parity of  max ⁡ { i ; a i = x } \max\{i~;~a_{i}=x\}  does not depend on  x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 4.21 . 
 
 It could be that  𝒂 {\boldsymbol{a}}  is strongly even but not  0 0 -even,  e.g.   𝒂 = ( 1 , 0 , 2 , 1 , 0 , 2 ) {\boldsymbol{a}}=(1,0,2,1,0,2) .
On the other hand, strongly even implies internally even. 
 
 
 
 We first show that  1 1 -summing a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with a strongly even  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph yields a  0 0 -even  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph. 
 
 
 Proposition 4.22 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊕ 1 H ′ G=H\oplus_{1}H^{\prime}  be a  1 1 -sum with  w ∈ N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) w\in N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime}) , and let any  u ∈ N ⁡ ( H ) ∖ { w } u\in N(H)\smallsetminus\{w\}  and  v ∈ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) ∖ { w } v\in N(H^{\prime})\smallsetminus\{w\} .
If  H ′ H^{\prime}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  such that  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , w ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,w)  is strongly even, then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 {\boldsymbol{B}}  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is both strongly even and  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  𝑨 ′ = ( A 1 ′ , … , A t ′ ) {\boldsymbol{A}}^{\prime}=(A^{\prime}_{1},\dots,A^{\prime}_{t})  be a Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  (where  𝑨 ′ ​ ( w , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}^{\prime}(w,v)  is not necessarily  0 0 -even).
We denote  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s}) .
Since  G G  is the  1 1 -sum  H ⊕ 1 H ′ H\oplus_{1}H^{\prime} , any two acyclic orientations  A i ∈ 𝑨 A_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{A}} ,  A j ′ ∈ 𝑨 ′ A^{\prime}_{j}\in{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\prime}  give rise to an acyclic orientation of  G G , so the acyclic orientations of  G G  are  A i ⊔ A j ′ A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{j}  for all  i ∈ [ s ] i\in[s]  and  j ∈ [ t ] j\in[t] .
Besides, since the total number of acyclic orientations of a graph is always even (see  Lemma   3.41 ), both  s s  and  t t  are even.
Consider  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  defined as: 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 𝑩 = ( A 1 ⊔ A ′ 1 , A 2 ⊔ A ′ 1 , … , A s ⊔ A ′ 1 , A s ⊔ A 2 ′ , A s − 1 ⊔ A 2 ′ , … , A 1 ⊔ A 2 ′ , A 1 ⊔ A 3 ′ , A 2 ⊔ A 3 ′ , … , A s ⊔ A 3 ′ , … , OPEN A s ⊔ A t ′ , A s − 1 ⊔ A t ′ , … , A 1 ⊔ A t ′ ) . \begin{array}[]{rl}{\boldsymbol{B}}=&\bigl(A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{1},\,A_{2}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{1},\,\dots,\,A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{1},\\
&A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{2},\,A_{s-1}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{2},\,\dots,\,A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{2},\\
&A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{3},\,A_{2}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{3},\,\dots,\,A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{3},\\
&\dots,\\
&A_{s}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{t},\,A_{s-1}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{t},\,\dots,\,A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{t}\bigr)\,.\end{array} 
 
 
 
 
 By construction,  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) .
Moreover,  ( A i ⊔ A j ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) ≠ 0 (A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{j})(u,v)\neq 0  if and only if  A i ​ ( u , w ) = A j ′ ​ ( w , v ) ≠ 0 A_{i}(u,w)=A^{\prime}_{j}(w,v)\neq 0 .
If  A j ′ ​ ( w , v ) = 0 A^{\prime}_{j}(w,v)=0 , then  ( A i ⊔ A j ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) = 0 (A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{j})(u,v)=0 , so in evaluating the above definition to get  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  the  j th j^{\text{th}}  row is  ( 0 , … , 0 ) (0,\dots,0)  (and it is of even length).
If  A j ′ ​ ( w , v ) = + 1 A^{\prime}_{j}(w,v)=+1  (resp.  − 1 -1 ), then  ( A i ⊔ A j ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) = 0 (A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{j})(u,v)=0  except if  A i ​ ( u , w ) = + 1 A_{i}(u,w)=+1  (resp.  − 1 -1 ), so the  j th j^{\text{th}}  row in the above is the sequence  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , w ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,w)  with its  − 1 -1  (resp.  + 1 +1 ) replaced by  0 0 :
since  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , w ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,w)  is strongly even, this sequence is formed by  1 1  (resp.  − 1 -1 ) separated by an even number of zeros with an even number of zeros at the beginning and at the end.
Therefore, by concatenation, the sequence  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 Furthermore,  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is strongly even.
Indeed, if  ( A i ⊔ A j ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) = + 1 (A_{i}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{j})(u,v)=+1  and  ( A k ⊔ A ℓ ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) = + 1 (A_{k}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{\ell})(u,v)=+1  are such that there is no  A m ⊔ A n ′ A_{m}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{n}  in between in  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  with  ( A m ⊔ A n ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) = + 1 (A_{m}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{n})(u,v)=+1 , then either  j = ℓ j=\ell  and both orientations are on the same row and separated by an even number of zeros as we have seen; either  j < ℓ j<\ell  and both orientations are separated by a certain number of rows (each of them of even length) and are an even number of zeros away from the endpoints of their respective rows.
Besides, the second condition for strong evenness of  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  straightforwardly follows from the one for  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Secondly,  1 1 -summing a  0 0 -even  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with an internally even  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph preserves both  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity and  0 0 -evenness. 
 
 
 Proposition 4.23 . 
 
 Let  G = H ⊕ 1 H ′ G=H\oplus_{1}H^{\prime}  be a  1 1 -sum with  w ∈ N ⁡ ( H ) ∩ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) w\in N(H)\cap N(H^{\prime}) , and let any  u ∈ N ⁡ ( H ) ∖ { w } u\in N(H)\smallsetminus\{w\}  and  v ∈ N ⁡ ( H ′ ) ∖ { w } v\in N(H^{\prime})\smallsetminus\{w\} .
If  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐀 ′ {\boldsymbol{A}}^{\prime}  such that  𝐀 ′ ​ ( w , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}^{\prime}(w,v)  is  0 0 -even and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐀 {\boldsymbol{A}}  such that  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , w ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,w)  is internally even, then  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  admits a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 {\boldsymbol{B}}  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is both internally even and  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The proof is very similar to the one of  Proposition   4.22 , only the last part of the argument needs to be modified. 
 
 
 We construct  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  as in  Equation   4.1 .
We call the  i th i^{\text{th}}  row of  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  a  0 0 -row if  ( A k ⊔ A i ′ ) ​ ( u , v ) = 0 (A_{k}\sqcup A^{\prime}_{i})(u,v)=0  for all  k ∈ [ s ] k\in[s] .
Suppose that the  i th i^{\text{th}}  and the  j th j^{\text{th}}  rows of  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  are non- 0 0 -rows but that the  k th k^{\text{th}}  row is a  0 0 -row for all  i < k < j i<k<j .
Since  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , w ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,w)  is  0 0 -even, the  0 0 -rows come in consecutive sequences of even length:  i i  and  j j  have the same parity.
Let  f i f_{i}  be the number of final  0 0 s in the  i th i^{\text{th}}  row of  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  and  ℓ j \ell_{j}  the number of leading  0 0 s in its  j th j^{\text{th}}  row.
As  i i  and  j j  have the same parity,  f i f_{i}  and  ℓ j \ell_{j}  are either both a minimum  k k  such that  A k ′ ​ ( w , v ) ≠ 0 A^{\prime}_{k}(w,v)\neq 0  or both a maximum  k k  such that  A k ′ ​ ( w , v ) ≠ 0 A^{\prime}_{k}(w,v)\neq 0 .
Since  𝑨 ′ ​ ( w , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}^{\prime}(w,v)  is internally even,  f i f_{i}  and  ℓ j \ell_{j}  have the same parity ( i.e.   f i + ℓ j f_{i}+\ell_{j}  is even).
Thus, the number of consecutive  0 0 s between the last non-zero value of the  i th i^{\text{th}}  row of  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  and the first non-zero value of its  j th j^{\text{th}}  row is even.
Consequently,  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 A similar reasoning as in the proof of  Proposition   4.22  for strong evenness shows that  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is also internally even.
∎ 
 
 
 
 With some case-by-case analysis, the reader can adapt  Propositions   4.22  and  4.23  for  1 1 -summing several graphs at once.
We do not state a general theorem here since it is unbearably notation-heavy.
However, we give the following corollary.
To prove it, we need the following notion. 
 
 
 Definition 4.24 . 
 
 For  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and four nodes  u , v , x , y ∈ N u,v,x,y\in N , their  combined direction  is 
 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( u , v ) ∧ A ⁡ ( x , y ) ≔ { A ⁡ ( u , v ) if  ​ A ​ ( u , v ) = A ⁡ ( x , y ) 0 otherwise . {A(u,v)\wedge A(x,y)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}A(u,v)&\text{if }A(u,v)=A(x,y)\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,. 
 
 
 
 
 As per usual, for a sequence of orientations  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A r ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{r}) , we denote  𝑨 ⁡ ( u , v ) ∧ 𝑨 ⁡ ( x , y ) ≔ ( A 1 ​ ( u , v ) ∧ A 1 ​ ( x , y ) , … , A r ​ ( u , v ) ∧ A r ​ ( x , y ) ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)\wedge{\boldsymbol{A}}(x,y)\coloneqq\bigl(A_{1}(u,v)\wedge A_{1}(x,y),\,\dots,\,A_{r}(u,v)\wedge A_{r}(x,y)\bigr) . 
 
 
 
 Corollary 4.25 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and let  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  such that  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even.
Let  H H  be the  1 1 -sum at  u u  of  G G  with a path  P m P_{m}  with endpoint  x x , and let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be the  1 1 -sum at  v v  of  H H  with a path  P n P_{n}  with endpoint  y y . 
 
 
 Then both  H H  and  G ′ G^{\prime}  are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian.
If, moreover,  m m  and  n n  have the same parity, then there exists a Hamiltonian cycle  𝐁 = ( B 1 , … , B t ) {\boldsymbol{B}}=(B_{1},\dots,B_{t})  in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G^{\prime})  such that  𝐁 ⁡ ( x , y ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(x,y)  is  0 0 -even. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  H H  is a  1 1 -sum, the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  is isomorphic to  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P m ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{m}) , while the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G^{\prime})  is isomorphic to  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P m ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{m})\times{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{n}) .
The Cartesian product of Hamiltonian graphs being Hamiltonian, and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P k ) {\mathcal{AO}}(P_{k})  being isomorphic to the (Hamiltonian) cube-graph  Q k Q_{k} , we get that both  H H  and  G ′ G^{\prime}  are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 Let  𝑺 {\boldsymbol{S}}  (resp.  𝑺 ′ {\boldsymbol{S}}^{\prime} ) be a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P m ) {\mathcal{AO}}(P_{m})  with  S 1 ​ ( x , u ) = 1 S_{1}(x,u)=1  and  S μ ​ ( x , u ) = − 1 S_{\mu}(x,u)=-1  (resp.  S 1 ′ ​ ( v , y ) = 1 S^{\prime}_{1}(v,y)=1  and  S μ ′ ′ ​ ( v , y ) = − 1 S^{\prime}_{\mu^{\prime}}(v,y)=-1 ).
Since  m m  and  n n  have the same parity,  μ \mu  and  μ ′ \mu^{\prime}  also have the same parity (which is the parity of  m + 1 m+1 ).
Consider the usual Hamiltonian cycle  𝑻 {\boldsymbol{T}}  in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P m ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( P n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(P_{m})\times{\mathcal{AO}}(P_{n})  constructed via  Equation   4.1  on  𝑺 {\boldsymbol{S}}  and  𝑺 ′ {\boldsymbol{S}}^{\prime} .
For an acyclic orientation  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(G) , we have  ( A ⊔ S ⊔ S ′ ) ​ ( x , y ) = 1 (A\sqcup S\sqcup S^{\prime})(x,y)=1  (resp.  − 1 -1 ) if and only if  S ⁡ ( x , u ) = A ⁡ ( u , v ) = S ′ ​ ( v , y ) = 1 S(x,u)=A(u,v)=S^{\prime}(v,y)=1  (resp.  − 1 -1 ).
The sequence  ( S ( x , u ) ∧ S ′ ( v , y ) ; S ⊔ S ′ ∈ 𝑻 ) \bigl(S(x,u)\wedge S^{\prime}(v,y)~;~S\sqcup S^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{T}}\bigr)  is internally even.
Indeed, it has a unique  1 1  at the first position, and a unique  − 1 -1  at position either  m ⋅ ( μ − 1 ) + μ ′ m\cdot(\mu-1)+\mu^{\prime}  if  m m  is even, or  m ⋅ ( μ − 1 ) + n − μ ′ m\cdot(\mu-1)+n-\mu^{\prime}  if  m m  is odd: this position is odd in both cases. 
 
 
 It is left to the reader as an exercise to prove that a variant of  Proposition   4.23  implies that the Hamiltonian cycle  𝑩 {\boldsymbol{B}}  of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G^{\prime})  constructed using  Equation   4.1  on  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  and  𝑻 {\boldsymbol{T}}  satisfies that  𝑩 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{B}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even.
This concludes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary 4.26 . 
 
 Let  G = ( N , E ) G=(N,E)  be a  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian graph with  𝐀 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and let  u , v ∈ N u,v\in N  such that  𝐀 ⁡ ( u , v ) {\boldsymbol{A}}(u,v)  is  0 0 -even.
Let  H H  be the  1 1 -sum at  u u  of  G G  with a path  P m P_{m}  with endpoint  x x , and let  G ′ G^{\prime}  be the  1 1 -sum at  v v  of  H H  with a path  P n P_{n}  with endpoint  y y . 
 
 
 If  m m  and  n n  have the same parity and  ∑ k ℓ k \sum_{k}\ell_{k}  is even, then  ⊕ x , y ℓ G ′ \oplus_{x,y}^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}G^{\prime}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Direct application of  Corollaries   4.11  and  4.25 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Corollary   4.26  is our third result (with  Corollaries   4.12  and  4.18 ) explaining how we can glue a multipath at a pair of nodes  ( u , v ) (u,v) , then glue another multipath at another pair of nodes  ( x , y ) (x,y) .
These three statements are of different nature and suggest the following problem for future research. 
 
 
 Question 4.27 . 
 
 Find conditions (such as  0 0 -even, strongly even and internally even) that a graph  G G  or (a Hamiltonian cycle in) its graph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  should satisfy to ensure that one can glue multipaths at the different pairs of nodes of  G G . 
 
 
 
 Note that this question is only an intermediate step.
If one wants to exploit the (open) ear decomposition of  2 2 -connected graphs in order to inductively certify whether a graph is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian or not, then he or she would not only need to solve the above question where the pairs of nodes are taken in the same starting graph  G G , but also to consider pairs of nodes where one or both lie on the glued ears ( i.e.  multipaths) of the previous steps. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix A  General statements about pattern lacing 

 
 A.1.  Loose lacing 

 
 In this section, we state more general methods of pattern (self-)lacing as compared to  Propositions   4.3  and  4.4 .
For a replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  of  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  and a replacing pattern  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  of  ( A s , … , A t ) (A_{s},\dots,A_{t}) , consider the graph, illustrated in  Figure   17 , whose node set is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s})  and where there are three types of edges: 
 
 •  
 
 red edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  such that  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ∉ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})\notin\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}}) ; and 
 
 •  
 
 blue edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  such that  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω s 𝑸 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( A ′ ) ∉ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑸 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})\notin\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{Q}}) ; and 
 
 •  
 
 green edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  such that  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ∈ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})\in\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}})  or such that  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω s 𝑸 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( A ′ ) ∈ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑸 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})\in\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{Q}}) . 
 
 
 
 
 We say that  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}   can loosely lace  if there exists a (directed) path  𝝆 {\boldsymbol{\rho}}  from  A ′ A^{\prime}  with  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) = 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})=1  to  B ′ B^{\prime}  with  ω s 𝑸 ​ ( B ′ ) = m \omega^{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=m  such that  𝝆 {\boldsymbol{\rho}}  contains all the  red edges  and all the  blue edges  (and some, perhaps none, of the  green edges ) of this graph.
One has to be careful, since several such paths may exist.
The  loose lace  of  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  according to  𝝆 {\boldsymbol{\rho}} , denoted  𝑷 ∧ 𝝆 𝑸 {{\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge_{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}} , is obtained by replacing each  red arc  (resp.  blue arc ) of  𝝆 {\boldsymbol{\rho}}  by the corresponding directed path in  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  (resp. in  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}} ), keeping each  green arc , and adding  𝑷 0 {\boldsymbol{P}}_{0}  and  𝑸 m {\boldsymbol{Q}}_{m}  (defined in the usual lacing  𝑷 ∧ 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{Q}} ). 
 
 
 A proof similar to the proof of  Proposition   4.3  ensures that:
If  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  can loosely lace, then any loose lace  𝑷 ∧ 𝝆 𝑸 {\boldsymbol{P}}\wedge_{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{Q}}  is a replacing pattern of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 , … , A t ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1},\dots,A_{t}) . 
 
 
 In the case of usual lacing, the graph at stake is a path with two edges between consecutive nodes, one of them being  green . 
 
 
 Figure 17 .  (Left) A replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  of  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  in  red  and a replacing pattern  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  of  ( A s , … , A t ) (A_{s},\dots,A_{t})  in  blue : for sake of clarity of the drawing, we have assumed that all  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i})  are the same, but in general many black dots  ∙ \bullet  would not be visited by replacing patterns.
Note that arcs in these replacing patterns can only be horizontal or vertical (not diagonal) since  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  are directed paths inside the product  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) × 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)\times{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}) .
The  green  arcs belong to  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  while the  olive  arcs belong to  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}} .
(Right) The graph on  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A s ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{s})  obtained by substituting directed paths in  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  by single edges in order to decide if  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and  𝑸 {\boldsymbol{Q}}  can loosely lace (here, they cannot).
 Green  arcs and  olive  arcs are  green  edges here since we no longer differentiate them. 
 
 
 Besides, for a replacing pattern  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  of  ( A 1 , … , A s ) (A_{1},\dots,A_{s})  with  A 1 = A s A_{1}=A_{s} , consider the graph whose node set is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A 1 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{1})  and where there are four types of edges: 
 
 •  
 
 red edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  such that  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ∉ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})\notin\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}}) ; and 
 
 •  
 
 blue edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  such that  ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{1}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{1}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ∉ cons 1 ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{1}(A^{\prime})\notin\cons_{1}({\boldsymbol{P}}) ; and 
 
 •  
 
 purple edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  if there is a path between  A 1 ⊔ A ′ A_{1}\sqcup A^{\prime}  and  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  in  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  and no  A 1 ⊔ B ′ A_{1}\sqcup B^{\prime}  nor  A s ⊔ B ′ A_{s}\sqcup B^{\prime}  lie on this path; and 
 
 •  
 
 green edges  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime}  such that  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω s 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ∈ cons s ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{s}(A^{\prime})\in\cons_{s}({\boldsymbol{P}})  or such that  ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( B ′ ) = ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) + 1 \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{1}(B^{\prime})=\omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{1}(A^{\prime})+1  and  ω 1 𝑷 ​ ( A ′ ) ∈ cons 1 ⁡ ( 𝑷 ) \omega^{{\boldsymbol{P}}}_{1}(A^{\prime})\in\cons_{1}({\boldsymbol{P}}) . 
 
 
 We say that  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}   can loosely self-lace  if this graph is loop-free and admits a (directed) cycle using all its  red ,  blue  and  purple  edges (and some, perhaps none, of its  green edges ).
The  loose self-lacing  according to  𝝆 {\boldsymbol{\rho}} , denoted  ∧ 𝝆 𝑷 {\wedge_{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{P}}}  is defined accordingly. 
 
 
 A proof similar to the proof of  Corollary   4.4  ensures that:
If  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  and  𝑷 {\boldsymbol{P}}  a replacing pattern for  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  that can loosely self-lace, then any loose self-lace  ∧ 𝝆 𝑷 \wedge_{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}{\boldsymbol{P}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle for  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G) . 
 
 
 In the case of usual self-lacing, the graph at stake contains no  blue edges , but only is a path of  green edges  from some  A ′ A^{\prime}  to some  B ′ B^{\prime}  together with another path of  red  and  green  edges from the same  A ′ A^{\prime}  to  B ′ B^{\prime} , and a unique  purple edge  between  A ′ A^{\prime}  and  B ′ B^{\prime} . 
 
 
 
 A.2.  Splitting subsequences with varying endpoints 

 
 One can easily generalize  Theorem   4.5  as follows, in order to weaken its hypotheses. 
 
 
 Let  G = H ⊔ H ′ G=H\sqcup H^{\prime}  be a bipartition of the edges of  G G .
Suppose that there exist  X 1 , … , X r ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ ) X_{1},\dots,X_{r}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime})  and a Hamiltonian path  𝑨 = ( A 1 , … , A s ) ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\boldsymbol{A}}=(A_{1},\dots,A_{s})\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H)  such that: 
 
 •  
 
 one can split the sequence  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  into splitting subsequences  𝑨 j = ( A i j , A i j + 1 , … , A i j + 1 − 1 ) {\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}=(A_{i_{j}},A_{i_{j}+1},\dots,A_{i_{j+1}-1})  for  j ∈ [ r ] j\in[r]  with  i 1 = 1 i_{1}=1  and  i r − 1 = s i_{r}-1=s ; and 
 
 •  
 
 X j ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i j ) ∩ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ′ | A i j − 1 ) X_{j}\in{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i_{j}})\cap{\mathcal{AO}}(H^{\prime}|A_{i_{j}-1})  is such that  𝑨 j {\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}  admits a replacing pattern which starts at  A i j ⊔ X j A_{i_{j}}\sqcup X_{j}  and ends at  A i j + 1 − 1 ⊔ X j + 1 A_{i_{j+1}-1}\sqcup X_{j+1} . 
 
 
 Then there exists a Hamiltonian path in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  obtained as the concatenation of these replacing patterns.
Moreover, if  𝑨 {\boldsymbol{A}}  is a Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( H ) {\mathcal{AO}}(H)  and if  X r = X 1 X_{r}=X_{1} , then  G G  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 This version of  Theorem   4.5  applies in a broader context, but has the drawback of making the pivotal orientations  X j X_{j}  depend on the splitting subsequences  𝑨 j {\boldsymbol{A}}_{j} . 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix B  A note on complete bipartite graphs 

 
 The  complete bipartite graph   K m , n {K_{m,n}}  is the graph on nodes  ( a 1 , … , a m ) (a_{1},\dots,a_{m})  and  ( b 1 , … , b n ) (b_{1},\dots,b_{n})  with edges  a i ​ b j a_{i}b_{j}  for all  i ∈ [ m ] i\in[m]  and  j ∈ [ n ] j\in[n] .
The longstanding question of which  K m , n K_{m,n}  are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian already appears in  [ 37 , Sections 3 & 5] .
In particular, the authors proved that if  K m , n K_{m,n}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then both  m m  and  n n  are odd, see  [ 37 , Theorem 3.3]  where they essentially prove that  σ ⁡ ( K m , n ) = 0 \sigma(K_{m,n})=0  if and only if both  m m  and  n n  are odd.
We conjecture (as other authors conducting research on the subject) that the reciprocal is true,  i.e.  that
the complete bipartite graph  K m , n K_{m,n}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian if and only if both  m m  and  n n  are odd. 
 
 
 Example B.1 . 
 
 As already explained in  [ 37 , below Thm. 3.3] , the graphs  K 1 , n K_{1,n}  are stars (hence, trees), so the graph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 1 , n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{1,n})  is isomorphic to the cube-graph  Q n Q_{n} .
In particular, by  Example   2.4 , the graphs  K 1 , n K_{1,n}  are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 The graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 3 , 5 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{3,5})  has  4 718 4\,718  nodes and  20 610 20\,610  edges, while the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 5 , 5 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{5,5})  has  329 462 329\,462  nodes and  1 902 750 1\,902\,750  edges, so a powerful computer and a good implementation of Hamiltonian cycle search might decide whether  K 3 , 5 K_{3,5}  and  K 5 , 5 K_{5,5}  are  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian or not. 
 
 
 Via a computer experiment (on a normal laptop), we confirmed that  K 3 , 3 K_{3,3}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, which was left open in  [ 37 ] : the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 3 , 3 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{3,3})  has  230 230  nodes and  702 702  edges.
To showcase the compactness of Gray codes, we give explicitly a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 3 , 3 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{3,3}) , namely: 
 
 
 [3 1 3 4 3 6 1 9 1 2 9 6 5 7 5 8 4 5 3 9 3 1 3 9 2 9 7 9 5 7 8 1 2 7 1 3 6 5 1 8 1 4 1 7 8 5 2 5 7 9 1 7 1
 2 5 2 1 8 3 5 6 2 9 6 9 7 2 3 2 7 2 1 5 2 6 3 4 1 7 5 3 6 8 1 4 1 9 1 3 2 6 7 1 8 7 2 1 7 8 6 2 8 1 8 7 3
 4 3 5 3 1 9 1 6 7 1 7 2 1 6 1 9 6 7 2 4 3 9 8 3 8 2 4 6 2 1 3 4 6 8 9 8 6 1 7 1 9 4 6 8 3 2 1 7 1 2 1 6 1
 3 6 8 1 8 9 4 5 9 2 3 9 7 4 9 6 4 7 4 1 5 1 7 1 2 3 5 2 7 2 3 7 8 3 4 7 1 5 8 5 2 9 7 1 2 4 2 8 4 6 1 5 7
 8 4 1 7 1 3 1 5 8 1 3 8 1 8 2 4 5 4] 
 
 
 To read this Hamiltonian cycle, one proceeds as follows.
The  i th i^{\text{th}}  digit  s i s_{i}  (read from left to right,  i.e.   s 1 = 3 s_{1}=3 ,  s 2 = 1 s_{2}=1 , etc.) of the above sequence corresponds to an acyclic orientation  A i A_{i}  defined by: 
 
 •  
 
 A 0 A_{0}  is the acyclic orientation depicted in  Figure   18 ; 
 
 •  
 
 A i A_{i}  is obtained from  A i − 1 A_{i-1}  by changing the orientation of the arc labeled  s i s_{i} . 
 
 
 Besides, to compute directly  A i A_{i}  from  A 0 A_{0} , one can count for each  j ∈ [ 9 ] j\in[9]  how many times it appears among  s 1 , … , s i s_{1},\dots,s_{i} : if  j j  appears an even number of times, then the arcs labeled  j j  are oriented the same way in  A 0 A_{0}  and in  A i A_{i} , otherwise they are not.
One can check that  A 230 A_{230}  (defined from  A 229 A_{229}  by changing the arc labeled  4 4 ) is indeed  A 0 A_{0} , so the above sequence is indeed a cycle. 
 
 
 
 Figure 18 .  The acyclic orientation  A 0 A_{0}  of  K 3 , 3 K_{3,3}  at the start of the Hamiltonian cycle of  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K 3 , 3 ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{3,3}) .
Colors are meaningless, they only help to read the labels of the arcs. 
 
 
 Let  𝒫 ⁡ ( i , j ) {\mathcal{P}}(i,j)  be the set of ordered partitions of  [ i ] [i]  into  j j  parts.
In  [ 37 ] , it is established that the acyclic orientations of the complete bipartite graph  K m , n K_{m,n}  are in one-to-one correspondence with triples  ( Q , Q ′ , δ ) (Q,Q^{\prime},\delta)  where  δ ∈ { 0 , 1 } \delta\in\{0,1\} ,  Q ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( m , k ) Q\in{\mathcal{P}}(m,k) ,  Q ′ ∈ 𝒫 ⁡ ( n , k + ε ) Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}(n,k+\varepsilon)  for some  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  and  ε ∈ { 0 , 1 } \varepsilon\in\{0,1\} .
Given an acyclic orientation  A A  of  K m , n K_{m,n} , one obtains such a triple as follows (see illustration in  Figure   19 ).
Let  S 1 S_{1}  be the set of sources of  A A , let  S 2 S_{2}  be the set of sources of  A ∖ S 1 A\smallsetminus S_{1} , and recursively let  S i S_{i}  be the set of sources of  A ∖ ( S 1 ∪ ⋯ ∪ S i − 1 ) A\smallsetminus(S_{1}\cup\dots\cup S_{i-1})  (where  S i ≠ ∅ S_{i}\neq\varnothing  since  A A  is acyclic), and let  S κ S_{\kappa}  be the last (non-empty) source set.
Note that each  S i S_{i}  must be contained exclusively in one of the two parts of  K m , n K_{m,n}  because  K m , n K_{m,n}  is the  complete  bipartite graph.
We set  ε = 1 \varepsilon=1  and  k = κ − 1 2 k=\frac{\kappa-1}{2}  if  κ \kappa  is odd, and  ε = 0 \varepsilon=0  and  k = κ 2 k=\frac{\kappa}{2}  otherwise.
We set  δ = 0 \delta=0  if  S 1 S_{1}  is contained in the part of  K m , n K_{m,n}  of size  m m , and  δ = 1 \delta=1  otherwise.
If  δ = 1 \delta=1 , then  Q = ( S 1 , S 3 , S 5 , … , S 2 ​ ( k + ε ) − 1 ) Q=(S_{1},S_{3},S_{5},\dots,S_{2(k+\varepsilon)-1})  is our ordered partition of  [ m ] [m] , and  Q ′ = ( S 2 , S 4 , S 6 , … , S 2 ​ k ) Q^{\prime}=(S_{2},S_{4},S_{6},\dots,S_{2k})  is our ordered partition of  [ n ] [n] ; if  δ = 1 \delta=1 , the opposite holds. 
 
 
 Equivalently,  S i S_{i}  is the set of elements of rank  i + 1 i+1  in the poset induced by  A A : this poset is ranked because  K m , n K_{m,n}  is the complete bipartite graph.
The reciprocal bijection can be found in  [ 37 ] . 
 
 
 We call the sequence  ( S 1 , … , S κ ) (S_{1},\dots,S_{\kappa})  and its encoding as triple  ( Q , Q ′ , δ ) (Q,Q^{\prime},\delta)  a  bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partition .
Two elements  p ∈ [ n ] p\in[n] ,  q ∈ [ m ] q\in[m]  are  neighboring  in  ( S 1 , … , S κ ) (S_{1},\dots,S_{\kappa})  if there exists an index  i ∈ [ κ − 1 ] i\in[\kappa-1] , such that  p ∈ S i p\in S_{i}  and  q ∈ S i + 1 q\in S_{i+1} . 
 
 
 Figure 19 .  Two acyclic orientations of  K 2 , 3 K_{2,3}  together with their representations as posets.
The left one corresponds to the sequence of source sets  3 | 4 | 1 ​ | 5 | ​ 2 {\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}1}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}2}  yielding the triple  ( 4 | 5 ,  3 ​ | 1 | ​ 2 ,  1 ) (4|5,\,3|1|2,\,1)  where  ε = 1 \varepsilon=1 , while the right one corresponds to  4 | 12 ​ | 5 | ​ 3 {\color[rgb]{1,0,0}4}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}12}|{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}5}|{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}3}  yielding the triple  ( 4 | 5 ,  12 | 3 ,  0 ) (4|5,\,12|3,\,0)  where  ε = 0 \varepsilon=0 . 
 
 
 In  [ 37 ] , the authors only focus on counting the number of bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions because they want to discuss a parity argument.
We go slightly further by describing the graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( m , n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(m,n)  thanks to bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions. 
 
 
 Let  ρ = ( Q , Q ′ , δ ) \rho=(Q,Q^{\prime},\delta)  be a bicolored partition and  p , q p,q  be two neighboring elements in  ρ \rho , with  p ∈ S i p\in S_{i}  and  q ∈ S i + 1 q\in S_{i+1} .
Consider the sequence
 𝑺 ⁡ ( p , q ) = ( S 1 , … , S i − 1 , S i ∖ { p } , { q } , { p } , S i + 1 ∖ { q } , S i + 2 , … , S κ ) . {\boldsymbol{S}}(p,q)=(S_{1},\dots,S_{i-1},S_{i}\smallsetminus\{p\},\{q\},\{p\},S_{i+1}\smallsetminus\{q\},S_{i+2},\dots,S_{\kappa})\,. 
This sequence  𝑺 ⁡ ( p , q ) {\boldsymbol{S}}(p,q)  may contain some empty sets (possibly  S i ∖ { p } S_{i}\smallsetminus\{p\}  or  S i + 1 ∖ { q } S_{i+1}\smallsetminus\{q\} ), so we  compress  it by removing all its empty sets and then merging adjacent sets of the same color together, that is by replacing all sub-sequences  ( S a − 1 , S a , S a + 1 ) (S_{a-1},S_{a},S_{a+1})  where  S a = ∅ S_{a}=\varnothing  by a sub-sequence  ( S a − 1 ∪ S a + 1 ) (S_{a-1}\cup S_{a+1}) . 
 
 
 Definition B.2 . 
 
 Denoting  ρ ′ \rho^{\prime}  the compressed version of  𝑺 ⁡ ( p , q ) {\boldsymbol{S}}(p,q)  as described above, we say that  ρ , ρ ′ \rho,\rho^{\prime}   differ by a flip  at  ( p , q ) (p,q) .
The  graph of flips  of bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions is the graph whose nodes are the bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions and where two nodes share an edge if they differ by some flip. 
 
 
 
 Lemma B.3 . 
 
 The graph  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( m , n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(m,n)  is isomorphic to the graph of flips of bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  A ∈ 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( m , n ) A\in{\mathcal{AO}}(m,n) , and  ρ = ( Q , Q ′ , δ ) \rho=(Q,Q^{\prime},\delta)  the associated triple.
We first need to show that flipping an edge  ( p , q ) (p,q)  where  p , q p,q  are neighboring in  ρ \rho  will not result in an oriented cycle.
Let  S i S_{i}  be the block containing  p p  and  S i + 1 S_{i+1}  the neighboring block of the partition  ρ \rho  (Because of the orientation of  ( p , q ) (p,q) ,  p p  comes before  q q ).
If there were an oriented cycle after flipping this edge, then there would be a directed path from  p p  to  q q  in  A A .
Since the graph is bipartite, the path has length at least  3 3  and is thus of the form  ( p , r 1 , r 2 , q ) (p,r_{1},r_{2},q) .
By construction of  A A , this means that the node  r 1 r_{1}  is in a block between  S i S_{i}  and  S i + 1 S_{i+1} , which is a contradiction. 
 
 
 Conversely, consider an edge  ( p , q ) (p,q)  between two non-neighboring blocks.
It follows that there are at least two blocks between  S i S_{i}  containing  p p  and  S j S_{j}  containing  q q .
Take one node of each block, then these  p , q p,q  and these two nodes form an oriented path in  A A  and would therefore form an oriented  4 4 -cycle with the edge  ( p , q ) (p,q) .
This finishes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Thus, the problem of finding a Hamiltonian cycle in  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( K m , n ) {\mathcal{AO}}(K_{m,n})  is equivalent to finding a Gray code for these bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions with respect to the flipping operation described above. 
 
 
 This latter problem has already been explored in the unlabeled case, so bicolored partitions where the elements in each block are not integers in  [ m ] [m]  and  [ n ] [n] , but rather indistinguishable elements.
Such an unlabeled colored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partition is a string of  m + n m+n  elements, namely  m m  zeros and  n n  ones.
Two such strings  s s  and  s ′ s^{\prime}  differ by a flip if and only if  s ′ s^{\prime}  is obtained from  s s  by swapping a (consecutive) sub-string  01 01  for a (consecutive) sub-string  10 10 , or the converse 2 2 
             2 
            
            
            
            
            
            
            
          Although this graph of flips has  ( m + n m ) \binom{m+n}{m}  elements, it is not the Johnson graph  J ⁡ ( m + n , m ) J(m+n,m)  since it is not regular. . 
 
 
 For example, the two unlabeled partitions corresponding to the bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions in  Figure   19  are respectively  10101 10101  and  01101 01101 . 
 
 
 Theorem B.4   ( [ 8 ,  15 ]  independently) . 
 
 If  n ≤ 1 n\leq 1  or  m ≤ 1 m\leq 1  or both  n n  and  m m  are odd, then the graph of flips of unlabeled bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions admits a Hamiltonian path starting at  11 ​ … ​ 100 ​ … ​ 0 11\dots 100\dots 0  and  00 ​ … ​ 011 ​ … ​ 1 00\dots 011\dots 1  with  m m  copies of  0 0  and  n n  copies of  1 1 . 
 
 
 
 Note that the above Gray code must start and end at the combinations  11 ​ … ​ 100 ​ … ​ 0 11\dots 100\dots 0  and  00 ​ … ​ 011 ​ … ​ 1 00\dots 011\dots 1  with  m m  copies of  0 0  and  n n  copies of  1 1 .
Consequently, a possible strategy to get a Gray code for the unlabeled case by lacing patterns into this existing Gray code could, at best, produce a Hamiltonian path, not a Hamiltonian cycle.
This strategy in general might not be fruitful, as the cardinality of unlabeled partitions is not easily relatable to the cardinality of the labeled case. 
 
 
 Question B.5 . 
 
 Is there a Gray code for the labeled bicolored  ( m , n ) (m,n) -partitions described above when both  m , n m,n  are odd?
Equivalently, is  K m , n K_{m,n}   𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian when both  m m  and  n n  are odd? 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix C  Parity argument via the count of acyclic orientations for multipaths 

 
 We refer to  Section   3.3.3  for the definitions concerning multipaths, no result from  Section   3.3.3  is needed.
We give an alternative proof that if a multipath is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian then its number of edges is even.
The first step is to count the number of (a)cyclic orientations of a multipath according to their rank with respect to the usual orientation  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}  from  u u  to  v v . 
 
 
 Proposition C.1 . 
 
 For  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}) , the number of acyclic orientations  A A  of  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  such that  rk D u → v ℓ ⁡ A = k \rank_{D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}}A=k  is: 
 

 
 
 | 𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | = ( ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p k ) − ∑ S , T ⊆ [ s ] S ∩ T = ∅ , S ≠ ∅ , T ≠ ∅ ( − 1 ) | S ⊔ T | ⋅ ( ∑ p ∉ S ⊔ T ℓ p k − ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p ) |{\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})|=\binom{\sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}}{k}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[s]\\
S\cap T=\varnothing,~S\neq\varnothing,~T\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|S\sqcup T|}\cdot\binom{\sum_{p\notin S\sqcup T}\ell_{p}}{k-\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Recall that  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) = 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) ∖ 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) {\mathcal{AO}}_{k}(D)={\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)\smallsetminus{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D) , and that  | 𝒪 k ​ ( D ) | = ( | E | k ) |{\mathcal{O}}_{k}(D)|=\binom{|E|}{k}  for all digraphs  D = ( N , E ) D=(N,E) .
Hence, it is enough to count  𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) {\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}) , remembering that  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}  has  ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p \sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}  arcs. 
 
 
 We denote by  E p E_{p}  the set of edges of the path  P ℓ p = ( u , i p , 2 , … , i p , ℓ p , v ) P^{\ell_{p}}=(u,i_{p,2},\dots,i_{p,\ell_{p}},v) , for  p ∈ [ s ] p\in[s] .
For a subset  X X  of edges of  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v} , the orientation  ( D u → v ℓ ) X (D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})^{X}  is cyclic if and only if  E p ⊆ X E_{p}\subseteq X  and  E q ∩ X = ∅ E_{q}\cap X=\varnothing  for some  p ≠ q p\neq q ,  i.e.  if and only if one of the paths in the multipath is directed from  u u  to  v v  while another one is directed from  v v  to  u u .
We count such sets  X X  according to their size, using inclusion-exclusion. 
 
 
 For  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , and  S , T ⊆ [ s ] S,T\subseteq[s]  with  S ∩ T = ∅ S\cap T=\varnothing , and  S ≠ ∅ S\neq\varnothing  and  T ≠ ∅ T\neq\varnothing , let  𝒳 k , S , T {\mathcal{X}}_{k,S,T}  be the set of subsets  X X  of edges of  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}  with  | X | = k |X|=k  such that  E p ⊆ X E_{p}\subseteq X  for  p ∈ S p\in S  and  E q ∩ X = ∅ E_{q}\cap X=\varnothing  for  q ∈ T q\in T .
Such a subset corresponds to picking  ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p \sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}  prescribed edges, then choosing the remaining  k − ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p k-\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}  among the edges that are not in  ⨆ p ∈ S ⊔ T E p \bigsqcup_{p\in{S\sqcup T}}E_{p} .
Thus (by convention, if  a < 0 a<0  or  b < 0 b<0  or  a < b a<b , then  ( a b ) = 0 \binom{a}{b}=0 ):  | 𝒳 k , S , T | = ( ∑ p ∉ S ⊔ T ℓ p k − ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p ) |{\mathcal{X}}_{k,S,T}|=\binom{\sum_{p\notin S\sqcup T}\ell_{p}}{k-\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}} . 
 
 
 The set of cyclic orientations of rank  k k , namely  𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) {\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}) , is in bijection with  ⋃ S , T ⊆ [ s ] 𝒳 k , S , T \bigcup_{S,T\subseteq[s]}{\mathcal{X}}_{k,S,T}  where the union is on all  S , T ⊆ [ s ] S,T\subseteq[s]  with  S ∩ T = ∅ S\cap T=\varnothing ,  S ≠ ∅ S\neq\varnothing  and  T ≠ ∅ T\neq\varnothing .
The latter union is not disjoint, but according to the inclusion-exclusion principle, we get the following, proving the claim: 
 

 
 
 | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | = | ⋃ S , T ⊆ [ s ] S ∩ T = ∅ , S , T ≠ ∅ 𝒳 k , S , T | = ∑ S , T ⊆ [ s ] S ∩ T = ∅ , S , T ≠ ∅ ( − 1 ) | S ⊔ T | ⋅ | 𝒳 k , S , T | ∎ \Bigl|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})\Bigr|=\left|\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[s]\\
S\cap T=\varnothing,~S,T\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}{\mathcal{X}}_{k,S,T}\right|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[s]\\
S\cap T=\varnothing,~S,T\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|S\sqcup T|}\cdot|{\mathcal{X}}_{k,S,T}|\hskip 14.22636pt\qed 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proposition C.2 . 
 
 If  ∑ p ℓ p \sum_{p}\ell_{p}  is even, then the  ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) (\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}) -multipath is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian. 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Let  ℓ = ( ℓ 1 , … , ℓ s ) {\boldsymbol{\ell}}=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{s}) .
We will apply  Lemma   2.9  (in the form discussed in  Remark   2.10 ):
if  P ℓ P^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then  ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | = 0 \sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})|=0 .
By  Proposition   C.1 , we get: 
 
 
 

 
 
 ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | \displaystyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})| 
 = ∑ S , T ⊆ [ s ] S ∩ T = ∅ , S , T ≠ ∅ ( − 1 ) | S ⊔ T | ⋅ ( ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ ( ∑ p ∉ S ⊔ T ℓ p k − ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p ) ) \displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[s]\\
S\cap T=\varnothing,~S,T\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|S\sqcup T|}\cdot\left(\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot\binom{\sum_{p\notin S\sqcup T}\ell_{p}}{k-\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ S , T ⊆ [ s ] S ∩ T = ∅ , S , T ≠ ∅ ( − 1 ) | S ⊔ T | ⋅ ( ∑ k ( − 1 ) k + ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p ⋅ ( ∑ p ∉ S ⊔ T ℓ p k ) ) \displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,T\subseteq[s]\\
S\cap T=\varnothing,~S,T\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|S\sqcup T|}\cdot\left(\sum_{k}(-1)^{k+\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}}\cdot\binom{\sum_{p\notin S\sqcup T}\ell_{p}}{k}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 Recall that  ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ ( m k ) = 0 \sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot\binom{m}{k}=0  if  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1  and equals  1 1  if  m = 0 m=0 .
Hence, the rightmost sum is non-zero if and only if  S ⊔ T = [ s ] S\sqcup T=[s] .
We obtain 
 
 
 

 
 
 ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | = ∑ S ⊔ T = [ s ] S ∩ T = ∅ , S , T ≠ ∅ ( − 1 ) | S ⊔ T | ⋅ ( − 1 ) ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p = ( − 1 ) s ⋅ ∑ S ⊆ [ s ] S ≠ ∅ , S ≠ [ s ] ( − 1 ) ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p \sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\sqcup T=[s]\\
S\cap T=\varnothing,~S,T\neq\varnothing\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|S\sqcup T|}\cdot(-1)^{\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}}=(-1)^{s}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[s]\\
S\neq\varnothing,~S\neq[s]\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}} 
 
 
 
 
 Note that  ( − 1 ) ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p = 1 (-1)^{\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}}=1  if and only if  S S  contains an even number of odd values  ℓ p \ell_{p} .
Hence, denoting  A ≔ { p ∈ [ s ] ; ℓ p ​  is odd } A\coloneqq\{p\in[s]~;~\ell_{p}\text{ is odd}\}  and  B ≔ { p ∈ [ s ] ; ℓ p ​  is even } B\coloneqq\{p\in[s]~;~\ell_{p}\text{ is even}\} , we get:
 ∑ S ⊆ [ s ] ( − 1 ) ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p = ∑ X ⊆ A Y ⊆ B ( − 1 ) | X | = 2 | B | ⋅ ∑ j = 0 | A | ( | A | j ) ​ ( − 1 ) j = 2 | B | ⋅ 0 = 0 \sum_{S\subseteq[s]}(-1)^{\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\subseteq A\\
Y\subseteq B\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|X|}=2^{|B|}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{|A|}\binom{|A|}{j}(-1)^{j}=2^{|B|}\cdot 0=0 .
Substituting in the above, we obtain: 
 

 
 
 ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | \displaystyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})| 
 = ( − 1 ) s ⋅ ( ∑ S ⊆ [ s ] ( − 1 ) ∑ p ∈ S ℓ p − ( − 1 ) 0 − ( − 1 ) ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p ) \displaystyle=(-1)^{s}\cdot\left(\sum_{S\subseteq[s]}(-1)^{\sum_{p\in S}\ell_{p}}~-~(-1)^{0}-(-1)^{\sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 = ( − 1 ) s + 1 ⋅ ( 1 + ( − 1 ) ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p ) \displaystyle=(-1)^{s+1}\cdot\left(1+(-1)^{\sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 By  Lemma   2.9  (see  Remark   2.10 ), if  D u → v ℓ D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v}  is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian, then  ∑ k ( − 1 ) k ⋅ | 𝒞 ​ 𝒪 k ​ ( D u → v ℓ ) | = 0 \sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\cdot|{\mathcal{CO}}_{k}(D^{{\boldsymbol{\ell}}}_{u\to v})|=0 , implying that  ( − 1 ) ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p = − 1 (-1)^{\sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}}=-1 ,  i.e.  implying that  ∑ p = 1 s ℓ p \sum_{p=1}^{s}\ell_{p}  is odd.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark C.3 . 
 
 Note that, contrarily to  Section   3.3.3 , the present reasoning does not allow computing the acyclic signature of multipaths, only determining whether it is  0 0  or not. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix D  Acyclic polynomials and  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity of some small graphs 

 
 To motivate further research, for  22 22  graphs (from the named-graph database of SageMath  [ 36 ] ), we computed the number of acyclic orientations  ψ ⁡ ( G ) \psi(G) , the acyclic signature  σ ⁡ ( G ) \sigma(G) , and the acyclic polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t) .
The chosen orientation  D D  of the graph  G G  is obtained by orienting each edge  i ​ j ij  of  G G  from  i i  to  j j  if  i < j i<j .
For each graph, we address  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonicity.
If  σ ⁡ ( G ) ≠ 0 \sigma(G)\neq 0 , then  G G  is not  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian; otherwise we need to construct the graph of acyclic orientations  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 ​ ( G ) {\mathcal{AO}}(G)  and search for a Hamiltonian cycle in it.
The latter is a  NP -hard problem.
Our computations took minutes for the Moser spindle, and did not finish within 24 hours for the Wagner graph. 
 
 
 Table 5 .  22 named graphs and their properties.
This table is to be read columnwise, the order of information is as follows:
A drawing of the graph, its name, if it is  𝒜 ​ 𝒪 {\mathcal{AO}} -Hamiltonian or not, the values  ( ψ ⁡ ( G ) , σ ⁡ ( G ) ) (\psi(G),\sigma(G)) , and the coefficients of the acyclic polynomial  Ψ ⁡ ( D , t ) \Psi(D;t)  where  D D  is the orientation from  i i  to  j j  for  i < j i<j  (if  i ​ j ij  is an edge) in the drawing given.

     Instructions for reporting errors 
     We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: 
     
       Click the "Report Issue"  ( 
             
           )  button, located in the page header. 
     
     Tip:  You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
     Our team has already identified  the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. 
     Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a  list of packages that need conversion , and welcome  developer contributions .
